DU LL.B. II Term · Paper LB-205 · Topic 4 · 8 Hours

State Responsibility

The whole of the law on when a State is responsible for an internationally wrongful act, what that responsibility is made of, and what must be done to discharge it — built article-by-article on the ILC's Articles on Responsibility of States for Internationally Wrongful Acts, 2001, on S.K. Verma's Chapter 11, and on the seven prescribed judgments: Corfu Channel, Barcelona Traction, Tehran Hostages, Nicaragua, LaGrand, Costa Rica v. Nicaragua and DRC v. Uganda.

MAPHow to Use These Notes

What this topic is. Topic 3 asked what happens when international law and municipal law meet inside a State's courts. Topic 4 asks the prior and larger question: what happens when a State breaks international law? The answer is the law of State responsibility — the body of rules that decides when a State is responsible, for whose conduct, and with what consequences. It is the topic on which the International Court of Justice has done more of its work than on any other, and it is the topic around which seven of the eight hours of this part of the syllabus are built.

What the syllabus requires. Six heads: 4.1 the basis of international responsibility (with Corfu Channel, Barcelona Traction, Tehran Hostages and Nicaragua); 4.2 the constituent elements (damage theory, fault theory, absolute liability and risk theory); 4.3 the Act of State — the rules of attribution (with LaGrand); 4.4 the forms of reparation (restitution, indemnity, satisfaction, guarantee against repetition, with Costa Rica v. Nicaragua and DRC v. Uganda); 4.5 the rule of exhaustion of local remedies; and 4.6 the ILC Draft Code on Responsibility of States for Internationally Wrongful Acts, 2001. All six heads are treated in that order below.

How each head is treated. Every head is built in four layers: (i) the text — the relevant articles of the ILC Articles, quoted, with the ILC's own commentary where it matters; (ii) the doctrine — S.K. Verma's Chapter 11 and the prescribed texts, which supply the theory; (iii) the cases — the seven prescribed judgments in full detail (facts, issues, arguments of both sides, decision, ratio), together with the supporting authorities (Chorzów Factory, Alabama Claims, Caire, Youmans, Tadić, Avena, Bosnia v. Serbia, Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros, Diallo, Pulp Mills, Italy v. Germany and the rest); and (iv) the extra material the syllabus does not print — related conventions, statutes and orders, added at the point where they belong, with real examples (Bhopal, Chernobyl, Cosmos 954, Enrica Lexie, Kulbhushan Jadhav, the Indian nuclear-liability statute).

Why the case-list matters here. The seven prescribed cases are not seven illustrations of one rule; they are the seven load-bearing joints of the topic. Corfu Channel establishes that responsibility can be founded on omission and proved by circumstantial evidence, and that a judicial declaration is itself satisfaction. Tehran Hostages separates responsibility for what a State's own organs do from responsibility for what private persons do, and shows how adoption converts the second into the first. Nicaragua fixes the threshold of "effective control" for attributing the conduct of irregular forces. Barcelona Traction is about who may invoke responsibility, and contains the modern statement of obligations owed erga omnes. LaGrand is the case on individual rights under a treaty, on the binding character of provisional measures, and on assurances of non-repetition. Costa Rica v. Nicaragua is the case in which the Court first awarded compensation for damage to the environment as such. DRC v. Uganda is the case in which the Court worked out how reparation is quantified on a mass scale, and how compensation relates to satisfaction. Together they cover the whole syllabus.

A note on sources. The case material prints the judgments of Nicaragua, LaGrand, DRC v. Uganda and the full text of the ILC Articles, and prints Corfu Channel and Tehran Hostages elsewhere in the volume (in the chapters on the law of the sea and diplomatic immunities). Barcelona Traction and Costa Rica v. Nicaragua are listed in the syllabus but not printed in the case material; they are set out below from the judgments themselves and from S.K. Verma's treatment, and the provenance is stated at the head of each case.

↑ top

4.0The Problem Stated

A. Why This Topic Matters

International law has no police force, no bailiff and no prison. What it has instead is the law of State responsibility: the set of rules that says that a breach of an international obligation entails legal responsibility, that responsibility produces legal consequences (cessation, reparation, and in defined circumstances countermeasures), and that the consequences can be demanded of the responsible State before courts, tribunals and political organs. Strip this topic out and the rest of international law becomes advice. As the International Law Commission put it when it sent its Articles to the General Assembly in 2001, the Articles seek "to promote the peaceful settlement of disputes by making available to States a body of rules which will enable them to determine whether a particular act or omission is wrongful, and if so, what the consequences should be".

Three things give the topic its present shape. First, it is secondary law: unlike the rules that prohibit the use of force, protect diplomats or allocate the continental shelf, the law of responsibility does not tell States what to do; it tells them what follows when they have not done it. That is why the subject can be stated in fifty-nine articles, and why those articles apply indifferently to a breached treaty, a violated boundary, an injured diplomat or a poisoned river. Second, it has moved from doctrine to codification: the subject on which Grotius, Vattel and Anzilotti wrote essays is now governed by a text — the ILC Articles of 2001 — which the International Court of Justice has repeatedly described as reflecting customary international law, and which national courts and investment tribunals cite as a matter of course. Third, it has been renewed by human rights, environmental and investment law, each of which tests the classical categories: can a State be responsible for failing to prevent a private wrong? Is damage to the environment compensable in itself? Are there obligations owed to the international community as a whole — and if so, who may invoke them?

Real examples — where this topic shows up
  • India and consular access. In Kulbhushan Jadhav (ICJ, 2019) India obtained a finding that Pakistan had breached Article 36 of the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963 by denying consular access to its national, and an order of "effective review and reconsideration" of his conviction and sentence. The reasoning is the reasoning of LaGrand and Avena (Case 27 below). India's remedy was not money but a declaration, review — and a guarantee of non-repetition.
  • Bhopal. The Union Carbide disaster of 1984 is the classic case in which a weak domestic liability regime and a foreign parent company met the international law of State responsibility for injury to aliens, and produced both the Union Carbide settlement (US$470 million, 1989) and India's later statutory response — the Public Liability Insurance Act, 1991 and the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986's section 15 (with the National Green Tribunal's compensation regimes).
  • Enrica Lexie. The killing of two Indian fishermen off Kerala in 2012 by Italian marines, and the arbitral award of 2020, turned in part on Italy's responsibility for the conduct of its armed forces and on India's counter-claims — a live example of the law of attribution and reparation in a maritime setting.
  • Chernobyl, Cosmos 954 and nuclear liability. The 1986 reactor disaster and the crash of a Soviet nuclear-powered satellite in Canada in 1978 are the standard examples of the "risk" or "absolute liability" theory (§4.2 C): lawful activities that carry serious risk, governed by special conventions imposing liability without proof of fault — a model India adopted in the Civil Liability for Nuclear Damage Act, 2010 and the Nuclear Damages Rules, 2011.

B. The Vocabulary You Must Control

Examiners lose patience with answers that use these terms loosely. Learn them as pairs and keep them apart.

Table 4.1 — The vocabulary of State responsibility, defined
TermMeaning, and the article or case that fixes it
International responsibilityThe set of legal consequences that follow from an internationally wrongful act — cessation, reparation, and the availability of invocation and countermeasures. It arises automatically on the breach; it does not depend on any court declaring it.
Internationally wrongful actConduct (act or omission) attributable to a State which breaches an international obligation — the two elements of Article 2 ILC.
Attribution (imputability)The legal operation that treats the conduct of an organ, official, entity or person as the conduct of the State (Arts. 4–11). It is a rule of law, not a question of who "really" did the act.
Primary / secondary rulesPrimary rules impose the substantive obligation (do not use force; protect diplomats); secondary rules determine when a breach occurs and its consequences (deferentially, the ILC Articles).
Circumstances precluding wrongfulnessConsent, self-defence, countermeasures, force majeure, distress, necessity — defences that make an act that would otherwise be wrongful lawful pro tanto (Arts. 20–27).
ReparationFull reparation for the injury caused: restitution, compensation (indemnity) and satisfaction, singly or in combination (Arts. 31, 34).
InvocationThe claim by an injured State (Art. 42), or exceptionally by a State other than the injured State (Art. 48), that responsibility be implemented.
CountermeasuresNon-forcible measures taken by an injured State to procure cessation and reparation; wrongfulness is precluded only within limits (Arts. 22, 49–53).
Erga omnes obligationsObligations owed to the international community as a whole, in whose breach all States have a legal interest: Barcelona Traction, para. 33; Article 48 ILC.
Jus cogensPeremptory norms from which no derogation is permitted; breaches attract the aggravated consequences of Arts. 40–41.
Diplomatic protectionA State's invocation of responsibility on behalf of its national — subject to the nationality of claims and exhaustion of local remedies (Arts. 44; 2006 Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection).
Denial of justiceFailure of a State's judicial system, after the alien has used it, to afford redress — the archetypal injury to an alien, and the conceptual home of the local remedies rule.
Due diligenceThe standard of care owed by a State in preventing harm (from private actors, from transboundary pollution); the Middle Way between fault and strict liability, and the standard in Costa Rica v. Nicaragua on environmental impact assessment.

C. The Three Questions

Every problem in this topic, whatever its facts, is answered by asking three questions in order. The first two together constitute the internationally wrongful act (Article 2); the third gives the consequences.

Fig. 4.1 — The structure of every answer on this topic

D. Where the Law Comes From

Four sources feed this topic, and a good answer names them in this order.

  1. Customary international law, which supplied the whole subject until 2001 and still governs whatever the Articles do not regulate (Article 56). The Alabama Claims (1872), Chorzów Factory (1927–28), Caire (1929), Corfu Channel (1949) and Tehran Hostages (1980) are customary-law cases.
  2. The ILC Articles on Responsibility of States for Internationally Wrongful Acts, 2001 — 59 articles in four parts, adopted by the Commission at its fifty-third session, annexed to General Assembly resolution 56/83 of 12 December 2001, and "commended" to Governments. They are not a treaty; they are the most authoritative statement of the law, and the ICJ has treated particular articles as reflecting customary international law (for example Article 31 in DRC v. Uganda, paras. 99–101 of the 2022 judgment, quoted below).
  3. Special regimes — lex specialis under Article 55: the WTO dispute-settlement system, the European Convention on Human Rights, investment treaties and ICSID arbitration, the law of the sea's liability provisions, and the nuclear and space liability conventions each create their own consequences for breach, displacing the general law to that extent.
  4. Related instruments built on the same foundations: the ILC Articles on Diplomatic Protection, 2006 (the home of the exhaustion rule in modern form); the ILC Articles on the Responsibility of International Organizations, 2011; the UN Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, 2004; and, in India, statutes such as the Protection of Human Rights Act, 1993, the Public Liability Insurance Act, 1991 and the Civil Liability for Nuclear Damage Act, 2010.

E. Five Practical Stakes

  • Whether a wrong can be proved at all. The classical difficulty is evidentiary: mines appear in a State's waters, or a ship is sunk, and the author is unknown. Corfu Channel answered it with circumstantial evidence, and Article 9 ILC now covers conduct in the absence of official authority; modern practice adds satellite imagery, open-source intelligence and the "purposive" reading of evidence by human rights bodies.
  • Whether the State or only the individual is answerable. Nuclear tests, drone strikes, cyber operations and corporate complicity all raise the question of attribution: the Nicaragua "effective control" test, the Tadić "overall control" test, and Article 8 ILC are the tools.
  • Whether a remedy exists. Article 31 makes reparation an obligation, not a favour; but the forms differ radically — the return of an embassy (restitution), a sum of money (indemnity), an apology or a declaration (satisfaction), or a promise and a programme (guarantee of non-repetition). DRC v. Uganda shows all four in a single case, and shows which of them the Court will refuse.
  • Whether the injured party can bring the claim. The individual has no standing; the State of nationality must act (diplomatic protection), subject to nationality and exhaustion rules — the lesson of Barcelona Traction and of the 2006 Draft Articles.
  • Whether the State can escape by pointing at its own law. It cannot. Article 3 ILC, Article 27 VCLT, Articles 3 and 32 ARSIWA and the Alabama Claims all say so; the corollary, developed in Topic 3, is that the State's courts may still be bound by the domestic rule, and the State remains responsible internationally.

4.1Basis of International Responsibility

A. What Responsibility Is

Every developed legal system distinguishes between the rule that creates a duty and the rule that tells you what happens when the duty is broken. In international law the second set of rules has a name — State responsibility — and its classic description in the prescribed text is a single sentence: "The invasion of the vested legal rights of a legal person by another or failure to observe obligations imposed by law on its subjects creates liability in various forms under any legal system, which is known as responsibility under international law." Two consequences follow immediately from that description.

(1) Responsibility is a consequence of breach, not a separate wrong. The law of State responsibility does not prohibit anything; it presupposes that some other rule — a treaty, a custom, a general principle — prohibited it. Article 1 of the ILC Articles states the consequence in one line: "Every internationally wrongful act of a State entails the international responsibility of that State." Article 12 adds that the obligation may be breached "regardless of its origin or character" — a treaty, a custom, a unilateral undertaking (the Nuclear Tests cases, 1974) or a general principle makes no difference. As the prescribed text puts it, "State responsibility is not based upon 'delict' in the municipal sense", and "there is a single general regime of State responsibility".

(2) The distinction between contract and tort does not exist here. In the Rainbow Warrior arbitration the tribunal stated the point in terms every examiner rewards: "in the field of international law, there is no distinction between contractual and tortious responsibility". A State that breaks a treaty and a State that drives its warships into a minefield it has not warned about stand in the same legal position: both are responsible, and both must make reparation.

Article 1 ILC, 2001 — "Every internationally wrongful act of a State entails the international responsibility of that State."

Article 2 ILC, 2001 — "There is an internationally wrongful act of a State when conduct consisting of an action or omission: (a) is attributable to the State under international law; and (b) constitutes a breach of an international obligation of the State."

Those two articles are the whole of the "basis" of responsibility in the modern law. Everything else in the topic is either an elaboration of Article 2 (who acts for the State under Part One, Chapter II; when conduct amounts to a breach under Chapter III) or an elaboration of the consequences (Part Two and Part Three). But the classical writers approached the subject differently, through the idea of an injury to another State, and that older way of thinking still shapes the constituent-elements debate in §4.2 and the diplomatic-protection rules in §4.5. It is worth setting out the classical position, because the syllabus asks about the "basis".

The classical three requisites

Through most of the twentieth century the textbooks — and the LED capsule and the classroom notes with them — stated the nature of responsibility through three requisites:

  1. the existence of an international legal obligation in force as between the two particular States (the point of Article 13 ILC);
  2. an act or omission which violates that obligation and which is imputable to the State responsible (Articles 2(a) and 4–11);
  3. and, on the classical view, that loss or damage has resulted from the unlawful act or omission (the point on which damage theory, §4.2, is built, and which the ILC commentary says is not a general requirement).

The third requisite is the one that has changed. It survives fully in the law of diplomatic protection, where a State may only espouse a claim on proof of injury to its national; it survives partially in the requirement of a causal nexus for compensation (Article 36, and DRC v. Uganda, paras. 85–88 of the 2022 judgment); and it has disappeared from the general law, where responsibility follows the breach. The syllabus's four prescribed cases for this head (§4.1) each mark one of those steps: Corfu Channel (injury and omission), Barcelona Traction (injury to whom?), Tehran Hostages (injury by whom?) and Nicaragua (breach without direct injury to a particular State, since the obligations are owed erga omnes partes).

B. The Doctrinal Foundation

Why does a breach of an international obligation entail responsibility? Four answers have been given, and a first-class answer will give all four and say which is which.

1. The general principle of law

The Permanent Court of International Justice put the foundation in the form of an axiom in Factory at Chorzów (Jurisdiction, 1927, PCIJ Series A, No. 9, p. 21): "It is a principle of international law that the breach of an engagement involves an obligation to make reparation in an adequate form." The same idea appears in the Phosphates in Morocco case (PCIJ, Series A/B, No. 74, 1938), where the Court spoke of responsibility as the necessary consequence of a violation of a right, and in the Reparation for Injuries advisory opinion (ICJ Rep. 1949, p. 174), which extended the concept to an international organisation. The foundation, in other words, is not a treaty rule but the legal order itself: obligation and responsibility are correlatives. That is why Article 1 can be stated as an unqualified rule and why it binds non-parties to the Articles as customary law.

2. The sovereign-equality foundation

The LED capsule's formulation, taken from Malcolm Shaw, is the second answer: responsibility "is a fundamental principle of international law, arising out of the nature of the international legal system and the doctrines of state sovereignty and equality of states". Its logic is that in a system of equal sovereigns which recognise no superior, the only guarantee that obligations will be performed is that their breach carries consequences at the hands of the injured State itself.

3. The "secondary rules" foundation — Ago's contribution

The third answer is the technical one, and it is the greatest single contribution to the topic. Before Roberto Ago became the ILC's second Special Rapporteur on the subject (1963) the ILC was entangled in the substantive law of the treatment of aliens — what a State ought to do for foreigners. Ago's insight was that the Commission should separate the primary rules (the substantive obligations, which belong to their own topics) from the secondary rules (when a breach occurs and what follows, which alone belong to State responsibility). The 2001 Articles are drafted on that basis: they "do not attempt to define the content of the international obligations, the breach of which gives rise to responsibility", but determine "when an obligation has been breached and the legal consequences of that breach".

4. The "no excuse" foundation — the primacy of international law over internal law

The fourth answer explains the topic's practical force. Responsibility attaches to the State whatever its internal arrangements: Article 3 ILC — "The characterization of an act of a State as internationally wrongful is governed by international law. Such characterization is not affected by the characterization of the same act as lawful by internal law." The rule is older than the Articles: it was applied against Great Britain in the Alabama Claims (1872), stated by the PCIJ in the Exchange of Greek and Turkish Populations (1925), codified in Article 13 of the ILC's own Draft Declaration on Rights and Duties of States (1949) and in Article 27 of the Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties (1969), and carried into Article 32 of the 2001 Articles.

The two faces of the rule (Topic 3 and Topic 4)

Topic 3 saw the rule from inside the State: an unincorporated treaty does not bind Indian courts (Jolly George Varghese), and a clear statute prevails (Gramophone). Topic 4 sees the same rule from outside: because the statute prevailed, India — not the court — bears the international responsibility. This is exactly the structure of the LaGrand case (Case 27), where the United States' own federal rules of procedure produced repeated breaches of the Consular Convention; and of Avena (2004), where the Court had to order "review and reconsideration" because the domestic finality rules stood in the way. The international obligation and the domestic rule can both be valid; the State alone answers for the gap.

C. Codification: From the Hague Conference of 1930 to the Articles of 2001

The law of responsibility grew up around the treatment of aliens, and the story of its codification is the story of its liberation from that subject. The milestones are worth a table, because an answer that mentions the ILC without mentioning Ago, Crawford or the rejection of Article 19 is an answer that sounds as if the Articles came from nowhere.

Table 4.3 — Codification milestones, 1924 – 2011
YearInstrument / eventSignificance for the topic
1924–30League of Nations codification process; Hague Conference for the Codification of International Law, 1930Three topics were chosen; the third — "Responsibility of States for damage caused in their territory to the person or property of foreigners" — failed, because States could not agree whether the standard of treatment was national or international. The failure fixed the topic's image as one about aliens.
1929Harvard Research Draft Convention on the Responsibility of States for Damage Done in Their Territory to the Person or Property of ForeignersThe most influential private codification; it supplied the vocabulary of "denial of justice", "imputability" and the exhaustion of local remedies for a generation.
1955–61ILC: Special Rapporteur F.V. García AmadorReports still organised around "Responsibility of the State for injuries caused in its territory to the person or property of aliens"; the Commission's work was, in effect, a draft on the treatment of aliens.
1963–79ILC: Special Rapporteur Roberto AgoThe decisive turn. Ago persuaded the Commission to restrict the topic to the secondary rules (the origin of responsibility and the consequences of breach), and to leave the substantive law of aliens to other topics. The structure of the 2001 Articles — attribution, breach, defences — is Ago's structure.
1980–86ILC: Special Rapporteur Willem RiphagenPart Two: the content of responsibility, including cessation and reparation, and the beginnings of the debate on countermeasures.
1987–96ILC: Special Rapporteur Gaetano Arangio-RuizForms of reparation in detail; countermeasures and dispute settlement; and the controversial Article 19 (1996 draft) dividing wrongful acts into "international crimes" and "international delicts" — a distinction that survived until the final reading.
1997–2001ILC: Special Rapporteur James CrawfordThe final text: Article 19 and the language of State crime were abandoned (the concept of jus cogens supplied what was necessary in Arts. 40–41, which were added in 2000); the articles were simplified into 59 provisions in four parts, adopted on 9 August 2001.
2001General Assembly resolution 56/83, 12 December 2001The Articles are annexed and "commended" to the attention of Governments — without prejudice to the question of a future convention. The Commission's commentaries, published in the Yearbook of the ILC 2001, vol. II, part two, are the standard aid to interpretation.
2004, 2007, 2010GA resolutions 59/35 and 62/61; the item deferred in the Sixth CommitteeThe question of turning the Articles into a treaty remains open; a series of Working Groups has reported and the item has been deferred repeatedly, so that the Articles now function as the most authoritative statement of customary law without being a convention.
2006ILC Articles on Diplomatic ProtectionCodifies the nationality-of-claims and local-remedies rules that Article 44 ILC presupposes (§4.5 below).
2011ILC Articles on the Responsibility of International OrganizationsApplies the same architecture to organisations — the point the ICJ had anticipated in the Reparation for Injuries case (1949).
2019–2022ILC work on jus cogens (2019 draft conclusions) and on the succession of States in respect of responsibilityConfirms how the 2001 Articles now serve as the frame within which new rules are built.
Why the Articles are not a treaty — and why that helps

Because they were not adopted as a convention, the Articles could not be weakened by reservations, and they bind as customary law rather than by consent. The ICJ has used them repeatedly without asking whether the parties had "accepted" them — for example in Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros (1997, on countermeasures and necessity), Bosnia and Herzegovina v. Serbia and Montenegro (2007, on Articles 16 and on the duty to prevent), Pulp Mills (2010) and — most explicitly — DRC v. Uganda (2022), where the Court recorded that "Article 31 of the International Law Commission's Articles on Responsibility of States for Internationally Wrongful Acts … reflects customary international law". The trade-off is the converse: without a treaty there is no text binding States that consistently object, and no clause providing for the settlement of disputes about the Articles themselves.

D. The Architecture of the 2001 Articles

The Articles fall into four parts and fifty-nine articles. Learn the four parts as the four questions of the topic — they are the skeleton on which every long answer should be hung.

Table 4.4 — The structure of the ILC Articles, 2001
PartChaptersArticles and content
Part One
The internationally wrongful act of a State
Arts. 1–27		I. General principles
II. Attribution
III. Breach
IV. Responsibility for another State's act
V. Circumstances precluding wrongfulness		1–3: responsibility, elements, characterisation of conduct. 4–11: attribution (organs; entities exercising governmental authority; organs at the disposal of another State; excess of authority; directed or controlled conduct; conduct in the absence of official authority; insurrectional movements; conduct acknowledged and adopted). 12–15: breach, obligation in force, extension in time, composite acts. 16–19: aid or assistance; direction and control; coercion; effect of the chapter. 20–27: consent, self-defence, countermeasures, force majeure, distress, necessity, jus cogens, consequences of invoking a defence.
Part Two
Content of the responsibility
Arts. 28–41		I. General principles
II. Reparation for injury
III. Serious breaches of peremptory norms		28–33: legal consequences; continued duty of performance; cessation and non-repetition; reparation; irrelevance of internal law; scope of the obligations. 34–39: forms of reparation (restitution, compensation, satisfaction), interest, contribution to the injury. 40–41: aggravated consequences of serious breaches of peremptory norms.
Part Three
Implementation of responsibility
Arts. 42–54		I. Invocation of responsibility
II. Countermeasures		42–47: invocation by an injured State; notice of claim; admissibility; loss of the right; plurality of injured and responsible States. 48: invocation by a State other than the injured State. 49–53: object and limits of countermeasures, proportionality, procedural conditions, termination. 54: measures taken by States other than an injured State.
Part Four
General provisions
Arts. 55–59		—55: lex specialis (the Articles do not apply where a special rule governs). 56: questions not regulated by the Articles remain governed by customary law. 57: responsibility of international organisations is unaffected. 58: individual responsibility is unaffected. 59: the Articles are without prejudice to the UN Charter.
jus cogens (40–41) PART THREE · Arts. 42–54 Who may invoke it? • injured State (42–47) • other States (48) • countermeasures (49–53) • collective measures (54) PART FOUR · Arts. 55–59 What else applies? • lex specialis (55) • customary law (56) • organisations (57) • individuals (58) • UN Charter (59) Residual by design: Art. 55 — a special rule (a treaty regime, the ECHR, WTO law) displaces the Articles; Art. 56 — whatever the Articles do not regulate remains governed by custom. That is why the same Articles can govern a breached boundary treaty, the seizure of an embassy, the failure to notify a consul and the felling of trees in a wetland.
Fig. 4.3 — The architecture of the 2001 Articles

E. Who Can Be Responsible

The syllabus says "international responsibility" but the case material and the Articles are about States. The reason is historical, and the modern position has three layers.

1. States

States are the primary subjects; Article 1 is unqualified. Responsibility is owed by the State as a legal person, so that when the International Court holds in Corfu Channel that "these grave omissions involve the international responsibility of Albania", it is holding Albania responsible, not the coastal commander. The practical consequence appears in the law of reparations, where a State may be ordered to pay a sum for injuries suffered by private persons (DRC v. Uganda, where the DRC was required to distribute the compensation to victims), and in the rule that a State may not plead the conduct of its own judges, legislators or federal sub-units.

2. International organisations

The Reparation for Injuries advisory opinion (ICJ Rep. 1949, p. 174) — read for Topic 1 — held that the United Nations possesses international legal personality "and is capable of possessing international rights and duties", and can bring international claims. The corollary, that an organisation can also bear responsibility, was assumed in the Cumaraswamy case (Difference Relating to Immunity from Legal Process of a Special Rapporteur, ICJ Rep. 1999, p. 62) and codified by the ILC in the Articles on the Responsibility of International Organizations, 2011. Its practical importance for this topic is twofold: first, a State acting through an organisation may still be responsible if it directed or controlled the conduct (the same logic as Article 8 for individuals); and second, dual attribution is possible, as in the Nuhanović case (Dutch Supreme Court, 2013, on the fall of Srebrenica), where the conduct of Dutchbat was attributed both to the United Nations and to the Netherlands.

3. Individuals — and the confusion an examiner looks for

Individuals now bear criminal responsibility under international law: the Nuremberg and Tokyo trials, the ICTY (1993), the ICTR (1994) and the International Criminal Court (2002). But that responsibility is different in kind from State responsibility, and Article 58 ILC says so expressly: the individual responsibility of a person acting on behalf of a State is not affected by the State's responsibility, and vice versa. The prescribed text makes four points that are worth reproducing:

  • Individuals may be responsible for criminal acts although they acted in an official capacity and in the perceived interest of the State (Rome Statute, Articles 27 and 33).
  • They may nevertheless enjoy jurisdictional immunity in national courts while in office: Arrest Warrant of 11 April 2000 (DRC v. Belgium), ICJ Rep. 2002, p. 3, paras. 51–61, and ex parte Pinochet Ugarte (No. 3) [2001] 1 AC 147 — a confusion of the law of immunity with the law of responsibility which the ICJ itself had to sort out.
  • Civil responsibility of individuals and corporations for breaches of international law remains undeveloped outside the United States' Alien Tort Claims Act (28 U.S.C. §1350) — see Kadic v. Karadžić, 70 F.3d 232 (2d Cir. 1995).
  • State responsibility is not criminal responsibility. The 1996 draft's attempt to create "international crimes of States" was abandoned in 2001; Articles 40–41 now provide special consequences for breaches of peremptory norms without using the language of crime, and satisfaction cannot be punitive (§4.4 D).

F. Municipal Law and Responsibility

The prescribed text states the point as two propositions that "give the occasion for international law to prevail over municipal law":

  1. Responsibility arises out of the breach of duty or non-performance of an international obligation by the State, even though the facts bring into question the rights and duties under municipal law between the citizen of the claimant State and the State alleged to be responsible.
  2. The authority or competence of the State agency through which the wrong was committed — even though it exceeded its power under municipal law — is no defence: "even though under municipal law, State will not be responsible."

Article 3 ILC, Article 32 ILC and Article 27 VCLT state the rule; the leading illustrations are the Alabama Claims (1872), where Great Britain pleaded the insufficiency of its own statutory powers to detain the confederate raiders; the Finnish Ships Arbitration (1934); and the Exchange of Greek and Turkish Populations (PCIJ, Series B, No. 10, p. 20), where the Court called the duty to bring internal law into conformity with treaty obligations "self-evident". In the modern law the same point decides LaGrand: the United States' "procedural default" rule was not itself unlawful, but its application produced a breach of Article 36(2) VCCR, and the United States could not point to its own rules as an answer.

Three refinements keep the rule honest, and they are what distinguish a first-class answer from a memorised one.

  • Internal law is a fact, not a source of international law. An international tribunal will examine domestic law — as the PCIJ did with the Serbian and Brazilian loan contracts (Serbian Loans, Brazilian Loans, PCIJ Series A, Nos. 20 and 21, 1929) and as the ICJ did with Canadian company law in Barcelona Traction, with Italian municipal law in ELSI, and with Spanish bankruptcy proceedings in Barcelona Traction — but it applies it as evidence of a situation, not as law binding on the tribunal. That is the point of Articles 4 and 5 ILC ("whatever its character as an organ of the central government or of a territorial unit of the State"): federalism, devolution and municipal incapacity are irrelevant internationally.
  • The rule cuts both ways. A State that acts within its law is still responsible (LaGrand); and a State that acts outside its law is also responsible (Caire, Youmans: ultra vires conduct of officials). The absence of domestic authorisation is neither a shield nor a discharge.
  • Consequences may be diminished where internal law is corrected. Since the local remedies rule (§4.5) gives the State the first opportunity to remedy, and Article 39 requires account to be taken of any contribution by the injured State, the domestic legal system is where responsibility is most often discharged in practice — through judgments, compensation schemes and legislative amendment (the Indian example being the amendment of Order 21, Rule 37 CPC after Jolly George Varghese, and the enactment of the POSH Act, 2013 after Vishaka).
Examination box

A question that asks for the "basis of international responsibility" wants five things in this order: (1) Article 1 and Article 2 — responsibility follows the breach, and is made of attribution plus breach; (2) the foundation in general principle (Chorzów Factory) and in the sovereign equality of States; (3) the secondary-rules insight of Ago, and the fact that the same general regime applies whatever the source of the obligation (Rainbow Warrior: no contractual/tortious distinction); (4) the "no excuse" rule — Article 3 ILC, Article 27 VCLT, Alabama Claims, and its mirror image in LaGrand; and (5) the consequences in outline: cessation and full reparation (Article 31), which the cases then particularise — Corfu Channel for omission and satisfaction, Tehran for attribution, Nicaragua for the effective control test, Barcelona Traction for who may invoke.

CASESCase Laws in Full Detail — Cases 23 to 29

The eight hours of this topic are built around seven prescribed judgments. Each is set out below in the same order: (1) why the case is in the syllabus; (2) the facts; (3) the issues as the Court framed them; (4) the arguments of both sides; (5) the decision; (6) the principle evolved; and (7) the examination angle, with the cross-references to the heads of the syllabus. Where a case is not printed in the DU case material in the State-responsibility chapter, the provenance is stated at the head of the case.

Case 23 · Corfu Channel Case (United Kingdom v. Albania)

Corfu Channel Case (United Kingdom v. Albania)

ICJ Reports 1949, p. 4 (Merits, 9 April 1949) · ICJ Reports 1949, p. 244 (Assessment of Compensation, 15 December 1949) · Bench: President Basdevant; Judges Guerrero, Alvarez, Hackworth, Winiarski, Zoričić, De Visscher, Badawi Pasha, Krylov, Read, Hsu Mo, Azevedo, McNair, with Judges ad hoc Daxner (Albania) and Eerola (United Kingdom)

Provenance. The case material prints this judgment in the chapter on the Law of the Sea (Topic 5, pp. 119–126), where it is prescribed for innocent passage and straits; it is prescribed for this topic as case 23 under §4.1 (Basis of International Responsibility). The facts, the pleadings and the judgment set out below are taken from the judgment itself, from the case material's extract, and from the treatment in S.K. Verma, Chapter 11.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Corfu Channel is the first case in the syllabus under the head "Basis of International Responsibility", and it earns the place by doing four things at once: it establishes that responsibility may rest on an omission (a failure to warn) rather than a positive act; it shows how responsibility may be proved by circumstantial evidence when the wrongdoing occurs in a place under the exclusive control of the respondent State; it supplies the international law's classic statement of two of its structural rules — that territorial control does not by itself prove knowledge, and that a State may not allow its territory to be used for acts contrary to the rights of other States; and it is the case in which the International Court first held that a declaration of wrongfulness is itself "appropriate satisfaction" — the anchor of §4.4 of the syllabus.

2. Facts

(a) The Channel. The North Corfu Channel lies between the Greek island of Corfu and the Albanian coast, and is the narrow waterway separating the island of Corfu from the mainland. The Channel is a strait connecting two parts of the high seas — the Adriatic and the Aegean — but it is also a frontier: its eastern part lies wholly within Albania's territorial waters, and it is of special importance to Greece because of the traffic to and from the port of Corfu.

(b) The sweeping and the announcement of a safe route. In October 1944, during the war, the North Corfu Channel was swept by the British Navy and no mines were found; in November 1944 the existence of a safe route through the Channel was announced internationally. In January and February 1945 the Channel was check-swept by the Royal Navy, again with negative results. The British Admiralty's own confidence in the Channel is shown by the fact that on 15 May 1946 it sent two cruisers, and on 22 October 1946 a squadron, through the Channel without any special precautions against moored mines.

(c) The incidents of 1946. On 15 May 1946 the British cruisers Orion and Superb, passing southward through the North Corfu Channel, were fired at by an Albanian battery near Saranda: twelve to twenty rounds were fired over a period of twelve minutes, though no ship was hit. Albania's note of 21 May 1946 stated that the Coastal Commander had ordered shots to be fired "in accordance with a General Order founded on international law", and demanded that permission be obtained from the Albanian authorities for passage through Albania's territorial waters. A further note of 19 June 1946 repeated the insistence on "formalities" and "permission".

(d) 22 October 1946. A squadron of British warships — the cruisers Mauritius and Leander and the destroyers Saumarez and Volage — left the port of Corfu and proceeded northward through the swept channel, the crews at action stations. Outside the Bay of Saranda the destroyer Saumarez struck a mine and was heavily damaged; while she was being towed by the Volage, the Volage also struck a mine and was badly damaged. Forty-four officers and men were killed and forty-two were injured. Both ships were saved and the survivors reached Corfu.

(e) Operation Retail. Three weeks later, on 13 November 1946, the North Corfu Channel was swept by British minesweepers, and twenty-two moored contact mines of German GY type were cut. The United Kingdom had notified Albania of its intention to sweep; Albania had replied on 31 October that it would not consent unless the operation took place outside Albanian territorial waters, and on 11 November it protested against the "unilateral decision" and proposed instead a Mixed Commission to delimit the channel. The operation — known as "Operation Retail" — went ahead on 12 and 13 November, protected by a covering force of an aircraft carrier and cruisers, in Albanian territorial waters, with a French naval officer present as an observer. Albania treated it as a deliberate violation of its sovereignty.

(f) The reference to the Court. By its resolution of 9 April 1947 the Security Council recommended that the two Governments submit the dispute to the International Court of Justice. The parties concluded a Special Agreement (compromis) on 25 March 1948, submitting two questions to the Court.

3. The issues

The Special Agreement put two questions, and the Court's answers to them structure the whole judgment:

Table 4.5 — The two questions in the Special Agreement, and the answers
Question submittedAnswer of the Court
(1) "Is Albania responsible under international law for the explosions which occurred on the 22nd October 1946 in Albanian waters and for the damage and loss of human life which resulted from them and is there any duty to pay compensation?"Yes, on both limbs. Albania was held responsible for the explosions and for the damage and loss of life, and it was held that "there is a duty upon Albania to pay compensation to the United Kingdom". The amount was reserved to a further phase of the proceedings.
(2) "Has the United Kingdom under international law violated the sovereignty of the Albanian People's Republic by reason of the acts of the Royal Navy in Albanian waters on the 22nd October and on the 12th and 13th November 1946 and is there any duty to give satisfaction?"No violation on 22 October; yes a violation on 12–13 November. The passage of the warships was the exercise of a right of innocent passage through a strait used for international navigation and did not violate Albanian sovereignty. "Operation Retail" did. And "this declaration … is in itself appropriate satisfaction" — so no further satisfaction was required.

4. The arguments of both sides

For the United Kingdom

(i) Albania is responsible. The minefield was newly laid in Albanian territorial waters in a channel constantly watched by Albania's coastal defences; the mine-laying took between two and two-and-a-half hours and could not have gone unobserved; Albania's protests and diplomatic notes, in which it complained of the British naval movements but never of the laying of the minefield in its own waters, show that it knew. The responsibility of the coastal State arises independently of who laid the mines, from the duty to warn shipping of a danger in its waters and from the principle that no State may allow its territory to be used for acts contrary to the rights of other States.

(ii) Free passage. The North Corfu Channel is a strait used for international navigation between two parts of the high seas; in time of peace a State may send its warships through such a strait without prior authorisation, provided the passage is innocent; and the passage of 22 October, undertaken at action stations after the earlier firing, was innocent.

(iii) The Court can and should fix the compensation in these proceedings — otherwise "an important part" of the dispute would remain unsettled, contrary to the intention of the Security Council's resolution and to the parties' own declarations of acceptance.

For Albania

(i) No knowledge, no responsibility. Albania denied all knowledge of the mine-laying and denied any connivance in it. The authors of the minelaying "remain unknown"; the mere fact that mines were found in Albanian waters cannot be converted into knowledge by the Albanian Government; the burden of proving knowledge lies on the United Kingdom, and it has not been discharged; the Court's own Experts' report does not establish observation.

(ii) The passage was not innocent. A coastal State may require previous authorisation for the passage of foreign warships through its territorial waters; the squadron passed with crews at action stations, in a narrow channel close to the Albanian coast, at a time of political tension, in order to test Albania's attitude and demonstrate force — which is not innocent passage; the earlier passage of 15 May 1946 had also been a deliberate and provocative act.

(iii) Operation Retail violated Albanian sovereignty. The sweeping of 12–13 November took place in Albanian territorial waters, with a large covering force, against Albania's clearly expressed refusal and without any authorisation from the mine-clearance organisation; it cannot be justified as the exercise of a right of passage or by urgency. Albania claimed a declaration and satisfaction.

(iv) Jurisdiction to assess compensation. Albania disputed the Court's competence to assess the amount of compensation in the same judgment, contesting the several heads of the British claim and putting forward its own claim to set-off.

5. The decision

(a) First question — did the explosions come from the minefield, and who was responsible?

The Court dealt with the facts in stages. First, the identity of the mines: the two ships were mined in a previously swept and check-swept channel just at the place where a newly laid minefield of moored contact German GY mines was discovered three weeks later; the character of the damage was inconsistent with damage from floating mines or from magnetic or GR mines, and was such as would be caused by mines of the type found — so "the explosions were due to mines belonging to that minefield". Second, the authors of the mine-laying remained unknown — a point of the first importance, because it means the case is not about Albania having laid the mines but about Albania's own failure.

The core passage (ICJ Rep. 1949, p. 18): "It is clear that knowledge of the mine laying cannot be imputed to the Albanian Government by reason merely of the fact that a minefield discovered in Albanian territorial waters caused the explosions of which the British warships were the victims. It is true, as international practice shows, that a State on whose territory or in whose waters an act contrary to international law has occurred, may be called upon to give an explanation. It is also true that that State cannot evade such a request by limiting itself to a reply that it is ignorant of the circumstances of the act and of its authors. The State may, up to a certain point, be bound to supply particulars of the use made by it of the means of information and inquiry at its disposal. But it cannot be concluded from the mere fact of the control exercised by a State over its territory and waters that that State necessarily knew, or ought to have known, of any unlawful act perpetrated therein… This fact, by itself and apart from other circumstances, neither involves prima facie responsibility nor shifts the burden of proof."

Third, the evidentiary solution — the most quoted passage in the case:

"On the other hand, the fact of this exclusive territorial control exercised by a State within its frontiers has a bearing upon the methods of proof available to establish the knowledge of that State as to such events. By reason of this exclusive control, the other State, the victim of a breach of international law, is often unable to furnish direct proof of facts giving rise to responsibility. Such a State should be allowed a more liberal recourse to inferences of fact and circumstantial evidence. This indirect evidence is admitted in all systems of law, and its use is recognized by international decisions. It must be regarded as of special weight when it is based on a series of facts linked together and leading logically to a single conclusion."

Fourth, the two series of facts which, "corroborating one another", satisfied the Court that the mine-laying "could not have been accomplished without the knowledge of the Albanian Government": the first was Albania's attitude before and after the disaster — its close watch over the Channel after May 1946, evidenced by its delegate's statement in the Security Council on 19 February 1947 and, above all, by its diplomatic notes, whose insistence on "formalities" and "permission" proved its intention to keep jealous watch over its waters; the fact that the minefield was laid during the period of close watch, which made the assertion of ignorance "a priori somewhat improbable"; the telegrams of 13 and 27 November 1946, in which Albania protested violently about the British naval movements but never mentioned the presence of a minefield in its own waters; and the failure either to notify the presence of mines or to hold the judicial investigation which a territorial sovereign would have been expected to conduct. The second was the feasibility of observation: the Bay of Saranda and the channel were easily watched from the heights; the nearest mine was only 500 metres from the coast; the minelayers must have passed within 500 metres of the coast; and the Court's Experts, after examining the locality, concluded that in either direction of laying the minelayers would have been seen from the look-out posts at Cape Kiephali and St. George's Monastery.

Fifth, the obligations which Albania's knowledge brought with it. The Court was careful about the source of the duty:

"The obligations incumbent upon the Albanian authorities consisted in notifying, for the benefit of shipping in general, the existence of a minefield in Albanian territorial waters and in warning the approaching British warships of the imminent danger to which the minefield exposed them. Such obligations are based, not on the Hague Convention of 1907, No. VIII, which is applicable in time of war, but on certain general and well-recognized principles, namely: elementary considerations of humanity, even more exacting in peace than in war; the principle of the freedom of maritime communication; and every State's obligation not to allow knowingly its territory to be used for acts contrary to the rights of other States." (p. 22)

Sixth, the window of opportunity. Even on the assumption most favourable to Albania — that the mines were laid on the night of 21–22 October — a general notification to shipping might have been difficult; but that would not have prevented Albania from warning ships already approaching. The warships were reported by the look-out at St. George's Monastery to the Commander of the Coastal Defences at about 13.00 hours, and the first explosion occurred at about 14.53 hours: "it was perfectly possible for the Albanian authorities to use the interval of almost two hours … to warn the vessels of the danger into which they were running. In fact, nothing was attempted by the Albanian authorities to prevent the disaster. These grave omissions involve the international responsibility of Albania."

Seventh, compensation. The United Kingdom claimed £875,000 in its final submissions. Albania did not dispute the Court's competence to decide "what kind of satisfaction is due", and both parties argued the question as one for the Court. The Court held that it had jurisdiction to assess the amount: if it "should limit itself to saying that there is a duty to pay compensation without deciding what amount of compensation is due, the dispute would not be finally decided", which would not conform to the Security Council resolution the parties had accepted. But the assessment could not be done in this judgment, because Albania had not yet stated which items of the claim it contested and the United Kingdom had not yet produced its evidence; further proceedings were ordered — leading to the Assessment of Compensation judgment of 15 December 1949, which fixed the sum at £843,947 (the Court reducing the British claim, taking into account the value of the two ships and the Albanian counterclaim, and awarding compensation for the two ships, for the damage to the warships' equipment and stores, and for the deaths and injuries of the officers and men).

(b) Second question — the passage of 22 October and Operation Retail

On the passage of 22 October the Court applied the law of straits: "States in time of peace have a right to send their warships through straits used for international navigation between two parts of the high seas without the previous authorization of a coastal State, provided that the passage is innocent", and no coastal State may prohibit such passage in time of peace. Albania argued that the Channel was only of secondary importance and used almost exclusively for local traffic; the Court rejected the test of volume of traffic in favour of "its geographical situation as connecting two parts of the high seas and the fact of its being used for international navigation". The Court accepted that, in the exceptional circumstances of Albanian–Greek tension, Albania "would have been justified in issuing regulations in respect of the passage of warships … but not in prohibiting such passage or in subjecting it to the requirement of special authorization". On the manner of the passage, the Court observed that four warships with crews at action stations had passed through a narrow channel close to the Albanian coast at a time of political tension, the intention being "not only to test Albania's attitude, but at the same time to demonstrate such force that she would abstain from firing again"; nevertheless, "having regard … to all the circumstances", the Court could not characterise these measures as a violation of Albanian sovereignty.

On Operation Retail the Court was unqualified. The United Kingdom did not dispute that the operation was carried out against Albania's clearly expressed wish, that it had no consent from the international mine-clearance organisations, that it could not be justified as the exercise of a right of innocent passage, and that "in principle, international law does not allow a State to assemble a large number of warships in the territorial waters of another State and to carry out minesweeping in those waters". The Court rejected the plea of "extreme urgency" and held:

"Between independent States, respect for territorial sovereignty is an essential foundation of international relations. The Court recognizes that the Albanian Government's complete failure to carry out its duties after the explosions, and the dilatory nature of its diplomatic notes, are extenuating circumstances for the action of the United Kingdom Government. But to ensure respect for international law, of which it is the organ, the Court must declare that the action of the British Navy constituted a violation of Albanian sovereignty. This declaration is in accordance with the request made by Albania through her Counsel, and is in itself appropriate satisfaction." (p. 35)

6. The principles evolved

  1. Responsibility for omission. A State is responsible not only for what it does but for what it fails to do; there "was a duty upon Albania to pay compensation", though the mines were not laid by Albania. The modern home of the rule is Articles 2 and 12 ILC ("conduct consisting of an action or omission").
  2. Control does not prove knowledge — and knowledge does not require direct proof. The two halves of the evidentiary rule: exclusive territorial control "neither involves prima facie responsibility nor shifts the burden of proof", but it does justify "a more liberal recourse to inferences of fact and circumstantial evidence". This is the ancestor of the modern practice of human rights courts and of the "duty to explain" in cases of death in custody and of environmental pollution by a State monopoly.
  3. The obligation not to allow one's territory to be used to the detriment of other States. The third limb of the Court's list — "every State's obligation not to allow knowingly its territory to be used for acts contrary to the rights of other States" — is the source of the modern rules on due diligence, of the duty to prevent (Article 16 ILC; Bosnia v. Serbia), of the corridor cases and of the law of transboundary environmental harm (Trail Smelter; Pulp Mills; Costa Rica v. Nicaragua, Case 28).
  4. "Elementary considerations of humanity" as a source of obligation. The Court derived the duty to warn not from a convention (it expressly rejected Hague Convention No. VIII as applicable in wartime only) but from general principles — a striking example of Article 38(1)(c) at work, and a foundation stone of international humanitarian law's Martens clause reasoning.
  5. Satisfaction by declaration. Where the injury is to dignity and sovereignty rather than to property, a judgment declaring the wrong is "in itself appropriate satisfaction" — the rule now in Article 37 of the ILC Articles and applied again in DRC v. Uganda (Case 29) and in Pulp Mills.
  6. Self-help is unlawful. Operation Retail — "the action of the British Navy constituted a violation of Albanian sovereignty" — is the standard authority that a State may not use force or coercion to enforce its rights in another State's territory, and it is the direct ancestor of the modern law of countermeasures confined to non-forcible measures (Articles 49–53 ILC).
  7. The assessment of compensation. The compensation judgment is an early workout of the principle of full reparation and of the deduction of losses which the injured State would have suffered in any event from the sum claimed — the ancestor of Article 39 (contribution to the injury) and of the "but for" causation analysis in DRC v. Uganda.

7. The examination angle

Fig. 4.4 — Corfu Channel: the chain of reasoning, and its two ledgers
Examination box

Ten-mark skeleton. Facts in three lines (swept channel; mines; 44 dead). Then the two questions of the Special Agreement and the two answers. Then the two evidentiary propositions (control ≠ knowledge; control → circumstantial evidence). Then the duty and its three sources ("elementary considerations of humanity; freedom of maritime communication; obligation not to allow territory to be knowingly used against others' rights"). Then the conclusion (grave omissions; duty to compensate; £843,947). Then Operation Retail and the satisfaction holding. Finish with the two legacy points: Article 9 ILC's due-diligence line, and Article 37 ILC's satisfaction.

If the question is on innocent passage (Topic 5) use the same facts but concentrate on the straits test — "geographical situation as connecting two parts of the high seas and the fact of its being used for international navigation" — and on the reasoning why four warships at action stations were still innocent. If the question is on State responsibility, mention the passage only in one sentence.
