Case 24 · Barcelona Traction, Light and Power Company, Limited (Belgium v. Spain)

Barcelona Traction, Light and Power Company, Limited (Belgium v. Spain)

Preliminary Objections, Judgment of 24 July 1964, ICJ Reports 1964, p. 6 · Second Phase, Judgment of 5 February 1970, ICJ Reports 1970, p. 3 · 1970 Bench: President Bustamante y Rivero; Vice-President Koretsky; Judges Sir Gerald Fitzmaurice, Tanaka, Jessup, Morelli, Padilla Nervo, Gros, Ammoun, Bengzon, Petrén, Lachs, Onyeama; Judge ad hoc Riphagen

Provenance. The syllabus prescribes this case under §4.1 by its 1964 citation (preliminary objections, ICJ Rep. 1964, p. 6). The case material lists it in the Topic-4 case list but prints no text of it; the account below is taken from the two judgments themselves, from the ICJ's own summaries and press releases, and from S.K. Verma's Chapter 11 (admissibility of claims; nationality of claims; nationality of corporations). Where later instruments have altered the position, that is stated expressly.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

The head of the syllabus under which Barcelona Traction is listed is 4.1, the "Basis of International Responsibility" — and the reason is that the case answers a question the other three cases of that head do not: responsibility to whom? Corfu Channel shows responsibility arising from an omission; Tehran Hostages shows it arising from conduct of organs and adopted persons; Nicaragua shows it arising for breaches of obligations owed to all. Barcelona Traction asks who may invoke responsibility when the injury is suffered by a company operating in a foreign country, and in answering that question in 1970 it produced two statements that have outlived the result: the rule that a corporation's claim belongs to the State of incorporation, and the famous distinction between obligations owed to a particular State and obligations owed erga omnes — obligations towards the international community as a whole, in whose protection "all States can be held to have a legal interest" (para. 33). Article 48 of the ILC Articles (invocation by a State other than the injured State) and Articles 40–41 (peremptory norms) are the codification of the second proposition; Article 44(a) (nationality of claims) is the codification of the first.

The case also matters practically in India, because it is the background to the two rules which decide whether a claim on behalf of an Indian investor abroad can be brought at all — nationality of claims and exhaustion of local remedies (§4.5) — and because the way out of the rule it laid down (investment treaties and ICSID arbitration) is precisely the route that India's 2015 Model Bilateral Investment Treaty and its 83-odd investment treaties address.

2. Facts

(a) The company. Barcelona Traction, Light and Power Company, Limited was incorporated in Canada in 1911, with its registered office in Toronto, and carried on the business of producing and distributing electricity in Catalonia, in north-eastern Spain, through a group of subsidiary companies. Its shares were widely held and were traded on the Toronto, New York, London and Brussels exchanges. The great majority of the shareholders — Belgium asserted some 88 per cent — were Belgian nationals, but the company itself was and remained a Canadian legal person. Canada took up the company's cause for a time and then, in 1955, announced that it would not pursue the claim further; the company's own shareholders' committee had earlier sought relief through Swiss proceedings.

(b) The measures complained of. Between 1948 and 1952 the company and its subsidiaries were made subject to Spanish bankruptcy and enforcement proceedings following the Spanish civil war and the Franco Government's refusal to authorise the transfer of foreign currency needed to service the company's sterling bonds. The company was declared bankrupt by a Spanish court in 1948, and in 1952 a Spanish court adjudged the bankruptcy to be "culpable" — a step which carried disqualification and criminal exposure for the directors. The Belgian Government alleged that the Spanish courts and authorities had acted contrary to international law in the way they treated the company and its property; Spain denied any wrongful act.

(c) The claim. Belgium instituted proceedings in 1958 for and on behalf of its nationals, the shareholders; its case was that in the special circumstances of the case — the company's "purely Canadian" incorporation being a technicality, the disappearance of any effective Canadian remedy, and the fact that the injury fell in reality on Belgian shareholders — Belgium was entitled to exercise diplomatic protection. Spain objected, first to the jurisdiction of the Court, and then to the admissibility of the claim.

3. The issues

  1. Jurisdiction (1964). Did Article 17, paragraph 4 of the 1927 Hispano-Belgian Treaty of Conciliation, Judicial Settlement and Arbitration give the Court jurisdiction, and was the claim barred ratione temporis?
  2. Admissibility — jus standi (1964 and 1970). Did Belgium have legal standing to exercise diplomatic protection on behalf of its national shareholders in a company incorporated in Canada — and, in particular, could it do so where the alleged wrong was done to the company and not to the shareholders?
  3. Exhaustion of local remedies (1964 and 1970). Had the company (and the shareholders) exhausted the available remedies in the Spanish courts, as the rule on local remedies required? — Spain's fourth preliminary objection, which the Court in 1964 joined to the merits along with the third.
  4. Obligations erga omnes (1970). Whether there exists a category of obligations owed to the international community as a whole, and, if so, what follows for the invocation of responsibility.

4. The arguments of both sides

For Belgium

(i) The corporate veil should be pierced. In the circumstances of the case, the nationality of a company meant little; the overwhelming Belgian interest in the company, and the fact that the loss fell on Belgian nationals, made the injury in substance an injury to Belgium's nationals. The Court had "the power to consider the economic reality" — and the case, Belgium argued, was one of the rare examples in which the Court should do so.

(ii) Canada's abstention was decisive. Canada had intervened and then withdrawn; in consequence no State could exercise protection on behalf of the company; shareholders who were overwhelmingly Belgian would be left without any remedy; international law could not tolerate such a denial.

(iii) Equity and "remedy" arguments. The "principles of equity" required the Court to look at who had really suffered, and the "efficient nationality" of the company could be established by reference to ownership and control (the Nottebohm "genuine link" argument, which Belgium pressed by analogy).

(iv) On local remedies and jurisdiction: that the objections be joined to the merits (on jurisdiction, the 1927 Treaty's Article 17(4) "provided for" reference to the Court and remained in force).

For Spain

(i) The company and the shareholders are legally distinct. A company is a legal person separate from its shareholders; a wrong done to the company's assets is not a wrong done to the shareholders' rights (which are confined to matters such as dividends and attendance at meetings). An injury to the company produces only a "reflection" of loss in the shareholders.

(ii) The right to protect belongs to the State of incorporation. Barcelona Traction was a Canadian company with its registered office in Canada; whatever the residence of its shareholders, the right of diplomatic protection belonged to Canada, and Canada's decision not to pursue the claim is irrelevant to Belgium's standing.

(iii) No equity-based exception. Equity cannot create a rule of law or confer a right of protection where none exists; an unlimited extension of standing to shareholders' States would produce chaos, since shares are "widely scattered and frequently change hands".

(iv) Local remedies were not exhausted — the Spanish courts, including the Supreme Court, remained open, and appeals were available until at least 1960.

5. The decisions

(a) The Preliminary Objections judgment, 24 July 1964

The Court dealt with Spain's four objections in order. The first two — that the Court lacked jurisdiction and that the claim was inadmissible ratione temporis — were rejected. On the first, the Court interpreted Article 37 of its Statute ("treaties … which provide for" recourse to the PCIJ, now the Court) and Article 17(4) of the 1927 Hispano-Belgian Treaty, and held that the Treaty continued to provide a valid basis of jurisdiction — the first case in which the Court applied Article 37 to a treaty concluded before 1945. The third and fourth objections — Belgium's jus standi, and the non-exhaustion of local remedies — were joined to the merits. The Court's reasoning on joinder is the part of the 1964 judgment worth remembering: the question whether Belgium had a right of its own to protect the shareholders was "too closely connected with the merits" to be determined at the preliminary stage, because it depended on the very existence of the substantive rights Belgium asserted; and the exhaustion objection was "inextricably interwoven with the issues of denial of justice which constitute the major part of the merits".

(b) The Second Phase judgment, 5 February 1970

The Court then decided the third objection and, having upheld it, found it unnecessary to decide the fourth (exhaustion). It rejected Belgium's claim for want of jus standi, by 15 votes to one (Judge ad hoc Riphagen dissenting; several judges in the majority filed separate opinions on the reasoning). The essential steps were these:

  1. Separate legal personality. The company is a separate legal entity from its shareholders; a wrong done to the company's rights — "the alleged acts" having been "directed against the company and against its property" — does not of itself involve a wrong to the shareholders. "The damage suffered by the shareholders was a reflection of the damage suffered by the company."
  2. Corporate nationality. For the purposes of diplomatic protection, the company had the nationality of Canada — "the State under the laws of which it is incorporated and in whose territory it has its registered office" — and Canada alone could espouse the company's claim. The Court expressly declined to extend the Nottebohm "genuine link" requirement to corporations (para. 70), and noted that no absolute test of "genuine connection" for corporate entities had found general acceptance.
  3. No right in equity. Belgium's appeal to "principles of equity" failed: equity "cannot be used to create a new rule of law" and could not confer a right of protection. A State's right of protection is "its own right"; the individual or company whose cause is espoused has no remedy in international law if its own State declines to act.
  4. The narrow exceptions. The Court recognised that there were circumstances falling within the "process of protection" of shareholders that might be admitted — an important reservation, since the judgment has been read as acknowledging (a) the case where the company has ceased to exist (then the shareholders' State may act for the losses sustained by them); (b) the case where the State of incorporation lacks the capacity to act; and (c) where a treaty (as in ELSI, the FCN Treaty) gives the shareholders' State a right of protection. The very narrowness of these exceptions has made them the target of later criticism and of the 2006 Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection (Article 11), which now allows shareholder protection in defined circumstances.
(c) The erga omnes dictum — the real legacy

Fifteen paragraphs into the judgment, the Court made the statement that is now in every textbook. It matters for this topic because it defines the outer boundary of the "basis" of responsibility: there are obligation-sets whose breach concerns everyone.

"… an essential distinction should be drawn between the obligations of a State towards the international community as a whole, and those arising vis-à-vis another State in the field of diplomatic protection. By their very nature the former are the concern of all States. In view of the importance of the rights involved, all States can be held to have a legal interest in their protection; they are obligations erga omnes." (para. 33)

"Such obligations derive, for example, in contemporary international law, from the outlawing of acts of aggression, and of genocide, as also from the principles and rules concerning the basic rights of the human person, including protection from slavery and racial discrimination. Some of the corresponding rights of protection have entered into the body of general international law … others are conferred by international instruments of a universal or quasi-universal character." (para. 34)

"The Court must now consider whether the Applicants' rights of protection … have been established. The Court's position is that … the Applicants cannot be considered to have established … any legal right or interest appertaining to them in the subject-matter of the claim." (para. 46 — the passage in which the Court recalled the South-West Africa requirement of a legal interest, and distinguished it from the erga omnes category.)

The dictum is obiter — the case was decided on jus standi — but it has been productive ever since. It is the conceptual ancestor of: Article 48 of the ILC Articles (invocation by a State other than the injured State, in the collective interest); the ICJ's holdings in the East Timor, Wall and Chagos advisory opinions; the "common heritage"/jus cogens jurisprudence; and the human rights litigation in which a State sues for the protection of a people other than its own nationals — as in Gambia v. Myanmar (2019, on the Genocide Convention), where the Court accepted that the Convention's obligations are owed erga omnes partes, a direct descendant of paragraph 33 of Barcelona Traction.

6. The principles evolved

  1. Who may invoke responsibility. Injury to a national gives the State of nationality a right of protection — but only that State, and only for a genuine injury to the national. Belgium's shareholders' State could not act for a wrong done to a Canadian company. Article 44(a) ILC ("the claim is not brought in accordance with any applicable rule relating to the nationality of claims")
  2. Corporate personality and the nationality of companies. The State of incorporation and registered office; no transfer of protection by the fact of minority or majority shareholding; no "genuine link" test for companies; exceptions only where the company has ceased to exist or the State of incorporation cannot act — now elaborated in Articles 9–12 of the 2006 Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection.
  3. Obligations erga omnes. A category of obligations owed to the international community as a whole, breach of which engages the legal interest of every State. This is the foundation of Articles 40–41 (serious breaches of peremptory norms) and Article 48 (invocation in the collective interest).
  4. Joinder of admissibility objections. Where jus standi and exhaustion are intertwined with the merits, they are joined to the merits — procedural practice now reflected in Articles 79 and 80 of the Court's Rules and in the equivalent practice of other tribunals.
  5. The individual's lack of standing, and the limits of the rule. "The State is the sole judge to decide whether to grant diplomatic protection"; the individual has no remedy in international law against its own State's refusal. The critique of the rule — that it leaves a real injury without a remedy because the State of incorporation is indifferent — produced the investment-treaty system (ICSID, Article 25 of the ICSID Convention giving direct standing to investors) and the 2006 Draft Articles' partial protection of shareholders (Article 11: shareholders may be protected where the company has ceased to exist, or where it had, at the date of injury, the nationality of the State alleged to be responsible and incorporation in that State was required as a precondition for doing business there).

7. The examination angle

Fig. 4.5 — Barcelona Traction: standing, and the erga omnes legacy
Examination box

If the question is on the basis of responsibility: lead with the erga omnes dictum — obligations owed to the international community as a whole; "all States can be held to have a legal interest"; examples (aggression, genocide, slavery, racial discrimination); trace it forward to Article 48 ILC, the Wall and Chagos opinions and Gambia v. Myanmar. Then add the case's second holding: a wrong to a company is not, in law, a wrong to its shareholders, so responsibility for it can be invoked only by the State of incorporation — Article 44(a) ILC, and the 2006 Draft Articles.

If the question is on the exhaustion of local remedies (§4.5), use the 1964 joinder decision: the objection was "inextricably interwoven with the issues of denial of justice which constitute the major part of the merits", so it was joined — a neat illustration of how admissibility and merits interact.

Remember the difference between the two judgments: the 1964 judgment is about jurisdiction and joinder; the 1970 judgment is about jus standi and contains the erga omnes passage. The syllabus's citation (ICJ Rep. 1964, p. 6) points to the first.

↑ top

Case 25 · Case Concerning United States Diplomatic and Consular Staff in Tehran (United States of America v. Iran)

Case Concerning United States Diplomatic and Consular Staff in Tehran (United States of America v. Iran)

ICJ Reports 1980, p. 3 (Judgment of 24 May 1980) · Provisional measures: Order of 15 December 1979, ICJ Reports 1979, p. 7 · Bench: President Sir Humphrey Waldock; Vice-President Elias; Judges Forster, Gros, Lachs, Morozov, Nagendra Singh, Ruda, Mosler, Oda, Ago, El-Erian, Baxter and others (Iran did not appear at the hearings and appointed no judge ad hoc)

Provenance. The case material prints this judgment twice over in its scheme: it appears in the Topic-4 case list as case 25, and its text is printed in the volume's chapter on diplomatic and consular immunities (Topic 7, pp. 189–207, where it is case 41). The account below follows the case material's text and adds the parties' submissions and the operative paragraphs from the judgment itself.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Under §4.1 Tehran Hostages is the case on attribution; under §4.3 it is the case in which the rule of "acknowledgment and adoption" was born — the rule now in Article 11 of the ILC Articles. But it is also much more. It is the case in which the Court drew the line between the responsibility of a State for what private persons do (which is not responsibility for their acts, but responsibility for the State's own failure to protect) and responsibility for what a State adopts as its own (which converts the private act into an act of State). It is the case in which the law of diplomatic immunities was described as a "self-contained régime", a passage that has become one of the most-cited sources of the modern law of lex specialis (Article 55 ILC). And it is the case in which the Court held that the "form and amount" of reparation could be reserved when the breach was still continuing — the ancestor of the two-stage approach the Court used again in DRC v. Uganda (Case 29) and in Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (Case 28).

2. Facts

(a) The setting. Relations between the United States and Iran deteriorated during 1979 after the Islamic revolution. On 22 October 1979 the former Shah, then in exile, was admitted to the United States for medical treatment — a step which the Iranian authorities treated as proof of American hostility.

(b) 4 November 1979 — the Embassy. At about 10.30 a.m. on 4 November 1979, during a demonstration of some three thousand people, the United States Embassy compound in Tehran was overrun by a strong armed group of several hundred persons, who described themselves as "Muslim Student Followers of the Imam's Policy" — the judgment calls them "the militants". The Iranian security personnel "simply disappeared from the scene"; at all events "they made no apparent effort to deter or prevent the demonstrators from seizing the Embassy's premises". The invaders gained access by force, set fire to parts of the Chancery, cut through the upper-floor doors, took control of the main vault and seized the other buildings. All the diplomatic and consular personnel and other persons present were seized as hostages; other United States nationals seized elsewhere in Tehran were later brought to the compound and added to the number. In all, 52 American diplomats and citizens were held for 444 days.

(c) The appeals for help, and their answers. During the three hours and more of the assault, repeated calls for help were made from the Embassy to the Iranian Foreign Ministry, and repeated efforts to secure help were made through the United States Chargé d'affaires, who happened to be at the Ministry with two members of his staff. Requests were also made to the Iranian Chargé d'affaires in Washington. Despite these requests "no Iranian forces were sent in time to provide relief and protection to the Embassy". When Revolutionary Guards finally arrived, they took the view that their task was merely to "protect the safety of both the hostages and the students". No attempt was made by the Iranian Government to clear the premises, to rescue the hostages, or to persuade the militants to withdraw.

(d) 5 November 1979 — the Consulates. The following morning the United States Consulates in Tabriz and Shiraz were seized — the Iranian Government again took no protective action. (The consulates had been closed to the public since February 1979, so no personnel were seized there.)

(e) The endorsement. On 5 November the Foreign Minister, Mr. Yazdi, stated at a press conference that the action of the students "enjoys the endorsement and support of the government, because America herself is responsible for this incident". Expressions of approval came immediately from numerous Iranian authorities — religious, judicial, executive, police and broadcasting. Above all, on 17 November 1979 the Ayatollah Khomeini issued a decree declaring that the Embassy was "a centre of espionage and conspiracy", that the premises and the hostages would remain as they were until the United States had handed over the former Shah for trial and returned his property, and that "[t]he noble Iranian nation will not give permission for the release of the rest of them. Therefore, the rest of them will be under arrest until the American Government acts according to the wish of the nation". The Court found that this policy "was complied with by other Iranian authorities and endorsed by them repeatedly", and that in consequence "the militants, authors of the invasion and jailers of the hostages, had now become agents of the Iranian State for whose acts the State itself was internationally responsible". On 6 May 1980 the Foreign Minister, Mr. Ghotbzadeh, said in a television interview that the occupation of the Embassy had been "done by Our nation".

(f) The proceedings. The United States brought the matter before the Security Council on 9 November 1979. On 29 November 1979 it filed its Application in the Court. On 15 December 1979 the Court indicated provisional measures calling for the immediate restoration of the Embassy and the release of the hostages; the order was publicly rejected by the Iranian Foreign Minister the following day and ignored thereafter. In two letters (9 December 1979 and 16 March 1980) Iran refused to participate, characterising the dispute as "a marginal and secondary aspect of an overall problem" involving "more than 25 years of continual interference by the United States in the internal affairs of Iran". The hearings were held on 18, 19 and 20 March 1980 with only the United States represented; judgment was delivered on 24 May 1980. On 24–25 April 1980, while the Court was preparing its judgment, United States military units made an incursion into Iranian territory — a matter the Court addressed at paragraphs 93–94.

3. The instruments before the Court

The Court's jurisdiction rested principally on the Optional Protocols on the compulsory settlement of disputes attached to the two Vienna Conventions, and on Article XXI(2) of the 1955 Treaty of Amity, Economic Relations and Consular Rights. The substantive rules were drawn from four instruments and from general international law:

  • Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961 — Articles 22 (inviolability of the premises; "special duty to take all appropriate steps to protect the premises … against any intrusion or damage"), 24 (archives inviolable), 25 (full facilities), 26 (freedom of movement), 27 (free communication), 29 (inviolability of the person of the diplomatic agent), 31 (immunity from criminal jurisdiction; no duty to give evidence), 37, 44 and 45 (protecting Power), and 47.
  • Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963 — Articles 5, 28, 31, 33, 34, 35, 36 and 40 (the consulates at Tabriz and Shiraz, and the consular staff).
  • Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of Crimes against Internationally Protected Persons, including Diplomatic Agents, 1973 — Articles 4 and 7.
  • Treaty of Amity, Economic Relations and Consular Rights, 1955 — Article II(4), requiring "the most constant protection and security" for the nationals of each party, and Article XXI(2) (jurisdiction). The Court held that the Treaty retained its force notwithstanding the rupture of diplomatic relations (para. 54).
  • The UN Charter — Articles 2(3), 2(4) and 33 (peaceful settlement).

4. The issues

  1. Jurisdiction and the "overall situation" objection: whether the Court should decline to hear the case because it formed part of a wider political dispute, and whether the Optional Protocols and the 1955 Treaty gave jurisdiction.
  2. Attribution: whether the conduct of the "militants" was imputable to Iran at the time of the attack, and whether it became imputable afterwards.
  3. Iran's own conduct: whether Iran's failure to protect the Embassy, the Consulates and their staffs was itself a breach — and of what obligations.
  4. Adoption: whether the approval and the decision to perpetuate the situation converted the militants' acts into acts of the Iranian State.
  5. Justification: whether the United States' alleged past misconduct in Iran, or the refusal to extradite the Shah, could justify or excuse the breaches.
  6. Consequences: what remedies were due — release, restoration, prosecution and reparation.

5. The arguments of both sides

For the United States

(i) Iran "in tolerating, encouraging, adopting, and endeavouring to exploit, as well as in failing to prevent and punish" the conduct, violated its obligations under the VCDR, the VCCR, the 1973 Convention and the 1955 Treaty, and under Articles 2(3), 2(4) and 33 of the Charter.

(ii) Iran must immediately secure the release of all United States nationals, restore the Embassy and Consulates to United States control under their exclusive control, ensure the protection, privileges and immunities of the personnel, and submit those responsible for the crimes "for the purpose of prosecution, or extradite" them.

(iii) The United States was entitled to reparation "in its own right and in the exercise of its right of diplomatic protection of its nationals", in a sum to be determined by the Court.

(iv) The "overall situation" argument was no answer: no provision of the Statute or Rules contemplated that the Court should decline to take cognizance of one aspect of a dispute merely because the dispute had other aspects.

For Iran

(i) The Court "cannot and should not take cognizance of the present case": the problem "is not one of the interpretation and the application of the treaties upon which the American Application is based, but results from an overall situation containing much more fundamental and more complex elements" — the "whole political dossier" of 25 years, including "the crimes perpetrated in Iran by the American Government", the coup d'état of 1953, the overthrow of Dr. Mossadegh and the restoration of the Shah.

(ii) Iran did not appear at the hearings and filed no Counter-Memorial; it addressed the Court by letter only.

(iii) Implicitly, that the seizure of the Embassy was a reaction to the United States' own wrongs — an argument the Court read as a plea of justification or of extenuation, and which it met in paragraphs 80–89. (Note that Iran's alleged counter-claim was never formulated as such; the Court observed that it had been open to Iran to present its case by way of defence or counter-claim, and that it had not done so.)

6. The decision

(a) Jurisdiction and the "overall situation" (paras. 32–54)

The Court recalled that its Order of 15 December 1979 had already made clear that the seizure of the Embassy and Consulates and the detention of internationally protected persons as hostages "cannot be considered as something 'secondary' or 'marginal', having regard to the importance of the legal principles involved", and that no provision of the Statute or Rules contemplated that the Court should decline to take cognizance of one aspect of a dispute merely because that dispute had other aspects. It found jurisdiction under Article I of the Optional Protocols (the dispute fell squarely within Articles 22, 24, 25, 27, 29, 31, 37 and 47 VCDR and the corresponding provisions of the VCCR) and under Article XXI(2) of the 1955 Treaty, holding that it "would … be incompatible with the whole purpose of the 1955 Treaty if recourse to the Court … were now to be found not to be open to the parties precisely at the moment when such recourse was most needed" (para. 54).

(b) Attribution in the first phase: the militants were not, at first, State agents (paras. 56–60)

The Court divided the events into two phases, and asked of each two questions — "how far, legally, the acts in question may be regarded as imputable to the Iranian State", and whether they were incompatible with Iran's obligations. On the first phase:

"No suggestion has been made that the militants, when they executed their attack on the Embassy, had any form of official status as recognized 'agents' or organs of the Iranian State. Their conduct in mounting the attack, overrunning the Embassy and seizing its inmates as hostages cannot, therefore, be regarded as imputable to that State on that basis. Their conduct might be considered as itself directly imputable to the Iranian State only if it were established that, in fact, on the occasion in question the militants acted on behalf of the State, having been charged by some competent organ of the Iranian State to carry out a specific operation. The information before the Court does not, however, suffice to establish with the requisite certainty the existence at that time of such a link…" (para. 58)

On the Ayatollah Khomeini's general declarations of 1 November 1979 ("it is up to the dear pupils, students and theological students to expand with all their might their attacks against the United States and Israel…"), the Court held that "it would be going too far to interpret such general declarations … as amounting to an authorization from the State to undertake the specific operation of invading and seizing the United States Embassy"; and that congratulations after the event "do not alter the initially independent and unofficial character of the militants' attack" (para. 59).

This paragraph is the direct ancestor of the Nicaragua "effective control" test (Case 26) and of Article 8 ILC: a State is not responsible for a private group's acts merely because it sympathises with them, or because it has failed to prevent them; something more — authorisation, direction or control of the specific operation — is required for attribution of the acts themselves.

(c) Iran's own responsibility in the first phase: the failure to protect (paras. 61–68)

The Court then held that the absence of attribution did "not mean that Iran is, in consequence, free of any responsibility in regard to those attacks; for its own conduct was in conflict with its international obligations" (para. 61). The obligations were of two kinds: treaty obligations under the VCDR and VCCR — Article 22(2) ("the receiving State is under a special duty to take all appropriate steps to protect the premises of the mission against any intrusion or damage…"), Articles 24, 25, 26, 27, 29 VCDR; Articles 31(3), 33, 28, 34 and 35 VCCR — and, the Court added, "obligations under general international law" (para. 62). On the facts, the Court found that Iran "failed altogether to take any 'appropriate steps' to protect the premises, staff and archives", and that "the failure of the Iranian Government to take such steps was due to more than mere negligence or lack of appropriate means" (para. 63) — a finding reinforced by the contrast with Iran's conduct on other occasions: the prompt rescue of the Ambassador and his staff on 14 February 1979, the Prime Minister's letter of regret and assurance of 1 March 1979, the police protection of the Embassy on 1 November 1979, and the prevention of attacks on other embassies and on the Iraqi Consulate at Kermanshah on 6 November 1979. The Court concluded that Iran had breached Article 22(2), Articles 24–27 and 29 VCDR and Articles 5 and 36 VCCR, and — in respect of the two private United States nationals seized — Article II(4) of the 1955 Treaty (paras. 66–68).

(d) The second phase: adoption and the transformation into acts of State (paras. 69–77)

The second phase "comprises the whole series of facts which occurred following the completion of the occupation", and here the Court's finding was different in kind:

"The approval given to these facts by the Ayatollah Khomeini and other organs of the Iranian State, and the decision to perpetuate them, translated continuing occupation of the Embassy and detention of the hostages into acts of that State. The militants, authors of the invasion and jailers of the hostages, had now become agents of the Iranian State for whose acts the State itself was internationally responsible." (para. 74)

The consequences were catalogued at paragraph 77: additional breaches of Article 22(2), and continuing breaches of Articles 22(1) and (3), 29, 25, 26, 27 and 24 VCDR; of the corresponding provisions of the VCCR (Articles 33 and others); and a renewed breach of Article II(4) of the 1955 Treaty in respect of the two private hostages. The Court also found a continuing breach of Articles 26 and 29 in respect of the Chargé d'affaires and two members of his staff detained at the Foreign Ministry (para. 78), and held that threats to put the hostages on trial or to compel them to give evidence would, if carried out, constitute grave breaches of Article 31(1) and (2) VCDR (para. 79).

(e) Justification and the "self-contained régime" (paras. 80–89)

Iran's letters had put forward the idea that its conduct "might be justified by the existence of special circumstances". The Court answered on three levels. First, the allegations were never established: even if the alleged activities of the United States were legally connected with the case, Iran was free to present them by way of defence or counter-claim, and it did not appear; the assertions remained "unsupported by evidence furnished by Iran", so the Court could form no judicial opinion on their truth (para. 82). Second, and decisively, even if established they would not justify the conduct:

"Furthermore, even if the alleged criminal activities of the United States in Iran could be considered as having been established, the question would remain whether they could be regarded by the Court as constituting a justification of Iran's conduct and thus a defence to the United States' claims. The Court, however, is unable to accept that they can be so regarded. This is because diplomatic law itself provides the necessary means of defence against, and sanction for, illicit activities by members of diplomatic or consular missions." (para. 83)

"The rules of diplomatic law, in short, constitute a self-contained régime which, on the one hand, lays down the receiving State's obligations regarding the facilities, privileges and immunities to be accorded to diplomatic missions and, on the other, foresees their possible abuse by members of the mission and specifies the means at the disposal of the receiving State to counter any such abuse." (para. 86)

The "means at the disposal" of the receiving State were identified as the declaration of a member of the mission persona non grata (Article 9 VCDR; Article 23 VCCR for consular officers), the refusal to recognise such a person as a member of the mission, and, in a serious case, the breaking off of diplomatic relations and the calling for the closure of the mission (Articles 44 and 45 VCDR; 26 and 27 VCCR). Iran had used none of them. The Court added that the inviolability of diplomatic agents and premises is "one of the very foundations of this long-established régime, to the evolution of which the traditions of Islam made a substantial contribution" (para. 86). It followed that "no circumstances exist in the present case which are capable of negativing the fundamentally unlawful character of the conduct pursued by the Iranian State" (para. 89) — while leaving open that the alleged circumstances might bear on the consequences of responsibility (a remark of considerable importance for the law of reparation and countermeasures, since it is the ancestor of the reasoning in DRC v. Uganda about context and of Article 39 ILC).

(f) Findings and remedies (paras. 90–95)

The Court's conclusions were these: Iran had incurred responsibility towards the United States for "successive and continuing breaches" of the two Vienna Conventions, the 1955 Treaty and "the applicable rules of general international law"; the breaches "clearly entail an obligation … to make reparation for the injury thereby caused"; and since "Iran's breaches of its obligations are still continuing, the form and amount of such reparation cannot be determined at the present date" (para. 90). The Court stressed "the cumulative effect" of the breaches — the escalation from failure to oppose the attack, to endorsement, to the deliberate maintenance of the occupation (para. 91) — and recalled the fundamental character of the institution of diplomacy (paras. 91–92). The operative part (para. 95) then declared, by 13 votes to 2, that Iran had violated and was still violating its obligations; by 13 to 2, that the violations engaged Iran's responsibility; unanimously, that Iran must immediately terminate the unlawful detention and release every hostage, ensure means of departure, and place the premises, property, archives and documents of the Embassy and Consulates in the hands of the protecting Power; unanimously, that no member of the diplomatic or consular staff might be kept in Iran for judicial proceedings or as a witness; by 12 to 3, that Iran was under an obligation to make reparation; and by 14 to 1, that "the form and amount of such reparation, failing agreement between the Parties, shall be settled by the Court", the subsequent procedure being reserved.

The aftermath. The hostages were released on 20 January 1981 under the Algiers Accords, in which the United States undertook not to pursue its claims before the Court and the two States established the Iran–United States Claims Tribunal at The Hague. The Court's judgment thus remained — as a judgment — an unenforced declaration; the reparation phase never took place. It is worth saying in an answer that this outcome does not diminish the case's authority: the case is cited for its law, not for its enforcement, and the Claims Tribunal it produced has itself generated much of the modern practice on State responsibility for injury to aliens (the Yeager case cited in the prescribed text is one of its awards).

7. The principles evolved

  1. Two-phase analysis of attribution. Conduct of private persons is not attributable to the State when it is done, but may become so by acknowledgment and adoption: Articles 8 and 11 ILC. The prescribed text describes the rule in exactly these words: "A State may be responsible for the effects of the conduct of private parties, if it failed to take necessary measures to prevent those effects … In the Tehran Hostages case, the subsequent endorsement of the seizure … by the Iranian authorities made the Iranian State internationally responsible for the acts of students."
  2. Responsibility for omission, and the heightened standard in diplomatic protection. The receiving State's "special duty to take all appropriate steps" makes inaction a breach; and where the inaction is "due to more than mere negligence" it cannot be excused by the absence of means.
  3. The "self-contained régime". Diplomatic and consular law provides its own remedies for abuse (declaration of persona non grata; severance of relations); a receiving State may not substitute self-help. This is the passage from which the modern doctrine of lex specialis and of "self-contained regimes" — Article 55 ILC; the ECHR and WTO systems; the ILC's study of fragmentation — takes its start.
  4. Obligations of cardinal importance distinguished from ordinary bilateral obligations. The Court emphasised the "cumulative effect" and the fundamental character of the rules; the obligations of diplomatic law are owed to the international community as much as to the sending State — an early expression of what Barcelona Traction would name obligations erga omnes.
  5. Continuous breach and deferred reparation. Where the wrongful act continues, the Court may declare responsibility, order cessation, and reserve the form and amount of reparation — the procedural template for DRC v. Uganda (2005 merits; 2022 reparations) and for Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (2015 merits; 2018 compensation).
  6. Declaratory and mandatory relief combined. The judgment orders cessation, restitution of premises and archives, and reparation — the full range of Article 30 and Article 34 remedies in a single operative part, and the clearest illustration in the syllabus of the difference between cessation and reparation.

8. The examination angle

Examination box

The "attribution" answer. Set out the two phases and the two questions the Court asked of each. Quote paragraph 58 for the rule that private persons are not organs; quote paragraph 74 for the rule that approval plus the decision to perpetuate converts their acts into acts of the State. Then map both onto Articles 8 and 11 of the ILC Articles. Add the failure-to-protect limb (Article 22(2) VCDR; "more than mere negligence") — because examiners look for the distinction between responsibility for the act and responsibility for the failure to prevent the act.

The "basis" answer. Use the same facts to show that responsibility does not depend on the wrongdoer being a State organ: a State can be responsible for what it fails to do, for what it adopts, and for what it fails to prevent — and that "the form and amount" of reparation can be deferred where the breach continues.

Cross-reference. For Topic 7 (diplomatic immunities) the same judgment supplies Articles 22, 24, 27, 29, 31, 44 and 45 VCDR; the "self-contained régime" passage; and the rule that even in armed conflict or broken relations the premises and archives remain inviolable. For Topic 4, keep the immunities in the background and foreground attribution and reparation.

↑ top

Case 26 · Military and Paramilitary Activities in and against Nicaragua (Nicaragua v. United States of America)

Case Concerning Military and Paramilitary Activities in and against Nicaragua (Nicaragua v. United States of America)

ICJ Reports 1986, p. 14 (Merits, Judgment of 27 June 1986) · Jurisdiction and Admissibility: ICJ Reports 1984, p. 392 (Order/Judgment of 26 November 1984) · Provisional measures: Order of 10 May 1984, ICJ Reports 1984, p. 169 · Bench: President Nagendra Singh; Vice-President de Lacharrière; Judges Lachs, Ruda, Ago, El-Erian, Sir Robert Jennings, Bedjaoui, Ni, Sette-Camara, Oda, Schwebel and others, with Judge ad hoc Colliard (appointed by Nicaragua); the United States did not appear after January 1985. The operative findings were adopted by majorities ranging from 11 votes to 4 to 15 votes to 0; Judges Oda, Schwebel and Sir Robert Jennings dissented, in whole or in part.

Provenance. The case material prints the merits judgment in the Topic-4 chapter (pp. 68–90, paragraphs 1–290 as excerpted) and the same judgment again as a leading case on the use of force. The account below follows the case material's text, with paragraph numbers of the judgment as printed there.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Nicaragua is prescribed under §4.1 (Basis of International Responsibility) as the case that defines the degree of control a State must exercise over persons before their conduct becomes that State's own conduct. The answer it gives — the "effective control" test, restated at paragraph 115 — is precisely the rule later codified in Article 8 of the ILC Articles and then contested by the ICTY in Tadić ("overall control"). It is also the case that shows the difference between attributing an act and being responsible for one's own conduct in relation to that act (para. 116) — the distinction that underlies the separate chapters of the ILC Articles on attribution (Arts. 4–11) and on breach (Arts. 12–15). And because the United States' Multilateral Treaty Reservation forced the Court to decide the case under customary law alone, Nicaragua is simultaneously the leading authority on how customary international law is formed (paras. 183–188, 202): the sister-topic of State responsibility throughout the DU course.

2. Facts

(a) The revolution of 1979. In July 1979 the Somoza government fell; the Sandinista National Liberation Front (FSLN) took power in Managua and the new government of Nicaragua was installed. Elements of the former Somoza National Guard and civilian opponents withdrew from the country and began to organise armed resistance — a process the Court found to be, at the outset, "disorganized … with limited and ineffectual resources".

(b) The United States enters the resistance. From late 1981 the United States "took advantage of the existence of these opponents and incorporated this fact into its policies vis-à-vis the régime of the Applicant". The Court found it established that the United States "largely financed, trained, equipped, armed and organized" the Fuerza Democrática Nicaragüense (FDN), one of the principal contra organisations; that an agency of the United States Government supplied the FDN in 1983 with a manual on psychological guerrilla warfare which, "while expressly discouraging indiscriminate violence against civilians, considered the possible necessity of shooting civilians who were attempting to leave a town", advised the "neutralization" of local judges, officials or notables "after the semblance of trial", advised the use of professional criminals and of provocation at mass demonstrations "to produce violence on the part of the authorities so as to make 'martyrs'" (paras. 108–122); and that in May 1983 the Intelligence Committee had assessed that the contras "constitute[d] an independent force" and that the "only element of control that could be exercised by the United States" was "cessation of aid" (para. 109).

(c) The direct operations against Nicaragua. The Court found the United States responsible for a series of acts carried out by its own personnel: the attribution to "United States military or intelligence personnel" of the laying of mines in Nicaraguan internal or territorial waters in early 1984 — near the ports of Corinto, Puerto Sandino and El Bluff — without any warning to international shipping, and the presence of a United States mother ship with two support vessels at the time (paras. 80 and 227); attacks on Nicaraguan ports, oil installations and a naval base; high-altitude overflights creating sonic booms over Nicaraguan territory, "designed to intimidate" (para. 91); and the encouragement of third-State support. An economic embargo and the reduction of the sugar quota followed (1981–1985).

(d) The surrounding States. The United States claimed that its conduct was justified as collective self-defence of El Salvador, Honduras and Costa Rica against Nicaraguan support for insurgents — particularly the supply of arms routed through Nicaraguan territory to the armed opposition in El Salvador. The Court found an "intermittent flow of arms" between July 1979 and the early months of 1981, but that "the evidence is insufficient to satisfy the Court that, since the early months of 1981, assistance has continued to reach the Salvadorian armed opposition from the territory of Nicaragua on any significant scale, or that the Government of Nicaragua was responsible for any flow of arms at either period" (paras. 160, 152–153).

(e) The proceedings. Nicaragua applied to the Court on 9 April 1984. On 6 April 1984 the United States had notified the termination of its 1946 declaration accepting the compulsory jurisdiction — but the withdrawal could not affect a case already filed (and filed on the very day of the notification); in its Judgment of 26 November 1984 on Jurisdiction and Admissibility the Court held that it had jurisdiction under Article 36(2) of the Statute and under Article XXIV(2) of the 1956 Treaty of Friendship, Commerce and Navigation, that the United States' Multilateral Treaty Reservation excluded claims based on multilateral treaties (which is why the whole merits were argued in customary law), and that El Salvador's request to intervene was inadmissible. The United States then withdrew from the proceedings altogether and did not appear on the merits. The merits judgment was delivered on 27 June 1986.

3. The issues

  1. Whether the contras' acts were imputable to the United States — what degree of dependence/control suffices?
  2. Whether the United States' own conduct (mining, port attacks, overflights, arming and financing the contras) breached the prohibition of the use of force and the principle of non-intervention.
  3. Whether that conduct was justified as collective self-defence under Article 51 of the Charter and customary law.
  4. Whether there had been an "armed attack" and whether the requirements of request and declaration by the victim State were satisfied.
  5. Whether the United States breached the 1956 Treaty of Friendship, Commerce and Navigation and the customary rules on sovereignty and freedom of navigation.
  6. How customary international law is established, and what the status of General Assembly resolutions is in that process.

4. The arguments of both sides

For Nicaragua

(i) The United States, by recruiting, training, arming, equipping, financing and directing the contras and by direct military and paramilitary operations (the mining of ports, attacks on oil installations, overflights), "used armed force against Nicaragua" in violation of Article 2(4) of the Charter and of customary law.

(ii) These acts constituted unlawful intervention in Nicaragua's internal affairs, aimed at overthrowing its government or coercing its policies.

(iii) The United States had violated Nicaragua's territorial sovereignty and the freedom of the high seas, and its obligations under the 1956 FCN Treaty.

(iv) The contras were, on the evidence, an instrument of the United States — "created", financed, equipped and directed by it — so that their violations of humanitarian law were imputable to the United States.

(v) The United States' claim of collective self-defence failed for want of an armed attack, want of a request from the victim States, and want of any report to the Security Council.

For the United States

(i) The Court lacked jurisdiction; the 1984 notification terminating the United States' acceptance of compulsory jurisdiction applied; and the Multilateral Treaty Reservation excluded claims arising under the Charter and the OAS Charter.

(ii) The United States' actions were a lawful exercise of collective self-defence, upon request, of El Salvador (and later Honduras and Costa Rica) against Nicaraguan armed attacks — the supply of arms and support to the Salvadorian insurgency — under Article 51 of the Charter and Article 21 of the OAS Charter. (This is the only justification the Court found implicit in the United States' pleadings; the United States also relied on the request of the three States.)

(iii) It did not "control" the contras: the contras were an independent force, and their acts, including violations of humanitarian law, could not be imputed to the United States. (The United States also argued that the contras' acts were militarily justified.)

(iv) The mining and other operations were not directed against Nicaragua's territorial integrity in the sense of Article 2(4) (the United States characterised the mining, in its pleadings, as a breach of the 1956 Treaty rather than of the prohibition of the use of force — a point the Court expressly rejected, para. 227).

(v) Nicaragua's own conduct — including support for subversion in El Salvador and its "state of emergency" — deprived it of any claim to relief.

5. The decision

(a) Attribution: the "effective control" test (paras. 108–116)

The Court refused to equate the contras with an organ of the United States merely because of the scale of the United States' support — "despite the heavy subsidies and other support provided to them by the United States, there is no clear evidence of the United States having actually exercised such a degree of control in all fields as to justify treating the contras as acting on its behalf" (para. 109). The central passage (para. 115) is one of the two or three most-examined passages in the whole course:

"The Court has taken the view that United States participation, even if preponderant or decisive, in the financing, organizing, training, supplying and equipping of the contras, the selection of its military or paramilitary targets, and the planning of the whole of its operation, is still insufficient in itself, on the basis of the evidence in the possession of the Court, for the purpose of attributing to the United States the acts committed by the contras in the course of their military or paramilitary operations in Nicaragua. All the forms of United States participation mentioned above, and even the general control by the respondent State over a force with a high degree of dependency on it, would not in themselves mean, without further evidence, that the United States directed or enforced the perpetration of the acts contrary to human rights and humanitarian law alleged by the applicant State. Such acts could well be committed by members of the contras without the control of the United States. For this conduct to give rise to legal responsibility of the United States, it would in principle have to be proved that that State had effective control of the military or paramilitary operations in the course of which the alleged violations were committed." (para. 115)

And the crucial distinction that follows (para. 116): the contras "remain responsible for their acts, and … the United States is not responsible for the acts of the contras, but for its own conduct vis-à-vis Nicaragua, including conduct related to the acts of the contras". This is the sentence to which every answer on Articles 8, 11 and 16 should be tied: general support is not direction; a State's own acts of support are its own wrongs, even where the supported party's acts are not attributable to it.

(b) Customary law: practice and opinio juris (paras. 183–188)

Because the Multilateral Treaty Reservation excluded the Charter, the Court had to establish the customary rules. It held that the shared view of the parties as to the content of a rule "is not enough" — the Court "must satisfy itself that the existence of the rule in the opinio juris of States is confirmed by practice" (para. 184). It further held that practice need not be perfect: "the Court does not consider that, for a rule to be established as customary, the corresponding practice must be in absolutely rigorous conformity with the rule"; conduct "in general" consistent with the rule suffices, and instances of inconsistency are to be treated as breaches, not as evidence of a new rule — "if a State acts in a way prima facie incompatible with a recognized rule, but defends its conduct by appealing to exceptions or justifications contained within the rule itself, then whether or not the State's conduct is in fact justifiable on that basis, the significance of that attitude is to confirm rather than to weaken the rule" (para. 186). And on General Assembly resolutions: opinio juris "may, though with all due caution, be deduced from … the attitude of States towards certain General Assembly resolutions, and particularly resolution 2625 (XXV)"; consent to such a text "cannot be understood as merely that of a 'reiteration or elucidation' of the treaty commitment undertaken in the Charter" but "may be understood as an acceptance of the validity of the rule … declared by the resolution by themselves" (para. 188).

(c) Collective self-defence (paras. 199, 227–238)

The Court held that "in customary international law, whether of a general kind or that particular to the inter-American legal system, there is no rule permitting the exercise of collective self-defence in the absence of a request by the State which regards itself as the victim of an armed attack"; the request is "additional to the requirement that such a State should have declared itself to have been attacked" (para. 199). It also stressed the duty of reporting to the Security Council under Article 51. On the facts, the plea of collective self-defence "against an alleged armed attack on El Salvador, Honduras or Costa Rica … cannot be upheld" (para. 238); consequently the United States had violated "the principle prohibiting recourse to the threat or use of force … by the acts listed in paragraph 227 above, and by its assistance to the contras to the extent that this assistance 'involve[s] a threat or use of force'" (para. 238). The acts listed at paragraph 227 were the mining of Nicaraguan internal or territorial waters in early 1984 and certain attacks on Nicaraguan ports, oil installations and a naval base; the military manoeuvres near the border were not held to constitute a breach of the threat-of-force prohibition.

(d) Non-intervention (paras. 202, 239–242)

The principle of non-intervention "involves the right of every sovereign State to conduct its affairs without outside interference" and "is part and parcel of customary international law" (para. 202). The Court found that the support given by the United States up to 30 September 1984 to the military and paramilitary activities of the contras — "by financial support, training, supply of weapons, intelligence and logistic support" — "constitutes a clear breach of the principle of non-intervention" (para. 242). It added the crucial qualification about the limits of the principle: "the provision of strictly humanitarian aid to persons or forces in another country, whatever their political affiliations or objectives, cannot be regarded as unlawful intervention, or as in any other way contrary to international law" (para. 242).

(e) Other findings and the operative part

The Court further found that the United States had violated Nicaragua's territorial sovereignty (overflights, para. 292(4)); that it had breached the 1956 Treaty of Friendship, Commerce and Navigation (Art. XIX) by the mining; that the United States' own conduct had violated the freedom of navigation and the rights of third States; and that Nicaragua's claim of an armed attack by the United States was rejected. It rejected the United States' counter-claim based on Nicaragua's alleged support to the Salvadorian opposition, holding that though aid had existed up to 1981 it had not been proved that the Nicaraguan Government was responsible, and that El Salvador's "armed attack" claim was on the facts unfounded. Finally, the Court held unanimously (para. 292(10)) that the United States was "under a duty immediately to cease and to refrain" from the unlawful acts it had identified — a declaration of cessation, not an award of damages. Nicaragua's claim for reparation was held over to a subsequent phase which never took place, the parties later discontinuing the case (1991).

6. The principles evolved

  1. Effective control. Support — even preponderant or decisive, even the financing, organising, training, supplying, equipping, targeting and planning of an entire operation — does not attribute the supported group's acts to the State unless the State had effective control of the specific operations in which the violations occurred: the ancestor of Article 8 ILC ("instructions, direction or control"), and the test against which Tadić's "overall control" (ICTY, 1999) must be contrasted in an answer. Application: both tests are applied together in the Bosnian Genocide case (2007), where the ICJ reaffirmed "effective control" for State responsibility while holding that "overall control" may suffice for the classification of an armed conflict in criminal law.
  2. Distinguish attribution from the State's own conduct. A State is responsible for its own acts of support (financing, training, arming) even where it is not responsible for the acts of the persons it supports (para. 116). This is the same structure as the Tehran first phase: no attribution of the act, but responsibility for the State's own failure or support.
  3. The requirements of collective self-defence. An armed attack; a request by the victim State; a declaration that it has been attacked; and (per Article 51) reporting to the Security Council.
  4. Non-intervention, and the humanitarian-aid exception. Support of an armed opposition is unlawful intervention; strictly humanitarian aid is not intervention at all.
  5. Customary law in formation. Practice need not be uniform; inconsistent conduct defended by reference to the rule's own exceptions confirms rather than weakens it; GA resolution 2625 is evidence of opinio juris — which is why the resolution appears on the handwritten note in the syllabus ("Short notes: GA Resolutions").
  6. Cessation, not compensation, as the primary remedy for a continuing breach. The Court ordered cessation and reserved reparation — the same two-stage structure seen in Tehran, Uganda and Costa Rica.

7. The examination angle

Examination box

If asked: "How far can a State be held responsible for the acts of persons acting on its behalf?" Use Nicaragua as the framework and Tehran Hostages as the contrast: Nicaragua — massive support, no attribution (para. 115, effective control); Tehran — no initial attribution, but attribution after adoption (para. 74, Article 11). Then test with Article 8 ("instructions, direction or control") and note the academic and judicial controversy with Tadić's overall control test. Finish with the practical consequence: even without attribution, the supporting State remains liable for its own conduct — non-intervention, use of force, and any failure of due diligence it owed.

If asked: "Is the prohibition of the use of force a rule of customary international law?" Nicaragua is the answer's spine: paras. 183–188 on the two elements, para. 186 on imperfect practice, para. 188 on GA resolution 2625, and paras. 227–238 on application (mining = use of force; self-defence fails for want of request/declaration/report).

If asked: short note on GA resolutions and opinio juris. Two cases, two sentences each: Nicaragua (resolutions may evidence opinio juris; the "with all due caution" formula) and the Legality of the Threat or Use of Nuclear Weapons Advisory Opinion, 1996 (resolutions "normally … do not create" law unless their content and adoption conditions so provide).

§ 4.2 Constituent Elements of International Responsibility

Article 2 of the ILC Articles defines the internationally wrongful act by two elements only: (a) conduct consisting of an action or omission attributable to the State under international law, and (b) that conduct constitutes a breach of an international obligation of the State. Everything else that students of international law have at one time or another described as an element — damage, fault, intention, negligence, risk — has had to be fitted either into the content of the primary obligation or into the law of remedies. The syllabus requires the three classical candidates to be studied separately, and they are studied below in their historical order: damage, fault, and absolute liability.

Why the question matters. The three theories are not merely academic. If damage is a constituent element, a State cannot complain at all until it has suffered loss, and purely formal breaches (the detention of a courier, the refusal of a visa to a diplomat, the failure to enact a promised statute) would go unpunished. If fault is a constituent element, an honest and diligent government escapes liability for the acts of its officials, and the injured State must discharge the near-impossible burden of proving a State of mind. If strict or absolute liability applies generally, any harmful act becomes a wrong, and State sovereignty is dissolved into an insurance regime. Modern law settles the question by presumption: responsibility is objective as a general matter, damage and fault re-enter only where the primary rule itself puts them there, and absolute liability is confined to the specific treaty regimes dealing with ultra-hazardous activities.

4.2.1 Damage Theory

The proposition. The damage theory says that the conduct of a State in disregard of its obligations does not engage responsibility "unless some further element exists, in particular, 'damage' to another State" — i.e. it must be shown both that the State acted in disregard of its obligations and that it caused damage to another State; if no damage has been done to a State, it cannot invoke the responsibility of another State.

The ILC's answer. The ILC commentary "makes it clear that there is no general rule in this respect. Whether the element of damage is required depends on the content of the primary obligation." The Commission deliberately refused to make damage a third element in Article 2. Its illustration is worth memorising:

"[T]he obligation under a treaty to enact a uniform law is breached by the failure to enact the law, and it is not necessary for another State party to point to any specific damage it has suffered by reason of that failure. Whether a particular obligation is breached forthwith upon a failure to act on the part of the responsible State, or whether some important event must occur, depends on the content and interpretation of the primary obligation and cannot be determined in the abstract." (ILC Commentary to Article 2)

In the prescribed text's words. Verma states the position exactly: "As evident from the Draft articles, there is no general requirement of harm or damage before the State responsibility is actually laid down. In certain situations, mere breach of an obligation is enough to give rise to the responsibility of the State, such as a minor infringement of the inviolability of an embassy or a consular mission. On the other hand, in trans-boundary environment pollution cases, such as river pollution, the actual damage needs to be established to fix the State responsibility." The conclusion the text draws: "the breach of an international obligation by a State is necessary in State responsibility without always establishing the material harm to another State or person. The main focus in fixing responsibility in such cases is to avoid repetition of such conduct in future, rather than seeking compensation." And where the breach is of a "due diligence" or "prevention" obligation, the harm is the very trigger of the duty: in Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros the Court found that "preparations for the diversion of the Danube was not found to be in breach of treaty until the diversion went ahead and caused damage to Hungary"; compare the Lake Lanoux Arbitration (1957) 24 ILR 101, where France's scheme was held lawful precisely because it caused no injury to Spain.

"Injury" in the law of remedies — the wider concept. Damage is not displaced from the law of responsibility; it is relocated. Article 31(2) ILC provides: "Injury includes any damage, whether material or moral, caused by the internationally wrongful act of a State." The commentary explains that "material" damage means damage to property or other interests of the State and its nationals assessable in financial terms, while "moral" damage includes such items as individual pain and suffering, loss of loved ones or personal affront associated with an intrusion on one's home or private life. Article 31(1) — "full reparation for the injury caused" — is therefore measured by injury, not by pecuniary loss; and moral injury is remedied principally by satisfaction (Article 37) rather than compensation. The Corfu Channel declaration — that the finding of the Court's judgment on Albania's violation of sovereignty was "in itself appropriate satisfaction" — is the classic example.

The "legal injury" refinement and the question of standing. The damage theory in its strongest form is really a disguised theory of standing: no damage, no right to complain. The ILC's solution is Article 42: a State is "injured" and entitled to invoke responsibility if the obligation is owed to it individually, or to a group of States including it, or to the international community as a whole and the breach "specially affects" that State. Where the State is not specially affected, it may still invoke responsibility under Article 48 (obligations erga omnes partes and erga omnes) — but only to claim cessation and assurances, not compensation. The South West Africa cases (1966) stand as the warning of what happens without such a doctrine: Ethiopia and Liberia were held to have no "legal right or interest" in Namibia's mandate, and the case collapsed on the merits without any assessment of damage. Barcelona Traction (Case 24) supplied the corrective with its erga omnes dictum, and Gambia v. Myanmar (2019/2020) confirmed that a State party to the Genocide Convention may invoke responsibility without showing damage of its own.

Real examples at a glance
Table 4.6 · Where damage is, and is not, an element
SituationIs damage required?Authority
Failure to enact a uniform law promised by treatyNo — the failure itself is the breachILC Commentary to Art. 2
Minor infringement of the inviolability of an embassyNo — mere breach sufficesVerma, citing ILC; Tehran Hostages (1980)
Passage of warships through a territorial sea contrary to the coastal State's rulesNo — sovereignty is a legal interest in itselfCorfu Channel (1949), Operation Retail
Trans-boundary air/water pollutionYes — actual damage must be establishedTrail Smelter (1938/1941); Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros (1997); Lake Lanoux (1957)
Denial of consular notification without prejudice to the trialYes as to reparation, no as to breach — the breach is complete on detentionLaGrand (2001), Case 27
Breach of an obligation owed to the international community (genocide, aggression, slavery)No — all States parties may invoke responsibilityBarcelona Traction (1970), ¶33–34; Arts. 42(b), 48 ILC

4.2.2 Fault Theory

The proposition. The fault theory asks "whether fault constitutes a necessary element of the internationally wrongful act of a State" — i.e. whether the State must have acted intentionally or negligently (with culpa) before its conduct can be characterised as wrongful. On the subjective view (associated with the early doctrine of Anzilotti and the "will" theory of the State), responsibility is founded on the fault of the State's organs; on the objective view (associated with Ago and with the modern ILC Articles), responsibility follows from the mere non-conformity of conduct with an obligation.

The ILC's answer. "In the absence of any specific requirement of a mental element in terms of the primary obligation, it is only the act of the State that matters, independently of any intention." Neither Article 1 nor Article 2 nor Articles 4–15 mentions intention or negligence. Responsibility is therefore, in principle, objective; but the matter is not closed, because the primary obligation may itself be expressed in terms of conduct — "due diligence", "all appropriate steps", "necessary measures", "prevent", "exercise surveillance" — and where it is, the standard of care becomes part of the content of the obligation and negligence becomes relevant not as a general element but as the measure of the obligation itself.

The prescribed text's position. Verma writes: "Culpa or fault is a debatable issue in attributing responsibility for the breach of an international obligation by a State. The supporters of 'objective responsibility' are of the view that there should be a causal connection between the act and the breach by the State or its organs. The doctrine of 'objective responsibility' is the responsibility of the State for the acts of its officials or organs, which may devolve upon it despite the absence of any fault on its part." The text then reaches its own conclusion: "there is no clear rule that responsibility is always based on fault, nor one that it is independent of it. Hence, there is no clear presumption either way. Where responsibility is essentially based on acts or omission, like in Corfu Channel case or Diplomatic and Consular Staff in Tehran case, fault becomes a vital consideration. But if the act is carried out deliberately, consideration of fault has insignificant role. So, everything depends on the context and the interpretation of the obligation breached. The international law on State responsibility, as stated in the Draft Articles (2–12), does not require fault as a pre-requisite before an act or omission be characterized as an international wrong. But the case law point to the conclusion that fault is necessary condition for responsibility, having regard to the alleged conduct of the State."

The case law
  • Caire Claim (France v. Mexico), 1929, 5 UNRIAA 516 — a French national was killed in Mexico City in 1914 by soldiers who had taken him from his house; the arbitral tribunal affirmed that the State is responsible for the acts of its officials performed "in their official character" or by using powers appropriate to that character, even where the officials exceeded their instructions or acted outside their competence. This is the fountainhead of the modern rule in Article 7 ILC ("ultra vires" conduct of an organ is attributable) — and thus the classical demonstration that responsibility does not wait upon proof of the State's own intention.
  • Corfu Channel (United Kingdom v. Albania), 1949 — the Court refused to impute knowledge of the mine-laying to Albania merely because the minefield was discovered in its territorial waters: "it cannot be concluded from the mere fact of the control exercised by a State over its territory and waters that that State necessarily knew, or ought to have known, of any unlawful act perpetrated therein … This fact, by itself and apart from other circumstances, neither involves prima facie responsibility nor shifts the burden of proof." Albania's responsibility was established only because the evidence — the two-hour interval in which the passage of the warships had been watched, and Albania's later protests — permitted an inference that Albania knew and failed to warn. The case shows both faces of the fault debate: control alone is not responsibility, but the evidentiary inference supplyable from the circumstances can convert a failure of diligence into a breach.
  • Tehran Hostages (1980) — discussed in Case 25 above: the Court held that the failure of the Iranian Government to take protective steps "was due to more than mere negligence or lack of appropriate means", i.e. the standard of the primary obligation (Article 22(2) VCDR's "special duty to take all appropriate steps") was itself a diligence standard, and the finding of fault (in a broad sense) made the case one of the strongest findings of breach in the Court's history.
  • LaGrand (2001), Case 27 — the converse example: the United States conceded breach of Article 36 VCCR without any question of bad faith, and the Court found responsibility without enquiring into intention; the United States' duty arose from the fact of detention, not from any culpable failure. Fault had no role whatever.
  • Due diligence obligations at large — the obligation of a State to protect aliens, to prevent trans-boundary harm, to prevent the financing of terrorism, to prevent and punish genocide, to suppress slave trading: in each case the obligation is one of conduct, and the international standard is an objective standard of reasonable diligence measured by the means available to the State — which is why these obligations are sometimes described as "fault-based" but are always analysed as primary rules.

4.2.3 Absolute Liability and Risk Theory

The proposition. The risk (or "absolute liability") theory abandons the search for wrongfulness altogether. It fixes responsibility on the State or operator that carries on an activity which is highly risky but perfectly lawful — nuclear installations, space launches, maritime carriage of oil, carriage of dangerous goods, ultra-hazardous industrial processes — on the basis that the person who creates the risk, and who profits from it, should bear its loss. The theories of damage and fault both presuppose that something wrongful has occurred and look for its consequences; the risk theory presupposes that nothing wrongful has occurred and yet imposes liability for the harm.

The prescribed text's statement. "The absolute liability theory establishes the liability of States arising out of the performance of certain activities which are highly risky but lawful, such as nuclear and spatial activities. According to this theory, the principle of risk is applied, not as a general principle of responsibility, but in circumstances and conditions which are clearly defined in international treaties. For example, the Convention on International Liability for Damage Caused by Space Objects, 1972 imposes absolute liability on launching States for all damages caused by its space object on the earth or to aircraft in flight."

Why it is treaty-based and not customary. No general rule of international law imposes liability without wrongfulness. The techniques of the risk treaties are all variations on six devices: (i) strict or absolute liability, subject to narrow exceptions (war, natural disaster of an exceptional nature, gross negligence of the claimant); (ii) channelling of liability to a single person (the operator/installator/launching State), so that the claimant need not identify the actual tortfeasor; (iii) compulsory insurance and financial security; (iv) a ceiling on liability (absent from the 1972 Space Objects Convention, hence the international outcry in the Cosmos 954 episode); (v) a single forum and time-limit for claims; and (vi) a complementary fund where the ordinary ceiling is exhausted.

The treaty regimes in a table
Table 4.7 · The principal absolute/risk liability conventions
InstrumentActivityStandard & key features
