Convention on International Liability for Damage Caused by Space Objects, 1972Space objectsAbsolute liability of the "launching State" for damage caused on the earth's surface or to aircraft in flight; fault-based for damage in outer space; no ceiling; claims through diplomatic channels or a Claims Commission
Paris Convention on Third Party Liability in the Field of Nuclear Energy, 1960 (OECD); Brussels Supplementary Convention, 1963Nuclear installations (Western Europe)Strict liability; channelled to the operator; licensed operators must insure; capped sums supplemented by public funds
Vienna Convention on Civil Liability for Nuclear Damage, 1963 (IAEA); CSC 1997Nuclear damage (worldwide)Strict liability channelled to operator; liability limited in amount and in time; jurisdiction exclusive to the courts of the State where the accident occurred
Brussels Convention on the Liability of Operators of Nuclear Ships, 1962Nuclear shipsAbsolute liability of the operator; insurance compulsory; cap per ship
International Convention on Civil Liability for Oil Pollution Damage, 1969 (CLC); Fund Convention, 1971; Protocols 1984/1992/2000; Bunker Oil Convention, 2001Persistent oil from tankersStrict liability channelled to the registered shipowner; limited by tonnage; compulsory insurance; a Supplementary Fund pays above the limit
Convention on Civil Liability for Damage Caused during Carriage of Dangerous Goods by Road, Rail and Inland Navigation Vessels, 1989 (CRTD)Dangerous goodsStrict liability of the carrier; channelled; insured
Basel Protocol on Liability and Compensation, 1999; Minamata Convention, 2013 (mercury); Hazardous Wastes (Transboundary Movement) regimeHazardous waste and chemicalsStrict liability for the exporter/notifier/disposer; insurance; State liability for failures of notification and re-import
Antarctic Treaty, Antarctic Environmental Protocol, 1991 and Annex VI (2005)Antarctic environmental emergenciesStrict liability of the operator for failure to respond to an environmental emergency

Where the ILC Articles stand. The ILC deliberately excluded "liability for injurious consequences of acts not prohibited by international law" from the scope of the 2001 Articles (which deal with responsibility for wrongful acts). It treated the subject separately: the Prevention of Transboundary Harm from Hazardous Activities (2001) and the Draft Principles on the Allocation of Loss in the Case of Transboundary Harm arising out of Hazardous Activities (2006), which recommend exactly the risk-techniques listed above — strict liability, channelling, insurance, compensation funds — while stating clearly that "the innocent victim should not be left to bear the loss". Article 55 of the 2001 Articles preserves these special regimes: "These articles do not apply where and to the extent that the conditions for the existence of an internationally wrongful act or the content or implementation of the international responsibility of a State are governed by special rules of international law." The 1972 Space Objects Convention is the purest example: absolute, unlimited liability of the launching State, and the model on which India's own liability as a launching State under the same Convention rests.

Real examples
  • Trail Smelter Arbitration (United States v. Canada), 1938 and 1941, 3 UNRIAA 1905 — sulphur dioxide from a smelter at Trail, British Columbia, damaged farms and timber in Washington State. The tribunal stated the rule that has since been repeated in the Rio Declaration (Principle 2), the Climate Change and Biodiversity Conventions, and in Pulp Mills (2010): "no State has the right to use or permit the use of its territory in such a manner as to cause injury by fumes in or to the territory of another or the properties or persons therein, when the case is of serious consequence and the injury is established by clear and convincing evidence." It also awarded compensation for the damage done — the first international environmental damages award. Note carefully: Trail Smelter is a case of State responsibility for a wrongful act (the failure to prevent), not of absolute liability; but because it arose from a lawful industrial activity it is the historical bridge between the two theories, and it is the case most often cited in India in the development of the "polluter pays" and "precautionary" principles.
  • Cosmos 954 (Canada/USSR, 1978) — the Soviet nuclear-powered satellite disintegrated over Canada in January 1978, scattering radioactive debris across the Northwest Territories. Canada claimed against the USSR under the 1972 Convention (absolute liability of the launching State) rather than under general law; the USSR ultimately paid CAD 3 million in settlement. The episode contributed to the negotiation of the 1979 Moon Agreement and to the ILC's work on prevention.
  • Chernobyl, 1986 — the Soviet Union accepted responsibility in practice for the trans-boundary nuclear accident; the international response was the 1986 Convention on Early Notification of a Nuclear Accident and the 1986 Convention on Assistance in the Case of a Nuclear Accident or Radiological Emergency, and the reinforcement of the Vienna Convention regime discussed above.
  • Bhopal, 1984 (from the Indian side) — the Union Carbide disaster is the classic Indian illustration of strict liability in municipal law: the Supreme Court in M.C. Mehta v. Union of India (1987) 1 SCC 395 held that an enterprise engaged in a hazardous activity owes "absolute and non-delegable" liability to the community, and that this liability "is not subject to any exceptions like an Act of God"; the court applied the principle to award compensation in Union Carbide Corpn. v. Union of India (1989). The legislative expression is the Public Liability Insurance Act, 1991 (no-fault liability and compulsory insurance for accidents in handling hazardous substances) and the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986 with the National Green Tribunal Act, 2010 (which codifies the polluter-pays and precautionary principles). These Indian developments are the domestic shadow of the international "risk" theory and are worth a line in any answer.
  • Pulp Mills on the River Uruguay (Argentina v. Uruguay), ICJ 2010 — the Court recognised the obligation of environmental impact assessment as part of the due diligence duty of the State, and held that it must be conducted before the activity begins; the case shows how the law has chosen to develop through preventive duties rather than through generalised absolute liability.

The three theories compared

Table 4.8 · Damage, fault and risk — a comparison
Point of comparisonDamage theoryFault theoryAbsolute liability / risk theory
Essential questionHas another State suffered a loss?Is the State culpable (a mental element)?Who created the risk?
Status in ILC Articles 2001Not a general element (Art. 2); relegated to remedy (Arts. 31, 36, 37)Not a general element (Arts. 1–2, 4–15); relevant only if the primary rule so providesExcluded (Art. 55; subject treated in the 2001 Prevention Articles and 2006 Allocation of Loss Principles)
Where it does operateTrans-boundary pollution; denial of justice; environmental harm (Trail Smelter, Gabčíkovo, Lake Lanoux)Due diligence obligations; "appropriate steps"; "necessary measures" (Tehran, Corfu, Caire)Space objects (1972); nuclear (1960/1963); oil pollution (1969/1971/2001); dangerous goods (1989); hazardous waste (1999)
Remedy associatedCompensation for material and moral damage (Art. 36)Satisfaction; cessation (Arts. 30, 37)Insurance, funds and caps rather than "reparation" as between States
Leading example for an answerFailure to enact a uniform law; minor infringement of embassy inviolabilityCorfu Channel (knowledge required); Tehran Hostages ("more than mere negligence")Cosmos 954; Chernobyl; M.C. Mehta (1987); Public Liability Insurance Act, 1991

Fig. 4.6 — How damage, fault and risk fit around the two elements of Article 2 ILC.

Examination box — model opening for the 8/10-mark question

"Discuss the constituent elements of international responsibility with reference to the damage, fault and absolute liability theories." Open with Article 2 and its two elements, and state at once that the ILC's text is silent on damage and fault because the Commission deliberately refused to make them general elements. Then take each theory in turn: (i) damage — the argument that "no damage, no responsibility"; the ILC's answer that the requirement "depends on the content of the primary obligation" (uniform-law example); the survival of damage as injury under Article 31(2), with moral damage remedied by satisfaction (Corfu Channel's declaration) and material damage by compensation; the standing dimension (Arts. 42 and 48; South West Africa contrasted with Barcelona Traction and Gambia v. Myanmar). (ii) fault — subjective vs objective responsibility; Caire (official character and ultra vires), Corfu Channel (control ≠ knowledge; the burden of proof), Tehran ("more than mere negligence"), and Verma's conclusion that the answer depends on the interpretation of the obligation breached. (iii) absolute liability and risk — the treaty regimes (space, nuclear, oil, dangerous goods, hazardous waste), the six design techniques (strict liability, channelling, insurance, caps, single forum, funds), the ILC's separation of wrongful from non-wrongful liability, Article 55, and the Indian link through M.C. Mehta and the 1991 Act. Conclude with the modern synthesis: responsibility is objective; damage and fault are not elements of the wrongful act but may be elements of the remedy or of the primary rule; absolute liability belongs to lex specialis.

↑ top

§ 4.3 The Act of State (Rules of Attribution)

The syllabus places this section under the old-fashioned heading "The Act of State". International lawyers today speak of attribution (in French imputabilité, in the ILC's terminology "attribution of conduct to a State"), because "act of State" has a second, quite different meaning in the municipal law of England and the United States. Both meanings are examined here: the international rules in Articles 4 to 11 of the ILC Articles, and the municipal doctrine of "act of State" as a doctrine of judicial abstention, which the case material also prints (the Kuwait Airways case).

A. The principle — what attribution means, and why it is necessary

International law addresses States as legal persons. A legal person can act only through human beings; therefore, before a State can be said to have done anything, the law must supply a rule which counts the acts of particular individuals as the acts of the State. That operation — "imputability", "attribution", "imputation" — is described in the prescribed text as follows: "Since the State is responsible only for acts of its servants that are imputable/attributable to it as its own, it becomes necessary to examine the concept of imputability/attribution. Imputability is the legal fiction which assimilates the actions or omissions of State officials to the State itself and which renders the State liable for damage resulting to the property or person of an alien. The doctrine depends on the link that exists between the State and the person or persons actually committing the unlawful act or omission. Articles 4–11 of the ILC's Draft Articles deal with acts [that] can be attributed to a State."

Three propositions follow, and each is examinable:

  1. Attribution is not the same thing as wrongfulness. Article 4 says that the conduct of a State organ "shall be considered an act of that State under international law" — it says nothing about whether the conduct is lawful. Legality is tested separately, under Articles 12–15 and, if a defence is pleaded, Articles 20–27. That is why the ILC's structure is Part One, Chapter II (attribution) and Chapter III (breach): two questions, asked in that order.
  2. Attribution is governed by international law alone. Article 3: "The characterization of an act of a State as internationally wrongful is governed by international law. Such characterization is not affected by the characterization of the same act as lawful by internal law." Hence the defence often attempted in practice — "our constitution divides power, so the federal government cannot answer for a province, a state, or a court" — is precisely what LaGrand (Case 27) rejected, and what the Constitutional Court of a State is met with when it refuses to give effect to an international judgment.
  3. Attribution operates in favour of the injured State. Every department of government is the State for this purpose — the legislature that enacts the discriminatory law, the executive that expropriates, the army that shoots, the judge who denies justice, the policeman who tortures, the municipality that demolishes. There is no "private" part of the State, and no part of the State which is a "private person".

B. The eight rules of attribution (Articles 4–11) at a glance

Table 4.9 · Articles 4–11 ILC — text, key idea, illustration
ArticleCore text (I.L.C. Articles, 2001)Key idea & leading example
Art. 4
Organs of a State		"The conduct of any State organ shall be considered an act of that State under international law, whether the organ exercises legislative, executive, judicial or any other functions, whatever position it holds in the organization of the State, and whatever its character as an organ of the central government or of a territorial unit of the State. An organ includes any person or entity which has that status in accordance with the internal law of the State."Every organ, at every level, whatever its function. Federal States, provinces, municipalities, courts, parliaments. The organ need not be "superior" or "central". LaGrand (federal responsibility for Arizona's courts and Governor); Tehran Hostages (state organs); Turkish occupation of Cyprus (Loizidou) for the local administration.
Art. 5
Governmental authority		"The conduct of a person or entity which is not an organ of the State under article 4 but which is empowered by the law of that State to exercise elements of the governmental authority shall be considered an act of the State under international law, provided the person or entity is acting in that capacity in the particular instance."Delegated public power. Private security firms with powers of detention, privatised prisons, air-traffic control, school boards, professional bodies with disciplinary powers. The test is legal empowerment plus the exercise of governmental functions in the specific instance.
Art. 6
Organs placed at the disposal of another State		"The conduct of an organ placed at the disposal of a State by another State shall be considered an act of the former State under international law if the organ is acting in the exercise of elements of the governmental authority of the State at whose disposal it is placed."Borrowed organs. Foreign judges, advisers, medical or police units, and notoriously peacekeeping contingents placed under national command: Behrami (attribution to the UN, ECtHR 2007) against Nuhanović (dual attribution; the Netherlands responsible for Dutchbat, Dutch Supreme Court 2013).
Art. 7
Excess of authority or contravention of instructions		"The conduct of an organ of a State or of a person or entity empowered to exercise elements of the governmental authority shall be considered an act of the State under international law if the organ, person or entity acts in that capacity, even if it exceeds its authority or contravenes instructions."Ultra vires is no defence. The official must be acting in that capacity — wearing the uniform, using the powers — not as a private individual on a frolic. Caire Claim (1929); Youmans (1926); the "private act" limit. This is the ILC's answer to the plea so often made after massacres that "the soldiers exceeded their orders".
Art. 8
Direction or control		"The conduct of a person or group of persons shall be considered an act of a State under international law if the person or group of persons is in fact acting on the instructions of, or under the direction or control of, that State in carrying out the conduct."Effective control. Nicaragua (1986), ¶115: even preponderant or decisive participation in financing, organising, training, equipping and planning is not enough; effective control of the specific operation must be proved. Contrast the "overall control" test of Tadić (ICTY 1999).
Art. 9
Absence or default of official authorities		"The conduct of a person or group of persons shall be considered an act of a State under international law if the person or group of persons is in fact exercising elements of the governmental authority in the absence or default of the official authorities and in circumstances such as to call for the exercise of those elements of authority."Power vacuum. Where the State's structure collapses and private persons take over governmental functions, their conduct becomes the State's. The Commission's own example was the situation in Iran in 1979 — although the Court resolved the case through acknowledgment and adoption (Art. 11) instead.
Art. 10
Insurrectional and other movements		"The conduct of an insurrectional movement which becomes the new government of a State shall be considered an act of that State … The conduct of a movement, insurrectional or other, which succeeds in establishing a new State … shall be considered an act of the new State … This article is without prejudice to the attribution to a State of any conduct, however related to that of the movement concerned, which is to be considered an act of that State by virtue of articles 4 to 9."Success determines attribution. If the movement wins and forms the government (or a new State), its earlier conduct is attributed retrospectively to that State: Bolívar Railway Co. (1903). If it loses, its conduct is not attributable — but the State's own failure to suppress it may be a separate breach: Home Missionary Society (1920); Pinson (1928).
Art. 11
Acknowledgment and adoption		"Conduct which is not attributable to a State under the preceding articles shall nevertheless be considered an act of that State under international law if and to the extent that the State acknowledges and adopts the conduct in question as its own."Adoption converts. Tehran Hostages (1980), ¶74: together with the decision to perpetuate the occupation, the approval given by the Ayatollah Khomeini and other organs "translated continuing occupation … into acts of that State", and the militants "had now become agents of the Iranian State". Radical-conservative rescue of otherwise unattributable acts.

C. The organ rule and the "unitary State" — Article 4 in detail

Article 4 is the master rule, and the ILC deliberately cast it in the widest possible terms. Four points are worth developing in an answer:

  • "any State organ" — the rule is not confined to organs of central government. Paragraph 1 expressly includes organs "of a territorial unit of the State" (a province, a canton, a Land, an Indian State, a municipality, a panchayat). In a federal constitution the division of competences is irrelevant to international responsibility. Where the central government argues that it "cannot instruct" its provinces, the answer is that under international law it is answerable for them — the very point made in LaGrand, where the United States was held responsible for the acts of its own courts in Arizona, notwithstanding the division of authority between the federal and State governments, and the point also made in the Genocide case (2007) about responsibility for the acts of the Republika Srpska.
  • "legislative, executive, judicial or any other functions" — separate powers, one State. A statute that expropriates without compensation, a decree that blocks the delivery of cargo (as in Kuwait Airways), a court that refuses relief, a police force that looks the other way: all are acts of the same State. Denial of justice through the courts is the classic "judicial act" of responsibility, and it also triggers the rule of exhaustion of local remedies (§4.5 below).
  • Status determined by internal law — but not conclusively. Paragraph 2 means that a State cannot escape responsibility by arguing that an entity is "not really" an organ; conversely, an entity which has the status of an organ under internal law is an organ for the purposes of responsibility, whether or not it is funded by the treasury, and whether or not it is part of the "government" in the narrow sense. Where internal law is silent, the question is one of fact and of international law: the "de facto organ" doctrine, applied in Nicaragua/Bosnian Genocide, treats a person or entity as an organ where there is complete dependence and identification.
  • De facto organs. The Genocide judgment (2007) explains the test: full dependence of the entity upon the State, so that the entity is a mere instrument ("complete dependence … as a mere instrument"), distinguished from the Article 8 case of a person acting on instructions, direction or control. In practice, the de facto organ test is even harder to satisfy than Article 8 — which is why in Nicaragua the Court refused to treat the contras as an organ of the United States and had resort to Article 8 instead.

What Article 4 does not cover. Private individuals — a company, a mob, a terrorist group, a militia — are not organs, and their conduct is not attributable merely because they are nationals of, resident in, or sympathetic to the State. This is the classical rule, and it is the rule that provoked the entire problem of "State responsibility for the acts of private persons" which is solved, in the modern law, by combining (i) the due-diligence obligations of the State itself, (ii) Articles 5, 8, 9 and 11, and (iii) the separate regime of "State responsibility arising in relation to the act" reflected in Articles 16 to 19 (aid, assistance, direction, coercion).

D. Excess of authority — Article 7 and the classical arbitrations

Article 7 is the codification of a line of arbitral awards which the examiners like to see named:

  • Caire Claim (France v. Mexico), 1929, 5 UNRIAA 516. Mexican military personnel took the French national Jean-Baptiste Caire from his home and killed him; the claim was upheld. The Commission's formula is the ancestor of Article 7: the State is responsible for the acts of its officials performed "in their official character", even though they exceeded their authority or disobeyed instructions, provided they acted "in the exercise of their authority" and used powers attaching to their official position.
  • Youmans Claim (United States v. Mexico), 1926, 4 UNRIAA 110. Three American nationals were killed by a mob at Angangueo; Mexican soldiers participated and the local authorities failed to protect them. The Commission held Mexico responsible — the case is used to show both the responsibility of a State for the failure of its officials to protect aliens, and the responsibility for persons whose official character made their conduct the State's.
  • Mallén Claim (Mexico v. United States), 1927, 4 UNRIAA 173. A Mexican consul assaulted a Mexican national in Texas; Mexico was held responsible for the wrongful acts of its own consul — which shows that it is not the nationality of the victim but the official character of the wrongdoer that matters.
  • Stephens Claim (United States v. Mexico), 1927, 4 UNRIAA 265 and the related Mexican claims: the principle that a State is responsible for the "irregular" conduct of its officials acting under colour of office; and the line, drawn finely, between "official acts" (attributable) and the purely private conduct of an official (not attributable — for instance a personal assault unconnected with the office).
  • The modern application. Article 7 defeated the plea made by the United States after the My Lai-type incidents and by many States after torture scandals: "the individuals went beyond their instructions". Article 7 also works the other way — it prevents the State from sheltering behind the unlawfulness of its own agents' conduct; the illegality under internal law is irrelevant (Article 3 is read with it). The limit is the phrase "acts in that capacity": a soldier who, on leave, robs a tourist is not acting in that capacity (compare the "private act" cases under municipal law).

E. Direction, control, default and adoption — Articles 8 to 11

Article 8 is the single most examined provision of Chapter II, because it governs the modern phenomenon of State-sponsored proxies. Its elements are four: (i) a person or group of persons; (ii) acting "on the instructions of" the State, or under its "direction or control"; (iii) "in fact" — i.e. the requirement is factual, not formal; and (iv) "in carrying out the conduct" — the control must relate to the specific conduct complained of, not to the group's existence or general aims.

  • Nicaragua (1986), ¶115 — "effective control". Quoted in full in Case 26 above: "For this conduct to give rise to legal responsibility of the United States, it would in principle have to be proved that that State had effective control of the military or paramilitary operations in the course of which the alleged violations were committed."
  • Prosecutor v. Tadić (ICTY Appeals Chamber, 1999), ¶¶131, 137, 145 — "overall control". In the criminal-law context the Appeals Chamber held that the test for attributing the acts of the VRS (Bosnian Serb forces) to the FRY for the purpose of classifying the conflict as international was "overall control", going beyond the mere financing and equipping of such forces and involving participation in the planning and supervision of military operations, but not requiring the issuance of specific orders for each act. Tadić famously criticised Nicaragua's "effective control" test as inconsistent with State practice and with the logic of State responsibility.
  • Application of the Genocide Convention (Bosnia and Herzegovina v. Serbia and Montenegro), ICJ 2007, ¶¶396–406. The Court declined to follow Tadić for the purposes of State responsibility: "the 'overall control' test … is unsuitable, for it stretches too far, almost to breaking point, the connection which must exist between the conduct of a State's organs and its international responsibility." It reaffirmed that the test for attribution under Article 8 is "effective control". But the Court added a highly practical observation: whether a State "had effective control of the operations in the course of which the alleged violations were committed" is a question of fact turning on the circumstances, and different degrees of control are required for different operations. The ICTY's test "may be equally applicable" — the Court accepted — for the classification of armed conflict.
  • The practical difference — and how to handle it in an answer. Under Nicaragua/Article 8, only acts over which the State had effective control are attributed, and general support leaves the State responsible only for its own conduct (para. 116). Under Tadić, systematic participation in planning and supervision brings the whole course of operations within the attribution. In the DU examination, the correct approach is to give both tests, state which is the ICJ's test for State responsibility (Nicaragua; reaffirmed 2007), note that Tadić has its own field (individual criminal responsibility and conflict classification), and then apply both to the facts given.
  • Regional human rights applications. Loizidou v. Turkey (ECtHR, 1996) and Cyprus v. Turkey (2001): the acts of the "Turkish Republic of Northern Cyprus" were attributable to Turkey because Turkey exercised "effective overall control" over northern Cyprus. Ilaşcu v. Moldova and Russia (ECtHR, 2004): Russia was responsible for violations in Transdniestria although its agents were not formally organs of Russia — a good illustration of attribution by control in the human rights context, and of the Court's willingness to look at substance over form. Note how these decisions sit between Article 4 (de facto organs) and Article 8.

F. Borrowed organs — Article 6 and "dual attribution"

Article 6 asks whether an organ of State A, placed at the disposal of State B, is exercising "elements of the governmental authority" of B. The ILC's example is a foreign official seconded to serve in another State's administration, or a military unit placed under another State's operational command. Its two great testing grounds are peacekeeping:

  • Behrami and Behrami v. France; Saramati v. France, Germany and Norway (ECtHR, Grand Chamber, 2007). The conduct of KFOR troops and UNMIK personnel in Kosovo was attributed to the United Nations, because the Security Council had retained "ultimate authority and control" over the operations; the troop-contributing States were not responsible. The decision is much criticised for treating attribution as an either/or (the only question being "who had ultimate command"), and for placing peacekeeping conduct outside the reach of human rights review.
  • Nuhanović v. The Netherlands (Dutch Supreme Court, 6 September 2013) — the contrary approach. Hasan Nuhanović lost his family when Dutchbat evacuated the compound at Srebrenica in July 1995; the Dutch courts held the Netherlands responsible for Dutchbat's conduct, on the footing that the Netherlands had "effective control" over the specific conduct. In the same episode the ECtHR Grand Chamber (Stichting Mothers of Srebrenica v. Netherlands, 2013) likewise found the Netherlands responsible under Articles 2 and 3 ECHR. The decisive legal idea is dual attribution: the same conduct may be attributed to the UN and to the contributing State, so that Article 6 need not be an "either/or" choice. The ILC Study Group on the responsibility of international organizations, and Article 7 of the 2011 DARIO, accept that a State and an organisation may both be responsible for the same conduct.

Article 6 should also be mentioned whenever a question concerns the acts of organs of an international organisation: the 2011 Draft Articles on the Responsibility of International Organizations (DARIO) parallel the State rules, but the ICJ has held that the attribution of conduct of organs of an organisation is governed by the rules of that organisation (Reparation for Injuries, 1949 — implied powers; Nuhanović and Behrami as above).

G. Collapse and insurrection — Articles 9 and 10

Article 9 covers the situation in which the official authorities are absent or in default and private persons "in fact exercise elements of the governmental authority" in circumstances "such as to call for the exercise of those elements of authority". The ILC's own commentary explains that the provision was drafted with the Iranian revolution in mind — the committees and revolutionary bodies that took over public functions in 1979 — and notes that the Court in Tehran Hostages took a different route. The provision has an obvious contemporary relevance to failed States, to occupied territory, and to situations of natural disaster or armed conflict where civil authority collapses.

Article 10 has a longer history, and the arbitration cases are the standard examination material:

  • Bolívar Railway Company Claim (Great Britain v. Venezuela), 1903, 9 UNRIAA 445. The "Restoration" revolution led by General Cipriano Castro captured power in Venezuela in 1899; the British claim for damage to the railway during the revolutionary operations was upheld, because the successful revolutionary movement had become the government of Venezuela and its earlier conduct was therefore an act of the State.
  • Home Missionary Society Claim (United States v. Great Britain), 1920, 6 UNRIAA 42. In the Sierra Leone rebellion of 1898 the native insurrection was defeated; the Commission rejected the claim, holding that the acts of the unsuccessful insurgents were not attributable to Great Britain and that Her Majesty's authorities had not failed in their duty to take the measures reasonably available to protect life and property. The award is the classical authority for the rule that an unsuccessful insurrection's acts do not bind the State.
  • Pinson Claim (France v. Mexico), 1928, 5 UNRIAA 327. Same principle — France's claim for damage by revolutionary forces was dismissed because the revolutionary movement did not succeed in establishing itself as the government of Mexico.
  • The modern qualification (Art. 10(3) and the commentary). Even where the insurrection fails, the pre-existing government remains responsible for its own conduct under Articles 4–9 — its failure to take the measures within its power to protect aliens, to prevent the insurgency from committing violations, or to punish those responsible. That is why a State cannot escape responsibility for what happened during an insurrection by pointing to the fact that the insurgents lost. And if the insurrection succeeds in establishing a new State (as in Bangladesh in 1971, or in the Baltic and Yugoslav cases of 1991), Article 10(2) attributes the movement's conduct to the new State, a point of considerable practical importance for the settlement of claims after the emergence of a new State.

H. Acknowledgment and adoption — Article 11 in detail

Article 11 is a residual rule of "adoption", and its only great case is Tehran Hostages. The elements, as distilled by the ILC from the Court's judgment, are three: (i) the conduct must be one which would otherwise not be attributable to the State — so Article 11 is additional to Articles 4–10, not a substitute; (ii) the State must acknowledge and adopt the conduct "as its own" — an acknowledgement for other purposes, or a mere expression of sympathy, does not suffice; and (iii) the adoption may be of the conduct "to the extent that" it is adopted — so that a State may adopt part of the conduct and not the rest ("and to the extent that" is deliberately inserted).

The facts of the adoption in Tehran were: the public endorsement by the Foreign Minister on 5 November 1979; the decree of the Ayatollah Khomeini of 17 November 1979 declaring the embassy a centre of espionage and decreeing that the premises and hostages remain as they were until the United States surrendered the Shah; the compliance of other Iranian authorities with that policy; and the decision to perpetuate the occupation, which converted the occupation into a means of coercing the sending State. The Court's conclusion — "the militants, authors of the invasion and jailers of the hostages, had now become agents of the Iranian State for whose acts the State itself was internationally responsible" (¶74) — is the high-water mark of adoption in international practice.

The contrast with Nicaragua and with mere sympathy. A State that "deplores" or "regrets" conduct, or that merely supports a group politically, does not adopt the group's acts. In Nicaragua the Court refused to attribute the contras' acts to the United States even though the United States financed, trained, equipped and directed them; a fortiori, encouragement and ideological affinity will not do. Since Tehran, Article 11 has been applied sparingly; the ILC's commentary notes that the cases are "limited" and that the article must not be used to circumvent Article 8, since otherwise every act of a group which a State's officials applauded would become the State's own act.

I. "Act of State" in municipal law — the doctrine which shares the name

The syllabus's heading uses the old term, and the case material prints the House of Lords decision in Kuwait Airways Corpn. v. Iraqi Airways Co. [1995] 3 All ER 694. That case is about a doctrine with a different function: the rule of judicial abstention under which the courts of one State decline to adjudicate upon, or call into question, the public acts of a recognised foreign State done within its own territory. The essence is captured in the following propositions, which are worth one paragraph of any answer that uses the term:

  • Definition. The English court will not adjudicate upon, or call into question, public acts of a recognised foreign sovereign done in its own territory: Lord Wilberforce's description of act of State "in the normal meaning" in Buttes Gas & Oil Co. v. Hammer [1982] AC 888 at 934. In the United States the same doctrine was stated in Underhill v. Hernandez, 168 U.S. 250 (1897) — "the courts of one country will not sit in judgment on the acts of the government of another done within its own territory" — and applied in Banco Nacional de Cuba v. Sabbatino, 376 U.S. 398 (1964), with its important qualification that the doctrine is one of federal law rather than of international law, and its exceptions (the "Bernstein exception" where the executive advises that the doctrine need not be applied, and the statutory reaction of the Second Hickenlooper Amendment).
  • Function. The doctrine is a rule of justiciability — "judicial restraint or abstention" — and not an immunity ratione personae, nor a denial of jurisdiction in the strict sense. That is the distinction elaborated in Kuwait Airways (1995), where Lord Goff pointed out that a defendant who has already submitted to the jurisdiction may still invoke the doctrine, precisely because it is not a matter of submission; and the House held that the question whether the Iraqi acts could be called into question should be decided after the issues had been defined on the pleadings, in the Commercial Court, so that the action could proceed in relation to those parts of the claim in respect of which statutory immunity (State Immunity Act 1978, s. 14(2) and s. 5, the "commercial purposes" exception) was not available.
  • Not to be confused with attribution. The municipal act of State doctrine protects a foreign State's acts from examination in a domestic court. The international rules of attribution do the opposite work: they determine whether a particular piece of conduct is the State's conduct, so that the State may be answerable for it internationally. The two doctrines intersect only where domestic courts are asked to give effect to, or to refuse effect to, a foreign law or decree (as in the Iraqi resolution 369 nationalising the Kuwait Airways fleet). The sequel to the 1995 decision — Kuwait Airways Corpn. v. Iraqi Airways Co. (No. 3) [2002] UKHL 19 — illustrates the intersection perfectly: the House of Lords held, applying the 2002 International Law Commission Articles as an accurate statement of customary international law on reparation, that the 1990 Security Council resolutions and the doctrine of act of State did not prevent the English court from characterising the seizure of the aircraft as unlawful as a matter of Iraqi private international law, and awarded damages for conversion. It is also a beautiful bridge back to Topic 3: customary international law influencing the development of English law.

J. What is not attributable — and why the injured State is not left without a remedy

The negative half of the section is as important as the positive one. The acts of private persons, a mob, a company, a terrorist network, an unsuccessful insurrection — and the acts of any group which the State has neither directed nor adopted — are not acts of the State. Yet the injured State is not without a remedy, because the law of responsibility distinguishes four ways in which the State may still be responsible:

  1. Its own failing (Articles 2, 12 and the primary rule). The State's own omission — failure to prevent, to protect, to warn, to punish — is a breach of the duty of due diligence, where such a duty exists. Corfu Channel: Albania's failure to warn shipping of the minefield. Tehran Hostages: the failure to protect the embassy. Genocide (2007): Serbia's failure to prevent the Srebrenica genocide and to punish those responsible. The standard is one of reasonableness, measured by the State's means.
  2. Its own conduct in relation to the acts of others (Articles 16–19). The State that aids or assists another State in committing a wrongful act (Art. 16), directs and controls it (Art. 17), or coerces it (Art. 18), commits its own wrong — hence Nicaragua's formula that the United States "is not responsible for the acts of the contras, but for its own conduct vis-à-vis Nicaragua, including conduct related to the acts of the contras" (¶116).
  3. Adoption (Article 11). The radical cure: the State takes the conduct upon itself.
  4. Special regimes. Genocide (the obligation to prevent and to punish); terrorism and hostage-taking conventions (aut dedere aut judicare); torture (aut dedere aut judicare and universal jurisdiction, as in the Pinochet proceedings); diplomatic protection of aliens (§4.5 below).

Fig. 4.7 — The attribution decision tree of the ILC Articles 4–11 (with Articles 16–18 as the residual route to responsibility).

Examination box — standard questions and how to open

"Discuss the rules of attribution of conduct to a State under international law." Open with Article 2(a) and the proposition that attribution is a rule of international law, not of internal law (Article 3); then walk through Articles 4–11 in order, giving for each the text, the rationale and one case: Art. 4 LaGrand/Tehran; Art. 5 (delegated governmental authority); Art. 6 Behrami v. Nuhanović; Art. 7 Caire; Art. 8 Nicaragua ¶115 with Tadić and the 2007 Genocide case; Art. 9 (Iran 1979); Art. 10 Bolívar Railway and Home Missionary Society; Art. 11 Tehran ¶74. Close with the negative case: what is not attributable, and how the injured State's remedy is preserved through the State's own wrongful conduct (due diligence; Articles 16–18).

"Explain the difference between attribution and the municipal doctrine of act of State." Two paragraphs. Attribution answers "is this the State's conduct?" as a matter of international law (Arts. 4–11); the act of State doctrine (Underhill v. Hernandez; Buttes Gas; Kuwait Airways 1995) is a rule of judicial abstention preventing a domestic court from sitting in judgment on the public acts of a foreign State in its own territory. The sequel Kuwait Airways v. Iraqi Airways (No. 3) [2002] UKHL 19 shows the two doctrines meeting, because the English court had first to decide whether the seizure of the aircraft was an Iraqi "act of State" and then, having held that it was not, awarded reparation consistently with the ILC Articles.

"A State says its officials exceeded their orders. Is it responsible?" Yes — Article 7 — provided they acted in that capacity. Cite Caire and Youmans; distinguish purely private conduct; add Article 3 on internal law; and note that the State may still have a defence under Articles 20–27 (consent, self-defence, countermeasures) but not by reason of the excess of authority alone.

↑ top

K. Breach of an international obligation — Articles 12 to 15

Attribution tells us whose act it is; Chapter III of Part One tells us whether that act is a breach. Article 12 states the test in a single sentence, and the rest of the chapter deals with the dimension of time.

Article 12. "There is a breach of an international obligation by a State when an act of that State is not in conformity with what is required of it by that obligation, regardless of its origin or character."

Article 13. "An act of a State does not constitute a breach of an international obligation unless the State is bound by the obligation in question at the time the act occurs."

Article 14. "(1) The breach of an international obligation by an act of a State not having a continuing character occurs at the moment when the act is performed, even though its effects may continue. (2) The breach of an international obligation by an act of a State having a continuing character extends over the entire period during which the act continues and remains not in conformity with the international obligation. (3) The breach of an international obligation requiring a State to prevent a given event occurs when the event occurs and extends over the entire period during which the event continues and remains not in conformity with that obligation."

Article 15. "(1) The breach of an international obligation by a State through a series of actions or omissions defined in aggregate as wrongful, occurs when the action or omission occurs which, taken with the other actions or omissions, is sufficient to constitute the wrongful act. (2) In such a case, the breach extends over the entire period starting with the first of the actions or omissions of the series and lasts for as long as these actions or omissions are repeated and remain not in conformity with the international obligation."

What each article does — and the case that illustrates it
  • Article 12 — the objective test. "Regardless of its origin or character" means that it does not matter whether the obligation comes from treaty, custom, a general principle or a unilateral act, nor whether it is an obligation of conduct or of result, nor whether it is owed to one State or to the international community. The article also confirms the objective character of responsibility discussed in §4.2.2: the breach is measured by non-conformity, not by intention. Illustrations: the failure to enact a promised law; the detention of a diplomat; the mining of a foreign warship's channel (Corfu Channel); the execution of a national without consular notification (LaGrand); the occupation of foreign territory (DRC v. Uganda).
  • Article 13 — inter-temporal law. An act is a breach only if the obligation bound the State when the act occurred. This is the rule of non-retroactivity applied to responsibility: a State is not responsible for conduct which was lawful when done, even if a later treaty or custom makes such conduct unlawful (compare Article 28 of the Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties and the Island of Palmas principle that a juridical fact must be appreciated in the light of the law contemporary with it). The converse case, of an obligation which existed and was breached before the State's responsibility is invoked, is governed by the ordinary rules on the invocation of claims.
  • Article 14 — the dimension of time. Three situations are distinguished. (i) An instantaneous breach (an unlawful shooting, a refusal, a wrongful expulsion) occurs at the moment of the act, although its effects continue — and this distinction matters for computing interest and for determining the period of responsibility. (ii) A continuing breach (the unlawful detention of a hostage, the maintenance of an occupation, the continued presence of troops, the retention of confiscated property) extends over the whole period, so that the breach is renewed every day: the Tehran Hostages case ("successive and continuing breaches"), the DRC v. Uganda occupation, and the Nicaragua mining and support of the contras. (iii) A breach of an obligation to prevent a given event occurs when the event occurs (Article 14(3)) — the provision which underlies the analysis in the Genocide case (2007) of the duty to prevent, where the Court held that responsibility arises if the State "manifestly failed to take all measures to prevent genocide which were within its power".
  • Article 15 — the composite act. A "composite act" is a breach constituted by a series of actions or omissions defined in aggregate as wrongful — for example, a systematic practice of torture, a campaign of ethnic cleansing, a sustained policy of racial discrimination, or (in the language of Article 40(2)) a "gross or systematic failure" to fulfil an obligation. The breach begins with the first act of the series which, taken with the others, completes the wrongful aggregate. The distinction between "composite" and "continuing" matters for the application of the rules on cessation, on the point at which the claim arises, and on the temporal reach of reparation.
Articles 12–15 in one table
Table 4.9A · The four dimensions of breach
ArticleQuestionConsequenceIllustration
12Is the conduct inconsistent with what the obligation requires?The breach is objective; origin and character of the obligation are irrelevantFailure to notify a consulate (LaGrand); mining (Corfu Channel)
13Was the State bound when the act occurred?No retroactivity; obligations are tested as at the date of conductConduct lawful when done is not made wrongful by later law
14(1)–(2)Is the breach instantaneous or continuing?Continuing breach extends day by day; affects reparation and interestDetention of the Tehran hostages; the occupation in DRC v. Uganda
14(3)Is it an obligation to prevent an event?Breach occurs when the event occursDuty to prevent genocide (Genocide, 2007)
15Is it a composite act?Breach begins with the first act of the seriesSystematic torture; gross or systematic failure (Art. 40(2))

L. Responsibility of a State in connection with the act of another State — Articles 16 to 19

Part One, Chapter IV is a short chapter with large consequences: it makes a State responsible for its own conduct in relation to the wrongful act of another State. It is the provision which supplies the legal answer to the puzzles posed by sanctions, military support, arms transfers and proxy relationships — and it is the chapter to which Nicaragua's famous paragraph 116 ("the United States is not responsible for the acts of the contras, but for its own conduct vis-à-vis Nicaragua, including conduct related to the acts of the contras") gives practical effect.

Article 16 — Aid or assistance in the commission of an internationally wrongful act. "A State which aids or assists another State in the commission of an internationally wrongful act by the latter is internationally responsible for doing so if: (a) That State does so with knowledge of the circumstances of the internationally wrongful act; and (b) The act would be internationally wrongful if committed by that State."

Article 17 — Direction and control exercised over the commission of an internationally wrongful act. "A State which directs and controls another State in the commission of an internationally wrongful act by the latter is internationally responsible for that act if: (a) That State does so with knowledge of the circumstances of the internationally wrongful act; and (b) The act would be internationally wrongful if committed by that State."

Article 18 — Coercion of another State. "A State which coerces another State to commit an act is internationally responsible for that act if: (a) The act would, but for the coercion, be an internationally wrongful act of the coerced State; and (b) The coercing State does so with knowledge of the circumstances of the act."

Article 19 — Effect of this chapter. "This chapter is without prejudice to the international responsibility, under other provisions of these articles, of the State which commits the act in question, or of any other State."

Analysis
  • Article 16 — aiding and assisting. Four elements: assistance (financial, logistical, material, or by the provision of territory or facilities); knowledge of the circumstances of the wrongful act (the ILC's commentary makes clear that the aid must be given with a view to facilitating the act, and that the knowledge must be actual; inadvertent or merely negligent provision of means does not suffice); the act must be wrongful if done by the assisting State; and the wrongfulness of the principal act must be established. The Bosnian Genocide (2007) case discussed the provision — the Court examined whether Serbia's provision of assistance to the Republika Srpska could engage Article 16 and held that the evidence did not establish the required knowledge with respect to the Srebrenica massacres, while finding Serbia responsible for the failure to prevent. It is also the article that operates where a State supplies weapons or funds to a State which uses them in a breach of humanitarian law — a matter of daily importance in the contemporary practice of arms transfers.
  • Article 17 — direction and control. Here the directing State becomes responsible for the act itself, not merely for the assistance: the provision applies to situations of domination and actual direction — the classical example being a State which dictates the policy of another (the ILC's commentary refers to the "protectorate" and the puppet-State cases). It is distinct from Article 8 (which directs the acts of persons), because Article 17 concerns the direction of another State's acts.
  • Article 18 — coercion. A State which by force majeure-like pressure compels another State to commit a wrongful act answers for that act — the classic illustration being the coercion of a State into allowing its territory to be used, or the coercion of a government into signing an agreement. The coerced State remains responsible unless one of the circumstances precluding wrongfulness (force majeure, distress, necessity) applies to it; but the coercing State answers in any event.
  • Article 19 — cumulative responsibility. The chapter is without prejudice to the responsibility of the State that actually commits the act. Two or more States may therefore each be responsible for the same act on different bases (as principal or as aider), and Article 47 (plurality of responsible States) regulates the consequences: each responsible State may be invoked separately, no injured State may recover more than the damage suffered, and the responsible States have rights of recourse among themselves (DRC v. Uganda's treatment of the DRC's counter-claim and the Eritrea–Ethiopia Claims Commission's parallel award of compensation to both sides is a practical illustration of the phenomenon of mutual responsibility).
  • The relationship with Articles 4–11. Articles 16–19 are not substitutes for attribution; they are additional routes. If the act itself is attributable (Arts. 4–11), the State is responsible for it directly; if the act is not attributable but the State assisted, directed or coerced it, the State is responsible for its own conduct under Chapter IV — exactly the structure the ICJ used in Nicaragua (¶116) and in the Genocide case (2007). Anyone who has understood this distinction has understood the architecture of Part One.
Examination box

"Distinguish between attribution and complicity." Attribution (Arts. 4–11) answers "is this conduct the State's own?"; complicity (Arts. 16–19) answers "is the State responsible for its own conduct in relation to another State's wrongful act?" Apply it: a State whose soldiers trained and armed a militia is not, without more, responsible for the militia's atrocities (Nicaragua, ¶115); but it is responsible for its own acts of support if they breach an obligation owed to the victim State (¶116), and it may be responsible under Article 16 if its assistance was given with knowledge that the acts would be committed. Close with Article 19 and the principle of cumulative responsibility.

"What is a continuing breach, and why does it matter?" Article 14(2): a continuing breach extends over the entire period during which the act continues. It matters for: the calculation of reparation and interest (the longer the breach, the larger the loss); the availability of provisional measures (Tehran, LaGrand, DRC v. Uganda); the question whether the claim is premature or time-barred; and the availability of countermeasures, which end when "the internationally wrongful act has ceased" (Article 52(3)(a)). Cite Tehran Hostages ("successive and continuing breaches") and the occupation in DRC v. Uganda.

↑ top

Case 27 · LaGrand Case (Germany v. United States of America)

LaGrand Case (Germany v. United States of America)

ICJ Reports 2001, p. 466 (Judgment of 27 June 2001) · Order indicating Provisional Measures of 3 March 1999, ICJ Reports 1999, p. 9 · Bench: President Guillaume; Vice-President Shi; Judges Oda, Bedjaoui, Ranjeva, Herczegh, Fleischhauer, Koroma, Vereshchetin, Higgins, Kooijmans, Rezek, Al-Khasawneh, Buergenthal · Jurisdiction: Article I of the Optional Protocol concerning the Compulsory Settlement of Disputes to the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963

Provenance. The case material prints the judgment in the Topic-4 chapter (pp. 90–98, paragraphs 73–127 as excerpted). The account below follows that text; the operative paragraphs are taken from the judgment itself.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus — and where it fits

The syllabus places LaGrand under §4.3 "The Act of State (Rules of Attribution)" — and the placement is deliberate, though the case is famous for three other things. Its attribution dimension is this: the breaches were committed by the courts, the executive and the prosecuting authorities of a constituent State (Arizona) of the United States, and the United States could not point to the internal distribution of powers between the federal Union and its States. Article 4 of the ILC Articles covers the conduct of every organ "whatever position it holds in the organization of the State, and whatever its character as an organ of the central government or of a territorial unit of the State"; and Article 3 provides that the characterisation of an act as internationally wrongful is governed by international law and cannot be affected by its characterisation as lawful by internal law. LaGrand is the practical demonstration of both propositions in the criminal-justice context — and it is the most recent case in the syllabus in which the consequences of responsibility (cessation, assurance of non-repetition, reparation falling short of compensation) were worked out in detail. It also produced the single most important procedural decision of the modern Court: that orders indicating provisional measures under Article 41 of the Statute are legally binding.

2. Facts

(a) The arrests and the trials. The LaGrand brothers, Karl and Walter, were German nationals, born and raised in Germany, though they had lived much of their lives in the United States. In 1982 they were arrested in Arizona and charged with armed robbery and first-degree murder. They were tried, convicted and sentenced to death (1984). At no time — not on arrest, not on detention, not before or during trial — were they informed, "without delay", of their right under Article 36(1)(b) of the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963 to have their consulate informed of their detention and to communicate with it. Nor did the Arizona authorities at any time notify the German consular post of the detention. The German consulate learned of the case only in 1992 — by which time the appeals were far advanced — when Karl LaGrand himself wrote to it. By 1999, the judicial remedies had long been exhausted and the procedural default rule of United States law barred the brothers from raising the VCCR point in any United States court.

(b) Germany's efforts and the executions. After Germany learned of the case its consular officers did what they could — visiting the brothers, securing counsel, seeking clemency. On 24 February 1999 Karl LaGrand was executed. On 2 March 1999 Germany filed its Application instituting proceedings against the United States. On 3 March 1999, the day fixed for Walter's execution, the Court made an Order indicating provisional measures: the United States "should take all measures at its disposal to ensure that Walter LaGrand is not executed pending the final decision of the Court", and "should transmit this Order to the Governor of the State of Arizona". The United States Solicitor General filed a brief in the United States Supreme Court stating that "an order of the International Court of Justice indicating provisional measures is not binding and does not furnish a basis for judicial relief". The Governor of Arizona declined to act, even though the Arizona Clemency Board had recommended a stay; the Supreme Court refused a stay; and Walter LaGrand was executed on 3 March 1999.

(c) The case. Germany asked the Court to adjudge and declare that the United States had violated its obligations under Article 36(1)(a) and (b) and Article 36(2) of the VCCR; that the United States had violated the Court's Order of 3 March 1999; and that the United States must (i) cease the violations, (ii) give an assurance of non-repetition, and (iii) provide review and reconsideration of the convictions and sentences by reference to the violations of the VCCR. The United States did not dispute that it had failed to notify, apologised for that failure, and pleaded its extensive new programme of consular-notification training (a brochure, a pocket card for arresting officers, a permanent office in the Department of State) in answer to Germany's demand for assurances. It denied that Article 36 created individual rights, argued that its "procedural default" rule was lawful in itself and had not prevented the brothers from raising the point (they had learned of their rights in 1992), and — most importantly — contended that provisional measures were not binding.

3. The issues

  1. Whether the Court had jurisdiction and the claims were admissible under the Optional Protocol to the VCCR, and whether Germany's conduct (its own late diligence) affected admissibility.
  2. Whether Article 36(1) of the VCCR creates individual rights, or only rights of the sending State.
  3. Whether the United States' failure to inform breached Article 36(1)(b), and whether its application of the "procedural default" rule to the brothers breached Article 36(2).
  4. Whether orders indicating provisional measures under Article 41 of the Statute are binding, and whether the United States breached the Order of 3 March 1999.
  5. What consequences followed: cessation, assurances and guarantees of non-repetition, review and reconsideration, and reparation.

4. The arguments of both sides

For Germany

(i) Article 36(1)(b) imposes a duty on the receiving State to inform the detained individual "without delay", and the concluding words of that subparagraph ("The said authorities shall inform the person concerned without delay of his rights under this subparagraph") show that the provision is intended to protect the individual; the rights may therefore be invoked by the national State before the Court under the Optional Protocol.

(ii) Article 36(2) requires the receiving State to "enable full effect to be given to the purposes for which the rights accorded under this Article are intended"; the application of the procedural default rule to bar the VCCR claim defeated that purpose.

(iii) The Court's Order of 3 March 1999 was binding; the United States' Solicitor General's statement that it was "not binding" aggravated the breach; the United States failed to take the measures at its disposal, including the transmission of the Order to the Governor of Arizona.

(iv) There is a causal link between the breach of Article 36 and the execution of the brothers: with timely consular assistance, the German Government would have been able to do far more — including, as Germany had done successfully in other cases, to influence the proceeding and secure a lesser sentence or clemency.

(v) An apology is not sufficient; Germany asked for specific assurances of non-repetition and for the review and reconsideration of the convictions and sentences of German nationals sentenced to severe penalties in similar circumstances.

For the United States

(i) Non-notification occurred, and the United States apologised for it; but Germany's own conduct — it was German law and German inaction that prevented Germany from exercising its rights — made the claims inadmissible.

(ii) Article 36 confers rights on the sending State, not on individuals; the preamble and structure of the VCCR are interstate; the claim that there was an "individual right" would transform the treaty into a human rights instrument.

(iii) The "procedural default" rule does not violate Article 36; the brothers knew of their rights from 1992 and could have raised them; the rule is applied only when the defendant has failed to raise an issue in a timely fashion and no exception applies.

(iv) Provisional measures under Article 41 are not binding: the provision says that the Court "may indicate" measures, uses the weaker word "ought", and Article 94 of the Charter speaks only of compliance with "judgments".

(v) The United States has in any event responded to the problem by a vast and detailed programme of notification, training and reference cards for arresting officers at federal, State and local levels; that programme is the best assurance available in a federal system; no State could guarantee absence of error.

5. The decision

(a) Jurisdiction and admissibility

By 14 votes to 1 the Court held that it had jurisdiction under Article I of the Optional Protocol, and by 14 to 1 and 14 to 1 (subparagraphs) that Germany's submissions were admissible. The Court rejected the United States' reliance on Germany's own conduct: the United States "may not now rely on this fact in order to preclude the admissibility of Germany's first submission, as it was the United States itself which had failed to carry out its obligation to inform the LaGrand brothers". The Court also rejected the suggestion that Germany's claim of diplomatic protection added anything separate: the claim was Germany's own.

(b) Article 36(1) creates individual rights (¶¶73–77)

This is the holding that made the case a landmark of the "individual in international law" discussion that belongs to Topics 1 and 3. Having examined the text and structure of Article 36 — the "interrelated régime" under which the receiving State must inform the consular post, must forward communications "without delay", and must inform the detained person of his rights — the Court said:

"The clarity of these provisions, viewed in their context, admits of no doubt. … Based on the text of these provisions, the Court concludes that Article 36, paragraph 1, creates individual rights, which, by virtue of Article 1 of the Optional Protocol, may be invoked in this Court by the national State of the detained person. These rights were violated in the present case." (¶77)

And at ¶89, extending the point to the second limb: "The Court has already determined that Article 36, paragraph 1, creates individual rights for the detained person in addition to the rights accorded the sending State, and that consequently the reference to 'rights' in paragraph 2 must be read as applying not only to the rights of the sending State, but also to the rights of the detained individual."

(c) Article 36(2) and the "procedural default" rule (¶¶79–91)

The Court drew a sharp distinction "between that rule as such and its specific application in the present case. In itself, the rule does not violate Article 36 of the Vienna Convention. The problem arises when the procedural default rule does not allow the detained individual to challenge a conviction and sentence by claiming, in reliance on Article 36, paragraph 1, of the Convention, that the competent national authorities failed to comply with their obligation to provide the requisite consular notification" (¶90). On the facts the rule had precisely that effect, and the United States therefore breached Article 36(2) — a finding the Court later reinforced by refusing to allow the United States to characterise the point as one of German diligence: the "procedural default" defence defeated the very purpose of the obligation.

(d) Provisional measures are binding (¶¶92–109) — the case's most-cited holding

Article 41 of the Statute provides that the Court "shall have the power to indicate, if it considers that circumstances so require, any provisional measures which ought to be taken to preserve the respective rights of either party". Until LaGrand it was widely (though not universally) believed that such orders were merely recommendatory. The Court reviewed the object and purpose of Article 41, the travaux préparatoires, the practice of the Court and of the Permanent Court, the "principle universally accepted by international tribunals" enunciated in the Electricity Company of Sofia and Bulgaria order of 1939 (that the parties must abstain from any measure capable of exercising a prejudicial effect in regard to the execution of the decision to be given, and must not aggravate or extend the dispute), and Article 94 of the Charter — concluding that "Article 94 of the Charter does not prevent orders made under Article 41 from having a binding character", and finally:

"In short, it is clear that none of the sources of interpretation referred to in the relevant Articles of the Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties, including the preparatory work, contradict the conclusions drawn from the terms of Article 41 read in their context and in the light of the object and purpose of the Statute. Thus, the Court has reached the conclusion that orders on provisional measures under Article 41 have binding effect." (¶109)

"The Court will now consider the Order of 3 March 1999. This Order was not a mere exhortation. It had been adopted pursuant to Article 41 of the Statute. This Order was consequently binding in character and created a legal obligation for the United States." (¶110)

The Court found that the United States had failed to take all measures at its disposal — the Solicitor General's categorical statement that the order was "not binding" was itself incompatible with the Order, the Governor of Arizona had refused to act notwithstanding the recommendation of the Clemency Board, and the Supreme Court had declined a stay although, as one of its members urged, a preliminary stay would have allowed time to consider the jurisdictional and international legal issues. By 13 votes to 2 the Court found that the United States had breached the obligation incumbent on it under the Order.

(e) The consequences: apology, assurances, review and reconsideration (¶¶110–127)

Germany had not asked for compensation; it asked for cessation, assurances, and a remedy capable of giving effect to the Convention in future cases. On the assurance of non-repetition:

"The United States has presented an apology to Germany for this breach. The Court considers however that an apology is not sufficient in this case, as it would not be in other cases where foreign nationals have not been advised without delay of their rights under Article 36, paragraph 1, of the Vienna Convention and have been subjected to prolonged detention or sentenced to severe penalties." (¶123)

"The Court considers that the commitment expressed by the United States to ensure implementation of the specific measures adopted in performance of its obligations under Article 36, paragraph 1 (b), must be regarded as meeting Germany's request for a general assurance of non-repetition." (¶124)

"[I]f the United States, notwithstanding its commitment …, should fail in its obligation of consular notification to the detriment of German nationals, an apology would not suffice in cases where the individuals concerned have been subjected to prolonged detention or convicted and sentenced to severe penalties. In the case of such a conviction and sentence, it would be incumbent upon the United States to allow the review and reconsideration of the conviction and sentence by taking account of the violation of the rights set forth in the Convention. This obligation can be carried out in various ways. The choice of means must be left to the United States." (¶125)

Note the fine allocation of remedial functions in paragraph 125: the violation of Article 36(2) "was caused by the circumstances in which the procedural default rule was applied, and not by the rule as such" — an important illustration of the difference between a law and its application (compare the ILC's treatment of judicial and legislative acts under Article 4). The operative paragraphs of the judgment (para. 128) accordingly provide: by 14 to 1, that the United States breached its obligations under Article 36(1) by not informing the brothers without delay; by 14 to 1, that it breached Article 36(2) by not permitting review and reconsideration of the convictions and sentences; by 13 to 2, that it breached the obligation under the Order of 3 March 1999; unanimously, that the United States' commitment to implement the specific measures adopted in performance of its obligations "must be regarded as meeting Germany's request for a general assurance of non-repetition"; and by 14 to 1, that "should nationals of Germany nonetheless be sentenced to severe penalties, without their rights under Article 36, paragraph 1 (b) … having been respected, the United States, by means of its own choosing, shall allow the review and reconsideration of the conviction and sentence by taking account of the violation of the rights set forth in that Convention".

Aftermath. The "review and reconsideration" duty of LaGrand was applied and elaborated in Avena and Other Mexican Nationals (Mexico v. United States), ICJ Reports 2004, p. 12, where the Court held that review and reconsideration must be "meaningful and effective" and that clemency proceedings alone do not satisfy Article 36(2) — and that the presence of the procedural default rule made ex officio review necessary (Avena, ¶¶138–144). In Medellín v. Texas, 552 U.S. 491 (2008) the United States Supreme Court held that the Avena judgment was not directly enforceable as domestic law in the absence of implementing legislation, despite a memorandum of the President purporting to give it effect; the United States subsequently withdrew from the Optional Protocol to the VCCR (2005). The episode is a standing illustration, for the "relationship of international law and municipal law" topic (Topic 3), of a monist-sounding international obligation meeting a dualist municipal machinery — and the reason the ILC's Article 32 (irrelevance of internal law) exists.

6. The principles evolved

  1. Article 4 in operation. A State is responsible for the acts of the courts, prosecutors, governors and police of its constituent units — the internal federal division of powers is invisible to international responsibility. The United States did not dispute responsibility; it disputed the content of the obligation.
  2. Provisional measures are binding. Orders under Article 41 of the Statute create legal obligations; they are "not a mere exhortation". This is now the settled law (reaffirmed in Armed Activities (DRC v. Uganda), 2000, and in Georgia v. Russia, 2008, 2011).
  3. Individual rights under treaty regimes. A treaty may confer rights upon individuals which are invocable by their national State before the ICJ; the VCCR Article 36 régime is an "interrelated" one of State and individual rights.
  4. Apology is not enough. Where the breach is grave — prolonged detention or a severe sentence — satisfaction by apology must be supplemented by cessation and by a guarantee of non-repetition in the form of a concrete remedy (review and reconsideration).
  5. The choice of means belongs to the responsible State. "This obligation can be carried out in various ways. The choice of means must be left to the United States" — a text-book illustration of the principle that a responsible State has the discretion to choose the manner in which it discharges a duty of reparation, provided the result is achieved (compare Article 34 of the ILC Articles and its commentary).
  6. Law vs. application. The distinction between the procedural default rule "as such" and "its specific application" is a glimpse of the rule that a State's general legislative framework may be lawful, while its operation in a particular case produces responsibility.
  7. Reparation can be non-material. Germany claimed no compensation; the remedy was a declaration, an order of review and reconsideration, and the assurance of non-repetition. The case is therefore the standard authority for the proposition that counterbalancing satisfaction — and not money — may be the appropriate remedy for serious breaches which injure the dignity and interests of a State.

7. The examination angle

Examination box

If asked: "What are the consequences of an internationally wrongful act? Discuss with reference to LaGrand." Structure the answer around the judgment itself: (i) cessation — the obligation to stop the continuing breach; (ii) assurance and guarantee of non-repetition — the Court's acceptance of the United States' programme as a sufficient "general assurance", with the warning that an apology will not suffice in serious cases (¶¶123–124); (iii) reparation — no damages were claimed, and the Court held that the appropriate remedy was "review and reconsideration" (¶125); (iv) the binding character of provisional measures as an obligation arising from the Court's order, breach of which engages responsibility on its own account (¶110). Add the ILC articles: cessation and assurances (Art. 30), reparation (Arts. 31, 34–37), satisfaction (Art. 37).

If asked: "Is a State responsible for the acts of its courts and of the organs of its constituent units?" Answer with Article 4(1) ("whatever character as an organ of the central government or of a territorial unit of the State"), Article 3 (internal law is irrelevant), Article 32 (a State may not invoke internal law to justify a failure to comply with an international obligation) and Article 7 (ultra vires) — and then apply LaGrand (Arizona's courts and Governor; the United States responsible), Medellín (the difficulty of enforcing the obligation inside the United States), and Article 6 for organs placed at the disposal of another State.

If asked: "Do individual rights under Article 36 VCCR survive, and how are they enforced?" LaGrand ¶77 (individual rights), Avena ¶¶138–144 (meaningful and effective review), Medellín (enforcement problems), and Article 44(b)/the exhaustion rule (§4.5) — because the individual's right under consular law is one of the few international rights of the individual expressly enforceable through the State's own courts and then through the ICJ.

↑ top

Circumstances Precluding Wrongfulness — the "Defences" under Articles 20 to 27

Attribution (Chapter II) tells us whether the conduct is the State's; breach (Chapter III) tells us whether the conduct conforms to the obligation. Chapter V of Part One answers the last question of the sequence: even though the conduct is the State's and even though it does not conform to the obligation, is the wrongfulness nevertheless precluded? The six answers given by the ILC — consent, self-defence, countermeasures, force majeure, distress and necessity — are contained in Articles 20 to 27, whose full text, as printed in the course material, is set out below and then examined defence by defence.

(1) The text of Articles 20 to 27 (as printed in the course material)

Article 20 — Consent. Valid consent by a State to the commission of a given act by another State precludes the wrongfulness of that act in relation to the former State to the extent that the act remains within the limits of that consent.

Article 21 — Self-defence. The wrongfulness of an act of a State is precluded if the act constitutes a lawful measure of self-defence taken in conformity with the Charter of the United Nations.

Article 22 — Countermeasures in respect of an internationally wrongful act. The wrongfulness of an act of a State not in conformity with an international obligation towards another State is precluded if and to the extent that the act constitutes a countermeasure taken against the latter State in accordance with chapter II of Part Three.

Article 23 — Force majeure. 1.
