The wrongfulness of an act of a State not in conformity with an international obligation of that State is precluded if the act is due to force majeure, that is the occurrence of an irresistible force or of an unforeseen event, beyond the control of the State, making it materially impossible in the circumstances to perform the obligation. 2. Paragraph 1 does not apply if: (a) the situation of force majeure is due, either alone or in combination with other factors, to the conduct of the State invoking it; or (b) the State has assumed the risk of that situation occurring.

Article 24 — Distress. 1. The wrongfulness of an act of a State not in conformity with an international obligation of that State is precluded if the author of the act in question has no other reasonable way, in a situation of distress, of saving the author's life or the lives of other persons entrusted to the author's care. 2. Paragraph 1 does not apply if: (a) the situation of distress is due, either alone or in combination with other factors, to the conduct of the State invoking it; or (b) the act in question is likely to create a comparable or greater peril.

Article 25 — Necessity. 1. Necessity may not be invoked by a State as a ground for precluding the wrongfulness of an act not in conformity with an international obligation of that State unless the act: (a) is the only way for the State to safeguard an essential interest against a grave and imminent peril; and (b) does not seriously impair an essential interest of the State or States towards which the obligation exists, or of the international community as a whole. 2. In any case, necessity may not be invoked by a State as a ground for precluding wrongfulness if: (a) the international obligation in question excludes the possibility of invoking necessity; or (b) the State has contributed to the situation of necessity.

Article 26 — Compliance with peremptory norms. Nothing in this chapter precludes the wrongfulness of any act of a State which is not in conformity with an obligation arising under a peremptory norm of general international law.

Article 27 — Consequences of invoking a circumstance precluding wrongfulness. The invocation of a circumstance precluding wrongfulness in accordance with this chapter is without prejudice to: (a) compliance with the obligation in question, if and to the extent that the circumstance precluding wrongfulness no longer exists; (b) the question of compensation for any material loss caused by the act in question.

(2) Six general propositions about the whole chapter

  1. They preclude wrongfulness, not responsibility. If the act was wrongful when done but the circumstance arises later, the responsibility already incurred is unaffected. If the circumstance exists at the time, the act is simply not wrongful; there is nothing to repair. This is why the chapter is called "circumstances precluding wrongfulness" and not "defences" or "excuses".
  2. They are temporary and do not terminate the obligation. Article 27(a): the moment the circumstance disappears, the obligation revives in full. This is the point the ICJ made in Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros when it refused to accept that a state of necessity could be equated with the termination or suspension of a treaty: "a state of necessity ... may not be invoked as a ground for terminating a treaty", and the Court endorsed the ILC's (1980) view that "as soon as the state of necessity ceases to exist, the duty to comply with treaty obligations revives". Contrast the VCLT's grounds for termination or suspension (Arts. 61 and 62: supervening impossibility and fundamental change of circumstances), which operate on the treaty itself.
  3. They may still require compensation. Article 27(b) preserves "the question of compensation for any material loss caused by the act in question". So a ship that enters a foreign port in distress and damages the harbour may be excused of wrongfulness but not necessarily of the obligation to pay for the damage. This is one of the places where the "risk" idea studied in §4.2.3 shows through the ILC Articles.
  4. Some obligations admit of no excuse whatsoever. Article 26: nothing in the chapter precludes wrongfulness for a breach of a peremptory norm (aggression, genocide, torture, slavery, basic rules of international humanitarian law, the right of self-determination). A State cannot lawfully consent to its own genocide, cannot plead necessity to justify aggression, cannot take countermeasures that violate human rights or humanitarian law (Article 50).
  5. Self-induced circumstances are excluded. Articles 23(2)(a), 24(2)(a) and 25(2)(b) each deny the defence where the State contributed to the situation. A State cannot create the peril and then shelter behind it.
  6. The burden lies on the State invoking the circumstance, and the conditions are cumulative. Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros, ¶51: "the state of necessity can only be invoked under certain strictly defined conditions which must be cumulatively satisfied; and the State concerned is not the sole judge of whether those conditions have been met." And the Court's caution (¶51): necessity is "a ground recognized by customary international law ... only ... on an exceptional basis".

(3) Article 20 — Consent

Elements. A valid consent given by a State to the commission of a given act by another State precludes wrongfulness in relation to the consenting State only (so consent by State A cannot justify an act against State B), and "to the extent that the act remains within the limits of that consent" (so an act which goes beyond the consent is wrongful for the excess). Consent must be valid: attributable to the State, given by an authority competent to bind it, freely given and not vitiated by coercion or error, and clear. It may be given in advance or at the time; it may be withdrawn; and it cannot be presumed — the State that pleads consent must prove it.

Classical illustrations. Consent to the presence of foreign armed forces, to overflight, to the stationing of foreign officials, to the search of a vessel or the seizure of property, to the exercise of jurisdiction on the territory (the classic case in the Lotus, 1927, is about the absence, not the existence, of a prohibitive rule; the Savarkar arbitration, 1911, turned partly on the absence of consent). Modern examples abound: status-of-forces agreements, consent to humanitarian relief operations, consent to the enforcement of a foreign judgment, and the "government of the Republic of X consents to the entry of troops of State Y to fight a rebellion".

The leading modern case: Armed Activities on the Territory of the Congo (DRC v. Uganda), ICJ 2005 (Case 29 below). The Democratic Republic of the Congo consented to the presence of Ugandan troops in its territory for a limited purpose — to fight the anti-Ugandan rebels (ADF) along the common border, and later, by the Lusaka Agreement, for a United Nations-monitored withdrawal. The Court held that consent was limited in scope and time; Uganda could not rely upon it to justify the occupation of Ituri, the establishment of a military administration, the training of local forces and the exploitation of Congolese resources (¶¶42–54, 97–105, 160–165). Consent, in short, is not a licence: the acts must stay within the limits of the consent, and the burden is on the State relying upon it.

Limits. (i) Consent cannot preclude wrongfulness towards third States. (ii) Consent cannot validate a breach of a peremptory norm (Article 26): a government cannot consent to its own destruction or to the commission of genocide within its territory, and purported consent procured by coercion is invalid. (iii) Consent cannot displace the rights of other States under a multilateral treaty régime, e.g. consent to a position inconsistent with the fundamental obligations of a disarmament or human rights treaty — a more contested proposition, but one that follows from the general structure of obligations erga omnes partes.

(4) Article 21 — Self-defence

The wrongfulness of an act is precluded "if the act constitutes a lawful measure of self-defence taken in conformity with the Charter". The Charter's rule is Article 51: "Nothing in the present Charter shall impair the inherent right of individual or collective self-defence if an armed attack occurs against a Member of the United Nations, until the Security Council has taken measures necessary to maintain international peace and security." The conditions of a lawful exercise are well settled and each is examined in the DU course under the law of the use of force: (i) an armed attack — "the most grave forms of the use of force" (Nicaragua, ¶191); (ii) necessity and proportionality — the Caroline formula as stated by Webster in 1842 ("a necessity of self-defence, instant, overwhelming, leaving no choice of means and no moment for deliberation") and the "scale and effects" principle; (iii) immediacy; (iv) in the collective case, a request by the victim State which must have declared itself attacked (Nicaragua, ¶199); and (v) reporting to the Security Council (Article 51, second sentence; Nicaragua, ¶¶200, 235).

Cases to cite.

  • Nicaragua (1986). The United States' plea of collective self-defence failed for want of an armed attack, want of request and want of declaration (¶¶194–238), and the Court took the opportunity to hold that "the right of self-defence ... is subject to the conditions of necessity and proportionality" (Oil Platforms added "and ... must be measured against the specific rule").
  • Oil Platforms (Iran v. United States), ICJ 2003, ¶¶43–77. The United States' attacks on Iranian oil platforms were justified as self-defence against Iranian attacks on shipping. The Court, applying the 1955 Treaty's freedom-of-commerce clause and the requirements of self-defence, held (i) that the mining of the USS Samuel B. Roberts could not be attributed to Iran with the required certainty and (ii) that, in any event, the Court "was not satisfied that the attacks were necessary and proportionate" — so the plea failed. The case is the standard modern authority on the evidentiary burden in self-defence and on the requirement of proportionality.
  • Armed Activities (DRC v. Uganda), 2005, ¶¶143–147. Uganda pleaded self-defence against attacks by the ADF operating from the Congo. The Court rejected the plea: there was no armed attack by the DRC against Uganda; the Ugandan operations were not proportionate; and in any event the long occupation could not be justified as self-defence, since "self-defence does not permit the occupying Power to remain in the occupied territory". The case is a useful companion to the consent point above, since both defences were pleaded together and both failed.
  • Legal Consequences of the Construction of a Wall, Advisory Opinion, ICJ 2004, ¶¶138–141. The Court held that Article 51 has no application where the threat to the State does not emanate from another State but "originates within, and remains within" the territory under the State's own control — the most debated single sentence in the modern law of self-defence, and the point at which the drafting of Article 21 (which refers to "the Charter") interacts with the contested question of self-defence against non-State actors.
  • Corfu Channel (1949) — the negative illustration. The United Kingdom's minesweeping ("Operation Retail") was said to be necessary for the protection of its warships and for the preservation of the evidence. The Court rejected the plea: "The Court can only regard the alleged right of intervention as the manifestation of a policy of force, such as has, in the past, given rise to most serious abuses and such as cannot, whatever be the present defects in international organization, find a place in international law." Intervention, self-help and "self-protection" are not circumstances precluding wrongfulness.

(5) Article 22 — Countermeasures (with Articles 49 to 54)

A countermeasure (what used to be called a "reprisal") is conduct which would otherwise be unlawful but which is excused because it is taken against a State responsible for an internationally wrongful act, in order to induce it to comply with its obligations. The ills of countermeasures are notorious — they let the injured State judge its own cause and risk escalation — and the ILC therefore fenced them with an elaborate regime in Part Three, Chapter II (Articles 49 to 54), to which Article 22 cross-refers. That regime is one of the examinable core items of Topic 4, and it should be reproduced in any answer on this section:

Table 4.10 · The six gates a lawful countermeasure must pass (Articles 49–54 ILC)
ArticleRulePractical content
Art. 49(1)–(2)
Object and limits		An injured State may only take countermeasures against a State which is responsible for an internationally wrongful act, and only "in order to induce that State to comply with its obligations"; they must "as far as possible" be taken in such a way as to permit the resumption of performance.The purpose is not punishment, but compliance (cessation + reparation). Countermeasures are therefore temporary and reversible.
Art. 49(3)
Reversibility		Countermeasures "shall, as far as possible, be taken in such a way as to permit the resumption of performance of the obligations in question".You may suspend a treaty obligation (e.g., an air-services agreement) — you may not annihilate a population or destroy an asset which cannot be restored. This is the very reason why the ICJ, in Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros, refused to treat Czechoslovakia's irreversible diversion of the Danube as a lawful countermeasure.
Art. 50(1)
Absolute prohibitions		No countermeasures affecting: (a) the obligation to refrain from the threat or use of force; (b) obligations for the protection of fundamental human rights; (c) obligations of a humanitarian character prohibiting reprisals; (d) other obligations under peremptory norms.This is the modern and hard-won content of the rule: no hostage-taking, no starvation of civilians, no torture, no collective punishment, whatever the provocation.
Art. 50(2)
Dispute-settlement and inviolability		No countermeasure may excuse a State from (a) its obligations under any dispute-settlement procedure applicable between it and the responsible State; or (b) its obligations to respect the inviolability of diplomatic and consular agents, premises, archives and documents.This is the direct legislative consequence of Tehran Hostages (Case 25) and of the "self-contained régime" of diplomatic law. Whatever Iran's grievances, the sacrosanct character of embassies could not be traded away.
Art. 51
Proportionality		Countermeasures must be "commensurate with the injury suffered, taking into account the gravity of the internationally wrongful act and the rights in question".Not an eye-for-an-eye rule: a proportionate countermeasure need not be equivalent, but must not be manifestly excessive. Naulilaa (1928); Air Services Agreement (1978); Gabčíkovo (1997).
Art. 52
Procedural conditions		Before taking countermeasures, the injured State must (a) call upon the responsible State to fulfil its obligations; and (b) notify it of the decision to take countermeasures and offer to negotiate. Urgent measures necessary to preserve rights may be taken in the meantime. Countermeasures may not be taken, and if taken must be suspended, if the wrongful act has ceased and the dispute is pending before a court or tribunal with authority to make binding decisions, unless the responsible State fails to implement in good faith.The "cooling-off" and "judicialization" conditions: countermeasures are lawful only as a last resort and must yield to adjudication. This is why the ICJ in Gabčíkovo emphasised that Czechoslovakia should have negotiated.
Art. 53
Termination		Countermeasures must be terminated as soon as the responsible State complies with its obligations in respect of the wrongful act.An ongoing countermeasure after compliance is itself an unlawful act.
Art. 54
Non-injured States		This chapter does not prejudice the right of any State "to take lawful measures against that State to ensure cessation ... and reparation ... in the interest of the injured State or of the beneficiaries" of the obligation breached.The much-debated gateway to "collective countermeasures". The ILC deliberately used the word "lawful measures" rather than conferring a right, because State practice was sparse and contested. Read with Article 48 (invocation by a State other than the injured State).
The case law on countermeasures
  • Naulilaa Incident (Portugal v. Germany), 1928, 2 UNRIAA 1013. After the killing of three German officials in Portuguese Angola (an incident not attributable to Portugal), Germany attacked Portuguese posts. The tribunal held the German reprisals unlawful and stated the conditions which the modern law adopted: there must be a prior wrongful act; the reprisal must be preceded by an unsuccessful demand for redress; and the reprisal must be proportionate to the wrong. It is the birth certificate of the modern law of countermeasures.
  • Air Services Agreement Arbitration (United States v. France), 1978, 54 ILR 303. France, in response to the United States' change of gauge by Pan American, restricted the London–Paris route; the United States counter-suspended Air France's Los Angeles service. The tribunal held the United States' measure lawful — a proportionate response, taken after negotiation had failed — and articulated the test of proportionality by reference to the aims and effect of the measure ("the extent of the breach" and the "purpose of the countermeasure"). It is the modern counterpart of Naulilaa and the authority most cited for the proposition that countermeasures must be graduated and that lesser measures must be tried first.
  • Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros (Hungary/Slovakia), 1997, ¶¶82–87. Czechoslovakia argued that its unilateral diversion of the Danube ("Variant C") was a lawful countermeasure against Hungary's suspension of the works. The Court rejected the plea: the countermeasure must be proportionate, and the diversion — a unilateral, physical, permanent re-engineering of the river — was not "commensurate with the injury suffered"; the Court added that a State resorting to countermeasures must have attempted to settle the dispute. The judgment is the leading modern application of Articles 49–52 and, of itself, a demonstration that countermeasures rarely pass the tests.
  • Nicaragua (1986) — the United States did not plead countermeasures as such (it pleaded collective self-defence), but the Court's findings that the United States' support of the contras and its mining violated the non-intervention and use-of-force rules leave no room for countermeasures where the response affects the prohibition on the use of force (Article 50(1)(a)).
  • The contemporary practice. (i) Diplomatic expulsions and the withdrawal of ambassadors in mass (e.g., the 1995–2001 espionage expulsions, and the 2018 expulsions after the Skripal poisoning — the largest collective expulsion in the modern history of NATO and the EU). (ii) Economic measures: the measures taken within the WTO framework are not the ILC's countermeasures but a lex specialis (retaliation authorised by a dispute-settlement body, e.g. the EU's 2002 retaliation against US steel tariffs, or the long-running US–EU FSC dispute); outside treaty regimes, measures such as asset freezes and export controls raise the question of proportionality. (iii) The "suspension of development assistance" and "suspension of overflight rights" are classic reversible options. (iv) The 2022 and 2023 waves of measures against Russia following the invasion of Ukraine are analysed as simultaneous instances of countermeasures, measures in response to a breach of peremptory norms, and third-State reactions — the most recent illustration of Article 54's open question.

Countermeasures distinguished from "retorsion". An unfriendly but lawful act — the recall of an ambassador, the refusal of a visa, the suspension of voluntary aid, the closure of a consulate — is retorsion, and needs no legal justification because it breaches no obligation. A countermeasure is prima facie unlawful and needs the shield of Article 22. Candidates should also keep the two apart from (i) sanctions ordered by the Security Council under Chapter VII, which are obligations rather than countermeasures; (ii) suspension of a treaty for material breach under Article 60 of the VCLT, which operates in treaty law and not in the law of responsibility (though the two may coincide); and (iii) self-defence, which is governed by Article 51 of the Charter and not by the countermeasures regime.

(6) Article 23 — Force majeure

Content. "An irresistible force or … an unforeseen event, beyond the control of the State, making it materially impossible in the circumstances to perform the obligation." The defence is about impossibility, not about difficulty, expense or inconvenience. It is excluded where the situation is due to the State's own conduct, or where the State has assumed the risk of its occurring (Article 23(2)) — the second limb is the reason a State which contracts to perform a task in a zone of known danger cannot escape by pointing to that danger.

Illustrations. (i) A naval vessel driven into a foreign port by a storm and unable to leave; (ii) an aircraft forced to land and overfly without authorisation; (iii) a State prevented by a total breakdown of communications or by the destruction of the physical object of its obligation (the disappearance by natural disaster of the very thing required to be returned or preserved). (iv) The Rainbow Warrior arbitration (New Zealand v. France, 1990, 82 ILR 499) is the modern case in which a plea of this type was examined and rejected: France attempted to justify the premature removal of the two officers from the island of Hao; the tribunal held that the circumstances invoked — including the medical condition of one officer and the death of the other's father — did not satisfy the stringent requirements of a circumstance precluding wrongfulness, and France was held in breach of its obligations under the 1986 agreement. (v) The Russian Indemnity case (Russia v. Turkey, 1912, 11 UNRIAA 421) is the classic rejection of "financial impossibility": Turkey's plea that it was unable to pay the indemnity on time was rejected, because pecuniary difficulty is not material impossibility.

(7) Article 24 — Distress

Content. Justification is available where the author of the act "has no other reasonable way, in a situation of distress, of saving the author's life or the lives of other persons entrusted to the author's care". Like force majeure, it fails where the distress is self-induced or where the act would create "a comparable or greater peril". The difference between Articles 23 and 24 is the difference between impossibility (the State cannot perform) and necessity of a human kind (the State, or its agents, must choose life over law). Distress protects the person; force majeure protects performance.

Illustrations. (i) The master of a vessel who, to save his crew, enters a foreign port without authorisation or jettisons cargo in a prohibited area; (ii) a pilot who deviates from an authorised route to save a passenger's life; (iii) the Torrey Canyon incident (1967): the Liberian-flagged tanker ran aground off the Cornish coast and threatened catastrophic pollution of the English coast; the British Government, after rescuing the crew, bombed and burnt the ship. The action — destructive of foreign private property on the high seas — could be justified only by necessity/distress; the United Kingdom eventually made ex gratia payments, which is a perfect illustration of Article 27(b) (wrongfulness precluded, compensation for material loss still owed). (iv) The ILC's own example: a State aircraft in distress entering foreign airspace, or the entry of a warship into a foreign port for repair of damage or to save life. (v) Contemporary legal frameworks: the duty to render assistance at sea (the SAR Convention, SOLAS Chapter V, the 1958 High Seas Convention), and the treatment of "distress" in Article 98 of UNCLOS — the legal background which makes rescue at sea a duty and, correlatively, justifies the entry of rescue vessels into foreign ports. (vi) The converse case — the drug-smuggling vessel that claims distress — the requirement of "no other reasonable way" defeats it.

(8) Article 25 — Necessity

Content. The most restrictive of all the defences, and deliberately so. The conditions are cumulative: (a) the act must be "the only way for the State to safeguard an essential interest against a grave and imminent peril"; (b) it must not seriously impair an essential interest of the State or States to whom the obligation is owed, or of the international community as a whole; (c) the obligation must not exclude recourse to necessity; and (d) the State must not have contributed to the situation. Article 26 adds the peremptory-norm exclusions; Article 27(a) makes clear that necessity is temporary.

The leading case — Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros (Hungary/Slovakia), ICJ 1997, ¶¶49–58. Hungary suspended and abandoned the works on the Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros barrage system, pleading an ecological state of necessity. The Court, adopting the ILC's formulation (then Article 33 of the 1996 draft), held that a state of necessity may be invoked only in exceptional circumstances, that the conditions are strictly defined and cumulative, and that the State concerned is not the sole judge of whether they are met. On the facts: Hungary's suspension of the works was in fact wrongful, because at the relevant time the peril was neither sufficiently "grave" nor "imminent" — the ecological risks were uncertain and long-term; and even where a state of necessity does exist, it may not be invoked to terminate a treaty, since the obligation revives when the necessity ceases — "the Court ... is of the view that a state of necessity does not terminate or suspend a treaty". The judgment is the single most important authority in this branch; along with it, the Court's statement that environmental protection had become "an essential interest" of States shows the doctrine's dynamism.

Other applications.

  • Russian Indemnity (1912) — necessity and force majeure both rejected where the plea rested on financial strain.
  • Legal Consequences of the Construction of a Wall, Advisory Opinion, ICJ 2004, ¶140 — necessity could not justify the construction of the wall: the Court required the peril to be "objectively established and ... of a grave and imminent character", and found the plea unsupported and, in any event, that the wall was not the "only way" to safeguard Israel's interests. The Opinion also shows that necessity cannot override erga omnes obligations and humanitarian law.
  • The Argentinian financial crisis cases (2002–2008) — an important modern laboratory. Investment tribunals were divided on whether Argentina's emergency legislation was justified by necessity under the customary defence (and under the "essential security" clauses of the BITs): CMS v. Argentina (2005) and Sempra v. Argentina (2007) held that the conditions were not met, while LG&E v. Argentina (2006) and Continental Casualty v. Argentina (2008) held that they were. The jurisprudence shows that "essential interest" may include the internal economic stability of a State, but also that international courts and tribunals remain extremely reluctant to second-guess economic policy. For an answer, the safe propositions are: necessity is "exceptional"; it cannot be self-judged; and it is temporary.
  • Gabčíkovo's interim order and the subsequent 1997 judgment together also demonstrate the interface with the Court's provisional measures, and with the rule (Article 27(a)) that the obligation revives.
  • Illustrative real cases of necessity invoked but rejected or accepted outside the courts: the 2010 Eyjafjallajökull volcanic ash crisis (airspace closures as measures justified by safety obligations); the 2011 Fukushima emergency (Japan's release of contaminated water); and the refusal of a State to return cultural property saved from destruction. (The last is drawn from the ILC's own illustration: a State may take in, and refuse to return, artworks to preserve them from destruction — a necessity which ceases when the danger ends.)

(9) Article 26 — Peremptory norms, and Article 41 consequences

Article 26 is a single sentence which does a great deal of work: nothing in Chapter V precludes the wrongfulness of a breach of an obligation arising under a peremptory norm of general international law. No consent, no necessity, no countermeasure, no distress, no force majeure can justify aggression, genocide, slavery, torture, racial apartheid, or a denial of self-determination. The Articles go further in Part Two, Chapter III (Articles 40 and 41): a serious breach of such an obligation — one involving "a gross or systematic failure by the responsible State to fulfil the obligation" (Article 40) — carries aggravated consequences: all States are to cooperate to bring the breach to an end, no State shall recognise as lawful a situation created by the breach or render aid or assistance in maintaining it, and other consequences of the breach may be sought (Article 41). The ILC deliberately refrained from using the term "international crime of State" (which had appeared in the 1996 draft, Article 19) and preferred the objective language of "peremptory norms" and "serious breaches": the change of drafting is itself an examinable point of the codification history.

Illustrations. Iraq's invasion and annexation of Kuwait (1990) — aggression: the Security Council and the international community refused to recognise the annexation, and Article 41(2) describes exactly that reaction. The arms embargoes and non-recognition policies in respect of apartheid South Africa and Southern Rhodesia. The Srebrenica and Rwanda massacres, which the International Criminal Tribunal for Rwanda and the ICJ characterised as genocide. The duty of all States not to recognise the situation created by a serious breach is today one of the live issues in the debate over the annexation of territory in Ukraine and the legal obligations flowing from it.

(10) Article 27 — The consequences of a successful plea

Two, and only two, consequences are preserved: the obligation revives when the circumstance ceases; and the question of compensation for material loss caused by the act remains open. Gabčíkovo cites Article 27(b) in this sense: "the Court ... points out that, under Article 33(b) of the ILC's Articles (now Article 27(b)), the invocation of a circumstance precluding wrongfulness is without prejudice to the question of compensation for any material loss caused by the act in question" — so that a State which in distress damages the property of another may owe compensation even though no wrong was committed. This is the "risk" principle in miniature, and it links back to §4.2.3.

Fig. 4.8 — The two gates and the five doors of Articles 20–27.

Examination box — the two classic questions on this section

"Discuss the circumstances precluding wrongfulness under the ILC Articles, 2001." Open with the place of Chapter V in the structure (attribution → breach → defences) and with the six propositions listed at B above. Then take Articles 20–25 in order with text, elements and one case each: consent (DRC v. Uganda 2005); self-defence (Nicaragua; Oil Platforms; Wall AO); countermeasures (Naulilaa; Air Services Agreement; Gabčíkovo) with Articles 49–54 summarised; force majeure (Russian Indemnity; Rainbow Warrior); distress (Torrey Canyon); necessity (Gabčíkovo ¶¶49–58). Close with Articles 26 and 27 and the point that none of them excuses a breach of a peremptory norm.

"What are countermeasures? State the conditions of their lawfulness." Define (prima facie unlawful, taken against a responsible State to induce compliance), distinguish from retorsion, sanctions and treaty suspension, and then set out the conditions article by article (49–54), each with a case and an example. Conclude with the doctrinal criticism: countermeasures preserve self-help in a system that aspires to compulsory adjudication, which is why Article 52(3) suspends them once the dispute is before a tribunal and why Article 50(1) removes human rights and humanitarian obligations from their reach.

"Can a State ever plead necessity to justify a breach of its international obligations?" Answer: only exceptionally, and never successfully in the reported cases before the ICJ. Give the four cumulative conditions of Article 25, the two exclusions in Article 25(2), Article 26, and Gabčíkovo ¶¶49–58 with the Court's caution, plus the Wall and Argentinian cases. Finish with Article 27(a): necessity suspends, it does not terminate.

↑ top

§ 4.4 Forms of Reparation

Reparation is the pivot on which the whole law of State responsibility turns: it is the answer to the question "what follows from a finding that an internationally wrongful act has been committed?" The syllabus takes the four classical forms in the order restitution — indemnity — satisfaction — guarantee against repetition, and the ILC Articles (Chapter II of Part Two, Articles 31 and 34 to 39) follow a slightly different order, placing restitution, compensation and satisfaction in Article 34 and treating "assurances and guarantees of non-repetition" as part of cessation (Article 30(b)). Both the order and the difference are examined below.

A note on the syllabus page. Against 4.4.1 (Restitution) and 4.4.2 (Indemnity) the printed syllabus carries a handwritten annotation which reads as a reference to a "…Factory" case. In the context of these two heads of reparation the case meant can only be the Case Concerning the Factory at Chorzów (PCIJ, 1927 and 1928), which is the source of the general principle applied in both: "reparation must, as far as possible, wipe out all the consequences of the illegal act and re-establish the situation which would, in all probability, have existed if that act had not been committed", and which itself held that restitution in kind was the primary remedy and compensation ("the value of the undertaking", including loss of profits) the substitute where restitution was impossible. It is treated fully in A and B below.

A. The governing principle — full reparation: Chorzów and Article 31

The foundation of the modern law is the Permanent Court's statement in the Case Concerning the Factory at Chorzów (Germany v. Poland), Merits, 1928, P.C.I.J. Series A, No. 17 — the case material and the prescribed text both make it the starting point:

"it is a general conception of law that every violation of an engagement involves an obligation to make reparation" (p. 21)

"The essential principle contained in the actual notion of an illegal act — a principle which seems to be established by international practice and in particular by the decisions of arbitral tribunals — is that reparation must, as far as possible, wipe out all the consequences of the illegal act and re-establish the situation which would, in all probability, have existed if that act had not been committed. Restitution in kind, or, if this is not possible, payment of a sum corresponding to the value which a restitution in kind would bear; the award, if need be, of damages for loss sustained which would not be covered by restitution in kind or payment in place of it — such are the principles which should serve to determine the amount of compensation due for an act contrary to international law." (p. 47)

Article 31 then states the two governing propositions: "1. The responsible State is under an obligation to make full reparation for the injury caused by the internationally wrongful act. 2. Injury includes any damage, whether material or moral, caused by the internationally wrongful act of a State." And Article 34 names the forms: "Full reparation for the injury caused by the internationally wrongful act shall take the form of restitution, compensation and satisfaction, either singly or in combination."

Four structural points are worth remembering before the forms are taken one by one:

  1. Cessation is not reparation. Article 30 obliges the responsible State "to cease that act, if it is continuing" and "to offer appropriate assurances and guarantees of non-repetition, if circumstances so require". Ceasing an unlawful act is not a remedy for the past; it is compliance with the obligation breached. The distinction matters when the breach has ended: the duty to cease is then spent, but the duty to repair remains.
  2. Reparation is owed for injury caused — hence the central importance of causation. Article 31 requires a causal link between the wrongful act and the injury; Article 39 requires the contribution of the injured party to be taken into account; and Article 36 confines compensation to damage that is "financially assessable".
  3. Reparation takes the form or forms appropriate to the injury: "either singly or in combination". Where restitution is impossible and compensation covers only the material loss, satisfaction supplies the remainder. Where the injury is entirely material, compensation is the usual and sufficient remedy.
  4. Internal law is irrelevant (Article 32): a responsible State "may not rely on the provisions of its internal law as justification for failure to comply with its obligations under this Part" — the point made in LaGrand against the federal/State division of authority, and in Avena/Medellín about the enforceability of ICJ judgments in domestic law.

B. 4.4.1 Restitution (Article 35)

Text. "A State responsible for an internationally wrongful act is under an obligation to make restitution, that is, to re-establish the situation which existed before the wrongful act was committed, provided and to the extent that restitution: (a) is not materially impossible; (b) does not involve a burden out of all proportion to the benefit deriving from restitution instead of compensation."

Two kinds of restitution. (i) Restitution in kind — the return of what was taken, the release of what was seized: the return of confiscated property, archives, documents, cultural objects, vessels, aircraft; the release of detained persons; the withdrawal of troops from occupied territory; the restoration of the physical condition of land or rivers. (ii) Juridical restitution — the revocation, annulment or modification of the legal instrument which constitutes the breach: the repeal of a discriminatory law, the withdrawal of a decree of confiscation or nationalisation, the annulment of a judgment obtained in violation of international law, the restoration of a person's nationality, or the cancellation of an unlawful expulsion order.

The two limits.

  • Material impossibility. The ILC's commentary gives as examples the destruction of the object (a unique chattel destroyed or a lost archaeological discovery), the situation where the thing has passed irreversibly into the hands of a third party acting in good faith, and the case where restitution has become permanently impossible because of a change in the circumstances (as the prescribed text puts it, restitution is not to be made "firstly, where it is materially impossible"). Impossibility, not difficulty, is required.
  • Disproportionate burden. Restitution may be declined where the burden to the responsible State would be "out of all proportion to the benefit deriving from restitution instead of compensation". The comparison is between restitution and compensation: the question is whether the benefit of restoring the original situation (as against paying the value) is worth the burden. A country which could restore a piece of land only by demolishing a hydro-electric station and displacing thousands of people will normally be required to compensate rather than to restore.

Cases and examples.

  • Chorzów Factory (1928). Poland had expropriated the nitrate factory; the Court held that restitution in kind was the primary remedy, but that if restitution could not be made, the payment of the value "which a restitution in kind would bear" together with damages for loss not covered would be due. The case is the classical authority that restitution has priority in principle but is only one of the possible forms.
  • Temple of Preah Vihear (Cambodia v. Thailand), ICJ 1962. The Court held that the temple area lay in Cambodia and that Thailand was under an obligation to withdraw its forces; it also declared that Thailand must return the sculptures taken from the temple. A clear instance of restitution in kind ordered by the Court.
  • Rainbow Warrior (New Zealand v. France), 1990, 82 ILR 499. The tribunal held that New Zealand's request for the return of the two officers to the island of Hao could not be granted — restitution was no longer possible in the sense demanded — and that the appropriate remedy was satisfaction and a monetary payment. It is the standard illustration of the transition from restitution to satisfaction where the situation cannot be re-established.
  • Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros (1997). The Court held that Slovakia was not entitled to maintain its unilateral diversion of the Danube, and that the parties were obliged to negotiate in good faith to restore the joint régime of the 1977 Treaty — restitution here reaching beyond the return of a thing to the re-establishment of a legal and physical régime ("restitution" in the widest sense).
  • Modern treaty practice. The International Convention for the Protection of Cultural Property and the 1970 UNESCO Convention contemplate the return of cultural property; the 1998 Statute of the International Criminal Court gives victims the right to reparation, including restitution, in the Court's reparations machinery (Article 75); and the humanitarian law of occupation requires the restoration of the status quo ante at the end of occupation.

C. 4.4.2 Indemnity (Compensation) — Article 36

Text. "1. The State responsible for an internationally wrongful act is under an obligation to compensate for the damage caused thereby, insofar as such damage is not made good by restitution. 2. The compensation shall cover any financially assessable damage including loss of profits insofar as it is established."

Points of content.

  • The standard is full reparation for financially assessable damage. Compensation includes both damnum emergens (the actual loss, including the costs of repair, replacement and of the measures taken to mitigate the damage) and lucrum cessans (loss of profits, "insofar as it is established" — i.e., probable and not speculative). Verma adds the practical caution that "quantification of damage is only approximate" in some cases, while in others — "in cases involving loss of the profits" — the loss may be compensable if established.
  • Whose damage? Compensation is owed for the injury to the claimant State itself (damage to its property, warships, diplomatic mission, members of its armed forces — as in Corfu Channel and DRC v. Uganda), and, through the mechanism of diplomatic protection, for injury to its nationals (the older practice of "claims commissions" and the whole body of law in the 2006 ILC Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection). The prescribed text notes the "part of the State to transmit the damages received for any of the individuals": the classical rule is that a lump-sum settlement between States does not itself create individual rights, though modern practice increasingly channels awards to individuals.
  • Moral damage. Non-material damage (injury to a State's dignity, honour, symbols, or to individuals' pain and suffering) is "financially assessable" in principle and may be the subject of compensation; but practice is cautious, and where the injury is essentially moral the usual remedy is satisfaction (Article 37) — which is why the ILC placed satisfaction in the same article-cluster. The Corfu Channel award shows the Court separating the two: the material damage to the ships and the loss of life was compensated (in money), while the violation of sovereignty was remedied by a declaration of the Court ("a declaration is in itself appropriate satisfaction").
  • No punitive or exemplary damages. This is settled. In the Lusitania case (United States v. Germany, 1923, 7 UNRIAA 32) the umpire held that the United States was not entitled to claim punitive damages: "there is no authority for the proposition that ... a State should be allowed to ... punitive damages". International tribunals award reparation, not punishment; punishment belongs to the criminal law of individual responsibility. Verma states the same conclusion from the practice: "Damages have been awarded for non-material loss, but international tribunals have been reluctant to grant exemplary or punitive damages."
  • Interest. Article 38 provides that "[i]nterest on any principal sum due under this chapter shall be payable when necessary in order to ensure full reparation", running "from the date when the principal sum should have been paid until the date the obligation to pay is fulfilled". In recent practice the ICJ has fixed 6% per annum for post-judgment interest (DRC v. Uganda, 2022) and, in the Costa Rica v. Nicaragua compensation judgment (2018), 4% pre-judgment interest on costs and 6% post-judgment. The ILC's commentary discusses the choice between the "prime rate" and simple rates, and the controversial question of compound interest.
  • Causation and mitigation. Only damage caused by the wrongful act is compensable, and Article 39 requires account to be taken of "the contribution to the injury by wilful or negligent action or omission of the injured State or any person or entity in relation to whom reparation is sought" — the international counterpart of contributory negligence. In Gabčíkovo, the Court's overall disposition reflected the fact that both parties had contributed to the situation.

Leading awards in which the figures are examinable.

I'm Alone (Canada v. UN Compensation Commission (1991–2005) and the Eritrea–Ethiopia Claims Commission (2001–2009)Mass claims: US$52 billion awarded by the UNCC; compensation for unlawful expulsions, loss of life and propertyThe two great modern laboratories of the law of compensation: they show how reparation is administered where the wrongful act is on a vast scale, and how "full reparation" is translated into lump-sum methodology.

D. 4.4.3 Satisfaction (Article 37)

Text. "1. The State responsible ... is under an obligation to give satisfaction for the injury caused by that act insofar as it cannot be made good by restitution or compensation. 2. Satisfaction may consist in an acknowledgement of the breach, an expression of regret, a formal apology or another appropriate modality. 3. Satisfaction shall not be out of proportion to the injury and may not take a form humiliating to the responsible State."

Nature and function. Satisfaction is the remedy for the non-material injury — the affront to the dignity, honour or prestige of the injured State, and (in modern practice) for the moral injury to individuals. It is residuary: it arises only "insofar as" the injury cannot be made good by restitution or compensation. Its forms are open-ended, and modern practice has added the declaration of the court (a judicial finding of the breach), the punishment of the offending officials, the payment of a symbolic sum, the erection of a memorial, the publication of the judgment and the establishment of a fund.

Limits. Two, and both are important in practice: satisfaction must be proportionate to the injury; and it "may not take a form humiliating to the responsible State". The old practice of demanding a salute to the flag of the injured State — the practice of the age of gunboat diplomacy — was condemned by the arbitral tribunals precisely on this second ground, and the modern law has moved away from humiliating forms in favour of the declaration and the apology.

Cases.

  • Corfu Channel (1949) — the leading case. The United Kingdom's warships had, on 22 October 1946, passed through the North Corfu Channel, and on 12–13 November 1946 the Royal Navy cleared the channel ("Operation Retail") without Albania's consent — a violation of Albanian sovereignty. Albania and the United Kingdom each claimed compensation for the flagrant violation of their respective sovereignty. The Court held that the United Kingdom's action violated Albanian sovereignty, and then, on the question of reparation: "the Court considers that this declaration is in itself appropriate satisfaction", and declined to award compensation for that violation. The judgment is the standard authority for the proposition that a judicial declaration of the breach may itself constitute adequate satisfaction.
  • I'm Alone (1935). The Commission recommended as satisfaction the "formal acknowledgment" of the United States that the sinking was unlawful, together with a material indemnity; it declined to award damages for "the insult" as a separate head. Classic modern use of acknowledgment.
  • Rainbow Warrior (New Zealand v. France), 1990. Satisfaction was the central issue: the tribunal held that a "formal apology" by the Prime Minister of France, plus the payment of a fund of US$7 million to New Zealand, constituted the appropriate reparation for the breach — and it is one of the few modern cases in which the amount of the payment was expressly characterised as satisfaction rather than as compensation for material loss. The tribunal also emphasised the requirement of proportionality between satisfaction and the injury.
  • Bosnian Genocide (Bosnia and Herzegovina v. Serbia and Montenegro), ICJ 2007. The Court found Serbia responsible for failing to prevent the Srebrenica genocide and for failing to punish those responsible and to transfer accused persons to the ICTY. Bosnia's claim for compensation was not pressed. The Court held that "the Court's declaration ... is sufficient satisfaction" — a decision which attracted criticism precisely because of the gravity of the breach, and which is now often contrasted with the "aggravated consequences" of Articles 40–41.
  • Modern human rights practice. The Inter-American Court of Human Rights has developed an elaborate catalogue of satisfaction measures: publication of the judgment, acknowledgment of responsibility through public acts, naming of streets and schools after victims, and the "guarantees of non-repetition" (below). The UN Basic Principles and Guidelines on the Right to a Remedy and Reparation for Victims of Gross Violations of International Human Rights Law and Serious Violations of International Humanitarian Law (2005) list the forms of reparation — restitution, compensation, rehabilitation, satisfaction and guarantees of non-repetition — and are a standard reference for the modern expansion of satisfaction beyond the interstate framework.

E. 4.4.4 Guarantee against Repetition

Where it lives in the ILC Articles. The syllabus lists "guarantee against repetition" as a form of reparation (4.4.4). The ILC chose a different architecture: Article 30(b) requires the responsible State "to offer appropriate assurances and guarantees of non-repetition, if circumstances so require", as part of the cluster of obligations relating to cessation, and Article 34 deliberately omits it from the forms of reparation. The reason is conceptual: an assurance of non-repetition does not repair the injury — it reinforces the primary obligation and prevents future injury. The syllabus's usage is older (and is followed by many textbooks), so the two positions should be stated in any answer: the ILC classifies assurances of non-repetition with cessation; the syllabus and the older literature treat them as a species of reparation; and the practical result is the same.

Content. An assurance is a formal statement of the offender's intention not to repeat the wrongful act; a guarantee is something stronger — a structural or institutional change which makes repetition less likely: the amendment of a law or practice, the training of officials, the establishment of a review mechanism, the punishment of those responsible, or the payment of a bond. The condition is necessity — "if circumstances so require" — and the ILC commentary ties the requirement to the danger of repetition, the gravity of the breach and the need for satisfaction.

Cases.

  • LaGrand (2001) — the modern leading case (Case 27 above). Germany sought a general assurance of non-repetition. The Court held that the United States' apology was not sufficient in a case of "prolonged detention or … conviction and sentence to severe penalties", and treated the vast programme of consular-notification training, brochures and reference cards as "meeting Germany's request for a general assurance of non-repetition" (¶¶123–124), while adding that "if the United States … should fail in its obligation … an apology would not suffice", and that in such a case the United States must "allow the review and reconsideration of the conviction and sentence" (¶125). Both the content of the assurance and its limits are thus illustrated: a generic programme may suffice; a specific remedy is required if it fails.
  • Armed Activities (DRC v. Uganda) (2005 and 2022), Case 29 below. In the merits judgment of 2005 the Court found Uganda under obligations of cessation and reparation, and its declaratory findings operated as satisfaction; in the reparations judgment of 2022 the Court found that a declaration was "in itself appropriate satisfaction", and addressed the question of non-repetition through the mechanism of a fund and the ordered payment. The case shows the two-stage remedial process in full.
  • Nicaragua (1986). The Court declared that the United States was "under a duty immediately to cease and to refrain" from the unlawful acts — cessation plus a permanent obligation of abstention, which in substance is the strongest form of guarantee.
  • Tehran Hostages (1980). Cessation and release were ordered, and the "form and amount" of reparation was reserved — the classic case in which the guarantee against repetition consists in the removal of the situation itself.
  • Human rights practice. Guarantees of non-repetition are the most dynamic growth area of reparation law: the Inter-American Court has ordered the reform of domestic laws, the creation of human rights training programmes, and the establishment of investigative protocols; the 2005 UN Basic Principles and Guidelines list "guarantees of non-repetition" as a distinct head of reparation; and the Council of Europe/Council of Ministers supervision of ECtHR judgments operates in the same direction.
Table 4.12 · The four forms compared

F. Reparation in Indian practice — three real examples

  • The Enrica Lexie arbitration (Italy v. India, 2020) — compensation paid. Italian marines on board the tanker Enrica Lexie shot and killed two Indian fishermen off the Kerala coast in February 2012. An UNCLOS Annex VII tribunal (PCA, Award of 21 May 2020) held that Italy had jurisdiction over the marines, but that Italy had violated India's right to navigate; it ordered India and Italy to co-operate and directed Italy to pay compensation. Italy paid ₹10 crore (approximately US$1.5 million) to the families of the victims in 2021. The episode is a perfect modern illustration of Article 36 compensation for the killing of nationals, and of the interaction between responsibility and the law of the sea.
  • The Jadhav case (India v. Pakistan), ICJ 2019. India invoked the VCCR (through the Optional Protocol) after Pakistan sentenced its national Kulbhushan Jadhav to death without consular notification — the LaGrand/Avena line applied. The Court ordered Pakistan to provide consular access and, critically, "an effective review and reconsideration of the conviction and sentence", suspending the effect of the death sentence. The remedy was of the LaGrand type: satisfaction plus review, not a payment. (The judgment is printed in the course material alongside the Tehran Hostages case.)
  • Bhopal (1984) — the municipal counterpart. The Union Carbide disaster led to the Gas Leak Disaster (Processing of Claims) Act, 1985, the settlement of 1989 (US$470 million), and the domestic development of absolute liability in M.C. Mehta v. Union of India (1987). It illustrates how the same factual catastrophe is dealt with under the domestic tort/statutory regime (strict/absolute liability, compensation, insurance) while the international law of State responsibility remains, in the background, the law of the State-to-State claim.

Fig. 4.9 — The cascade of reparation: restitution first, compensation for what cannot be restored, satisfaction for the residue, and non-repetition across the whole.

Examination box — model skeleton on the forms of reparation

Open with Article 31 and Chorzów Factory ("wipe out all the consequences of the illegal act" / "re-establish the situation which would, in all probability, have existed"), and note Article 34's three forms "either singly or in combination". Then, for each form: definition — text — conditions and limits — authority. Restitution: Article 35, material impossibility, disproportionate burden, Chorzów, Temple of Preah Vihear, Gabcíkovo. Compensation: Article 36 ("financially assessable damage including loss of profits insofar as established"), damnum emergens and lucrum cessans, interest under Article 38, the exclusion of punitive damages (Lusitania), the awards in Corfu Channel, I'm Alone, DRC v. Uganda and Costa Rica v. Nicaragua, and contribution under Article 39. Satisfaction: Article 37, its residuary character, the declaration in Corfu Channel, the apology in Rainbow Warrior, the declaration in Genocide (2007), the limits of proportionality and non-humiliation. Guarantee of non-repetition: Article 30(b), "if circumstances so require", LaGrand (the US programme) and human-rights practice — adding that the ILC treats it as an aspect of cessation while the older literature (and the syllabus) treats it as a form of reparation. Close with the practical points which examiners reward: cessation is not reparation; reparation must be proportional and must respect the injured State's contribution (Art. 39); reparation may be agreed by lump sum; and the two-stage procedure (declaration on liability, followed by an assessment of quantum) is now standard — as in Corfu Channel (1949), Tehran Hostages (1980), DRC v. Uganda (2005 and 2022) and Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (2015 and 2018).

Case 28 · Certain Activities Carried Out by Nicaragua in the Border Area (Costa Rica v. Nicaragua)

Certain Activities Carried Out by Nicaragua in the Border Area (Costa Rica v. Nicaragua)

ICJ Reports 2015, p. 665 (Merits, Judgment of 16 December 2015) · Question of Compensation: ICJ Reports 2018 (I), p. 15, Judgment of 2 February 2018 · Provisional measures: Orders of 8 March 2011 and 22 November 2013 · Joined with Construction of a Road in Costa Rica along the San Juan River (Nicaragua v. Costa Rica) · Bench 2018: President Abraham; Vice-President Yusuf; Judges Owada, Tomka, Bennouna, Cançado Trindade, Greenwood, Xue, Gaja, Sebutinde, Bhandari, Robinson, Gevorgian; Judges ad hoc Guillaume and Dugard

Provenance. This case is prescribed in the Topic-4 syllabus as the leading modern authority on environmental damage as a compensable head under §4.4. The course material cites it within the DRC v. Uganda block (for the standard of the causal nexus) rather than printing the judgment separately; the account below is reconstructed from the 2015 and 2018 judgments, the Court's summaries and press releases, and scholarly commentary, and is labelled accordingly.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Because it answers a question on which the classical law was silent: is damage to the environment, in itself, compensable under the law of State responsibility — and how is it to be valued? The Court held that it is, and that compensation may cover both the impairment or loss of the ability of the environment to provide "goods and services" and the cost of restoring the damaged environment. In doing so it gave practical content to Article 36 of the ILC Articles (compensation covering "any financially assessable damage") and to the principle of full reparation of Chorzów. The case is also the first in which the ICJ adjudicated a claim for compensation for environmental harm, the first in which the Court appointed experts to assist in the valuation of damage, and the first in which the Court fixed a total sum including pre-judgment interest on only part of the claim — three reasons why examiners like it. For India, it is doubly relevant: the Court's refusal to prescribe a single valuation method, and its acceptance of approximation and of the "ecosystem-services approach", supply the vocabulary in which transboundary environmental harm claims (the Bhopal aftermath; the Teesta and Brahmaputra disputes; the pollution of the Ganga; and the climate-litigation arguments before Indian courts) are today argued.

2. Facts

(a) The disputed territory. The dispute concerns the northern part of Isla Portillos — a small island (also known as Harbour Head) in the delta of the San Juan River, which forms the boundary between the two States. The area is a wetland protected under the Ramsar Convention on Wetlands of International Importance, rich in timber, biodiversity and the environmental services of a humid tropical ecosystem. The 1888 Cleveland Award and the 1916 judgment of the Central American Court of Justice had settled the sovereignty of the island's northern part in Costa Rica, but the boundary remained contested on the ground.

(b) The 2010 and 2013 "caños". In October 2010 Nicaragua, while dredging the San Juan River, excavated a channel (a "caño") across Costa Rican territory in the disputed area, cutting off the flow of the river and beginning a military presence on the island. The Court's provisional measures order of 8 March 2011 required both Parties to refrain from sending personnel to the area. In 2013 Nicaragua again established a military presence and excavated two further caños and a trench in the wetland, removing soil by dredging; about 300 trees were felled and 6.19 hectares of vegetation cleared. Costa Rica complained that the dredging would alter the ecology of the wetland and that Nicaragua had failed to produce any environmental impact assessment.

(c) The related claim. Nicaragua counterclaimed in respect of a road (Route 1856) that Costa Rica had built along the southern bank of the San Juan River, alleging that the construction caused sediment to be deposited in the river. The Court, in the 2015 judgment, dismissed Nicaragua's claims for significant transboundary harm (Nicaragua had failed to prove it) but found that Costa Rica had breached the obligation to conduct an environmental impact assessment before constructing the road, and that this violation was appropriately remedied by a declaration of wrongfulness — a real-world application of "satisfaction" for a procedural environmental obligation.

(d) The two-stage litigation. The Court's merits judgment of 16 December 2015 found, among other things, that Costa Rica had sovereignty over the disputed territory and that Nicaragua had violated that sovereignty by excavating the caños and by establishing a military presence on Costa Rican territory; and that Nicaragua was under an obligation to compensate Costa Rica for the material damage caused. Since the parties failed to agree on the amount within twelve months, the matter came back to the Court in the compensation phase, which produced the judgment of 2 February 2018.

3. The issues

  1. Is damage to the environment, and the impairment or loss of the ability of the environment to provide goods and services, compensable under international law?
  2. What method of valuation should the Court use — the "ecosystem-services approach" pressed by Costa Rica, or the "replacement-cost approach" pressed by Nicaragua?
  3. What evidence must a claimant State produce to prove environmental damage, and what is the effect of scientific uncertainty and the absence of market values?
  4. Are restoration costs recoverable, and what is their relationship to compensation for the loss of services?
  5. Which of Costa Rica's costs and expenses (monitoring, overflights, inspections, the building of a dyke, satellite imagery) have a "sufficiently direct and certain causal nexus" with the wrongful acts?
  6. Is pre-judgment and post-judgment interest payable, and on which parts of the claim?
  7. (On Nicaragua's counterclaim) What remedy is appropriate for a breach of the obligation to conduct an environmental impact assessment?

4. The arguments of both sides (compensation phase)

For Costa Rica

(i) Environmental damage is compensable, and the Court should adopt the "ecosystem-services approach": identifying the goods and services the wetland provided (timber, raw materials, gas regulation and air quality, natural-hazard mitigation, soil formation and erosion control, biodiversity), valuing their loss over a recovery period of about 50 years, with a discount for gradual natural recovery.

(ii) The claim of US$54,925.69 for replacement soil in the two caños should be allowed; compensation for the loss of carbon-sequestration services should be included (Costa Rica sought to value it as a one-time loss).

(iii) Costa Rica's monitoring, inspection, dyke-construction and satellite-imagery costs were incurred to prevent and assess irreparable prejudice and were therefore compensable, as were the salaries of the officials involved.

(iv) Pre-judgment interest should be awarded from the date of the merits judgment (16 December 2015), and post-judgment interest in the event of delay.

For Nicaragua

(i) The proper approach is the "replacement-cost approach": Costa Rica is entitled only to the cost of replacing the lost environmental services, and (Nicaragua argued) the recovery of the wetland is much faster — in the order of 20 to 30 years — than Costa Rica claimed.

(ii) Many of the claimed categories are speculative; Costa Rica cannot prove the loss of natural-hazard mitigation or of erosion control, and the caños have naturally refilled and been recolonised by vegetation.

(iii) The costs claimed for salaries of officials and for monitoring activities are ordinary governmental expenses, not damage caused by Nicaragua.

(iv) No pre-judgment interest should be awarded; and the total sum must be proportionate and supported by evidence.

5. The decision

(a) Environmental damage is compensable

The Court's central holding, and the sentence to quote in an answer:

"[I]t is consistent with the principles of international law governing the consequences of internationally wrongful acts, including the principle of full reparation, to hold that compensation is due for damage caused to the environment, in particular the consequent impairment or loss of the ability of the environment to provide goods and services, and the cost of restoration of the damaged environment." (Judgment of 2 February 2018)

Two consequences flow from this: first, damage to the environment need not be "material damage to the State" in any narrow proprietary sense — the environment is itself an interest protected by international law, so that a State can recover for harm to a protected wetland even though it cannot sell the trees; and second, environmental damage is financially assessable within the meaning of Article 36 of the ILC Articles, and thus compensable rather than merely satisfiable.

The Court added a practical and generous rule of evidence:

"[T]he absence of adequate evidence as to the extent of material damage will not, in all situations, preclude an award of compensation for that damage."

That rule — combined with the Court's insistence that "international law does not prescribe any specific method of valuation" — is what makes the case workable: the injured State is not required to prove a market value for ecosystem services, and the Court may adopt an overall assessment if it has a reasonable basis for it. The Court expressly retained elements of Nicaragua's "corrected analysis" while adjusting it for its shortcomings, and adopted a valuation from the perspective of the ecosystem as a whole, taking account of the capacity of the damaged area for natural regeneration.

(b) The heads of damage allowed and rejected
  • Allowed: impairment or loss of environmental goods and services — US$120,000. The Court found that the excavation of the caños had affected four categories of environmental goods and services (trees, other raw materials, gas regulation and air-quality services, and biodiversity in terms of habitat and nursery services) and made an overall valuation for the period prior to recovery. It rejected the claim that the loss of carbon-sequestration services could be valued as a one-time loss.
  • Rejected: replacement soil — US$54,925.69. The evidence did not establish the loss; the caños had partly refilled naturally.
  • Rejected: natural-hazard mitigation and erosion control. Not proved to have been impaired — an important reminder that compensation requires proof of the injury, not merely of the interference.
  • Allowed: restoration costs of the wetland — US$2,708.39. The claim was upheld because restoration is a distinct head: a State may recover the cost of measures to restore an environment which will not recover by itself.
  • Allowed: costs and expenses — US$236,032.16. The Court sifted the claims, allowing (i) the two-day Ramsar inspection of April 2011 in so far as compensable; (ii) the costs of the dyke built to prevent irreparable prejudice and of the helicopter used to transport materials (with an adjustment for mistaken items); and (iii) the monitoring overflights after the dyke's completion. It rejected (iv) the salaries of monitoring personnel (ordinary expenses, not to be compensated unless "temporary and extraordinary"); (v) the press and cargo flights; and (vi) two satellite images, one of which fell outside the disputed area. The requirement applied was the causal nexus: "sufficiently direct and certain" between the wrongful act and the expense.
(c) Interest

The Court recalled that "interest is not an autonomous form of reparation, nor is it a necessary part of compensation in every case" (Article 38 of the ILC Articles and its commentary). It awarded no pre-judgment interest on the environmental damage, because the overall valuation had already accounted for the loss in the period before recovery; but it awarded pre-judgment interest at 4% per annum from 16 December 2015 (the merits judgment) to 2 February 2018 on the costs and expenses (US$20,150.04), and post-judgment interest at 6% per annum in the event of delay. Total: US$378,890.59, payable by 2 April 2018. Nicaragua paid the whole amount on 8 March 2018 — a rare instance in the modern practice of a defendant complying promptly with an ICJ award.

(d) The counterclaim: satisfaction for a procedural environmental breach

On Nicaragua's claim concerning the road, the Court held that Nicaragua had not proved significant transboundary harm, but that Costa Rica had violated the procedural obligation to conduct an environmental impact assessment. For that breach the Court held that a declaration of wrongfulness was the appropriate measure of satisfaction — a clean illustration of Article 37 (satisfaction for a breach which cannot be made good by restitution or compensation because no material damage was proved).

6. The principles evolved

  1. Environmental damage per se is compensable. Compensation lies for the impairment or loss of environmental goods and services even where there is no market and no material damage to the State's own property — the "environment as an interest" approach, which has since been echoed in the climate-change advisory proceedings and in the 2024 ITLOS/ICJ/ECtHR climate decisions.
  2. Two heads of environmental reparation. (i) The loss of services in the period prior to recovery; and (ii) the cost of restoration. They are distinct and cumulative, and the second recognises that natural recovery may not occur.
  3. No single method of valuation; approximation permitted. International law prescribes no method; the Court will use elements of the parties' methods where they offer a reasonable basis, and will value the ecosystem as a whole, taking account of natural regeneration.
  4. Evidentiary flexibility. "The absence of adequate evidence as to the extent of material damage will not, in all situations, preclude an award of compensation" — a necessary concession to the nature of environmental harm, but one which must be balanced against the requirement of proof (the very heads rejected in this case show that flexibility is not licence).
  5. Causation governs the recoverability of State expenses. Monitoring and remediation costs are recoverable only if they are "temporary and extraordinary" and causally connected — the "sufficiently direct and certain causal nexus" standard which the Court also applied in Diallo and which it restated in DRC v. Uganda (2022).
  6. Interest is instrumental, not autonomous. Article 38 interest runs only where necessary to ensure full reparation; the Court may divide its award between heads on which interest is and is not payable.
  7. Satisfaction for procedural environmental breaches. A declaration is the appropriate remedy where the breach is the failure to assess, and no material harm is proved.

7. The examination angle

Examination box

If asked: "Discuss the reparation of environmental damage in international law with reference to Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (2018)." Structure: (i) the classical position — restitution in kind is the primary remedy, and compensation covers "financially assessable damage" (Art. 36); (ii) the novelty of 2018 — damage to the environment, and the impairment of its goods and services, is compensable in itself; (iii) two heads — loss of services prior to recovery and the cost of restoration; (iv) valuation — no prescribed method, ecosystem-wide assessment, natural regeneration taken into account, approximate where necessary but not unproved; (v) causation — the "sufficiently direct and certain" nexus, applied both to the environmental damage and to the State's expenses; (vi) interest — Article 38, the 4%/6% split, the refusal of pre-judgment interest on the environmental head; (vii) satisfaction for the environmental impact assessment breach; (viii) the figures — US$120,000 + US$2,708.39 + US$236,032.16 + US$20,150.04 = US$378,890.59, paid on 8 March 2018.

If asked: "How is compensation quantified? Discuss with examples." Use three cases in ascending order of difficulty: Corfu Channel (physical damage to warships and loss of life — expert assessment, £843,947); Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (environmental services — approximation, US$120,000); DRC v. Uganda (mass violations in armed conflict — global sums, US$325 million, in five instalments, post-judgment interest 6%). Add the prohibitions and the refinements: no punitive damages (Lusitania), no compensation without causation, interest only when necessary (Art. 38), contribution of the injured State to be considered (Art. 39), and the sums must remain within the capacity of the responsible State to pay (DRC v. Uganda, ¶376).

Cross-reference to Topic 4 §4.2.1 (damage theory). The case shows the modern position precisely: in ordinary treaty breaches (the failure to enact a law, the violation of embassy inviolability) no proof of damage is needed; but where the claim is for compensation, damage must be established and valued — because compensation is measured by injury (Article 31(2)) and by Article 36's "financially assessable damage".
