Case 29 · Armed Activities on the Territory of the Congo (DRC v. Uganda)

Armed Activities on the Territory of the Congo (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Uganda)

ICJ Reports 2005, p. 168 (Merits, Judgment of 19 December 2005) · Reparation: Judgment of 9 February 2022 (ICJ Reports 2022) · Provisional measures: Order of 1 July 2000 · Bench 2022: President Donoghue; Vice-President Gevorgian; Judges Tomka, Abraham, Bennouna, Yusuf, Xue, Sebutinde, Bhandari, Robinson, Salam, Iwasawa, Nolte, Charlesworth; Judges ad hoc Daudet (DRC) and Kamanda (Uganda)

Provenance. The case material prints the 2022 Judgment on reparation (pp. 92–107, paragraphs 1–378 as excerpted), which recites the operative findings of the 2005 Judgment on the merits. The account below follows that text and the 2005 dispositif.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

DRC v. Uganda is the syllabus's modern case on forms of reparation (§4.4), and it is the single most useful case in the whole of Topic 4 for four reasons. First, it is a two-stage case: the Court decided responsibility in 2005 and quantum in 2022, seventeen years later — which makes concrete the proposition that a declaration of responsibility creates a standing obligation to repair which may be quantified in a later phase. Second, it shows the ICJ applying the ILC Articles as customary law (Article 31 and Article 34 are expressly so characterised in the 2022 Judgment). Third, it is the Court's leading essay on quantum in the mass-violation context: global sums, equitable considerations, the pool of evidence, capacity to pay, and instalments. Fourth, it contains the classic 2005 holdings on consent, self-defence and occupation, so that it can be used for §4.3 (attribution and the defences) as much as for §4.4.

2. Facts

(a) The background. After the fall of President Mobutu in May 1997 the Democratic Republic of the Congo was torn by a rebellion supported from outside. In August 1998 Ugandan and Rwandan forces entered Congolese territory. At the invitation of the Congolese Government, Uganda had earlier (in the 1990s) sent troops into the DRC to help fight the Allied Democratic Forces (ADF), a Ugandan insurgent group operating from the Congo; Uganda also became a party to the Lusaka Agreement of July 1999 which was supposed to govern the orderly withdrawal of foreign forces. Uganda's forces became one of the most substantial foreign contingents in Congolese territory, and Uganda established what the Court described as an occupation of the Ituri district. During the occupation there were widespread violations: killings, torture and other inhuman treatment, destruction of villages and civilian buildings, failure to distinguish between civilians and combatants and to protect the civilian population, the training of child soldiers, the incitement of ethnic conflict between the Hema and the Lendu, and the looting, plundering and exploitation of Congolese natural resources (gold, diamonds, timber and other resources were removed by Ugandan forces and by persons acting in concert with them).

(b) The proceedings. The DRC filed its Application against Uganda on 23 June 1999 (parallel proceedings were brought against Rwanda and Burundi), invoking the declarations under Article 36(2) of the Statute, and alleging "acts of armed aggression … in flagrant violation of the United Nations Charter and of the Charter of the Organization of African Unity". On 1 July 2000 the Court indicated provisional measures ordering both Parties to prevent violations and to respect the Lusaka Agreement and the Security Council resolutions. Uganda put forward counter-claims arising out of the conduct of the DRC's armed forces in Kinshasa — in particular the attack on, and occupation of, the Ugandan Embassy, the maltreatment of Ugandan diplomats and other persons on the embassy premises, and the seizure of the embassy's archives and property.

(c) The 2005 Judgment. The Court found: (i) that Uganda, "by engaging in military activities against the Democratic Republic of the Congo on the latter's territory, by occupying Ituri and by actively extending military, logistic, economic and financial support to irregular forces having operated on the territory of the DRC, violated the principle of non-use of force in international relations and the principle of non-intervention"; (ii) that Uganda's armed forces had themselves committed violations of international human rights law and international humanitarian law, and that Uganda had failed, as an occupying Power in Ituri, to respect and ensure respect for those bodies of law; and (iii) that Uganda, by acts of looting, plundering and exploitation of Congolese natural resources and by its failure as an occupying Power to prevent them, violated obligations owed to the DRC. On Uganda's counter-claims, the Court found that the DRC had violated its obligations under the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961 by the attack on the Ugandan Embassy and the maltreatment of its diplomats, and that the DRC was therefore under an obligation to make reparation to Uganda. It also found that Uganda's acceptance of the invitation of the DRC's President was insufficient to justify the presence and activities of its forces, and it declared that Uganda must make full reparation for the injury caused, reserving the question of the amount, failing agreement, for a subsequent phase (para. 345).

(d) The 2022 Judgment on reparation. No agreement was reached, and the DRC asked the Court to fix the compensation. The Court appointed expert assistance, examined the UN's Mapping Report on the violations in the DRC (2010), the parties' evidence on 22 different categories of damage, and its own previous jurisprudence, and delivered the reparations judgment on 9 February 2022.

3. The issues in the reparation phase (2022)

  1. What principles and rules govern the assessment of reparation — and what is the status of the ILC Articles?
  2. How is the causal nexus to be established in a large-scale armed conflict, and does it differ between Ituri (occupied by Uganda) and the rest of the DRC?
  3. What is the nature, form and amount of reparation — should the Court award itemised or global sums?
  4. How much for damage to persons (loss of life and other harm to individuals)? How much for damage to property? How much for damage related to natural resources?
  5. Are macro-economic losses recoverable?
  6. Should there be satisfaction additional to compensation (prosecutions, a reconciliation fund, payment for non-material harm)?
  7. How should the sum be paid — in a lump sum or by instalments — and with what interest, and what account should be taken of Uganda's capacity to pay?

4. The arguments of both sides (2022)

For the Democratic Republic of the Congo

(i) Uganda must repair the damage in full, and the Court should adopt the DRC's own valuation of the damage, based on its assessment of the number of victims, the value of destroyed property and the value of the natural resources removed (claims which, added together, ran to many billions of dollars).

(ii) The causal nexus is established by the fact of occupation: Uganda, as the occupying Power in Ituri, is responsible for all damage resulting from the conflict there, including that caused by third parties, because it had the duty to ensure respect for human rights and humanitarian law.

(iii) Compensation should also cover the loss to the macro-economy of the DRC — the general disruption caused by the war.

(iv) Compensation alone is insufficient: Uganda should be required to give satisfaction by (a) investigating and prosecuting the officers responsible; (b) paying US$25 million into a fund to promote reconciliation between the Hema and the Lendu; and (c) paying US$100 million for the non-material harm suffered by the DRC and its population.

For Uganda

(i) The DRC's valuation was based on estimates unrelated to the damage for which Uganda was held responsible, and the claim was disproportionate.

(ii) The causal link must be "direct and certain" and must be proved for each head; the DRC had not proved individual instances.

(iii) Uganda's own economic capacity was limited; any award must be within its means.

(iv) The claim for criminal investigation and prosecution was a new liability claim not raised at the merits phase, and the claim for US$125 million for satisfaction concerned injury already covered by the other heads; satisfaction should at most be a symbolic payment, and the 2005 declaration was itself sufficient.

5. The decision

(a) The applicable principles (¶¶99–106)

The Court restated the law of reparation in terms of the ILC Articles, treating them as declaratory of customary international law: full reparation is required (Article 31, as quoted and characterised in ¶69 of the Judgment); "[a]s stated in Article 34 of the ILC Articles on State Responsibility, '[f]ull reparation for the injury caused by the internationally wrongful act shall take the form of restitution, compensation and satisfaction, either singly or in combination'"; compensation is appropriate "particularly in those cases where restitution is materially impossible" (¶101); and "it is well established in international law that reparation due to a State is compensatory in nature and should not have a punitive character" (¶102) — the modern restatement of the Lusitania principle. Two further propositions were added: any reparation "is intended, as far as possible, to benefit all those who suffered injury" (¶102), and, because of the exceptional circumstances, the Court might award compensation in the form of a global sum, "within the range of possibilities indicated by the evidence and taking into account equitable considerations" (¶106).

(b) Causation and the special position of Ituri (¶¶85–88)

The Court reaffirmed its settled standard: compensation may be awarded "only if there is 'a sufficiently direct and certain causal nexus between the wrongful act … and the injury suffered by the Applicant, consisting of all damage of any type, material or moral'". But it drew a principled distinction between the areas of the DRC occupied and effectively controlled by Uganda (Ituri) and the rest of the country: "Uganda is under an obligation to make reparation for all damage resulting from the conflict in Ituri, even that resulting from the conduct of third parties, unless it has established, with respect to a particular injury, that it was not caused by Uganda's failure to meet its obligations as an occupying Power" (¶87). That reversal of the evidential burden in occupied territory is one of the most practically significant holdings of the judgment, and it is why the largest element of the award relates to Ituri. The Court also refused to transfer the more demanding standard of the Genocide case (2007), which had been "confine[d] … to determining the specific scope of the duty to prevent in the Genocide Convention" and had not "purport[ed] to establish a general jurisprudence applicable to all cases" of prevention (¶88).

(c) The heads of damage and the amounts (¶¶133–365)
  • Damage to persons — a global sum of US$225,000,000. The Court found it appropriate to award "a single global sum … for the loss of life and other damage to persons", given that Uganda had not established that particular injuries in Ituri were not caused by its failure as an occupying Power (¶256).
  • Damage to property — a global sum of US$40,000,000. The evidence (including the UN Mapping Report) was "limited" but substantiated many instances of damage; the Court made an overall assessment "taking into account equitable considerations" (¶257).
  • Looting, plundering and exploitation of natural resources — a global sum of US$60,000,000 (¶364).
  • Rejected: macro-economic damage. "The Court concludes that the DRC has not demonstrated that a sufficiently direct and certain causal nexus exists between the internationally wrongful acts of Uganda and any possible macroeconomic damage … The Court thus rejects the claim of the DRC for macroeconomic damage" (¶365), citing the approach of the Eritrea–Ethiopia Claims Commission. The refusal to allow "general disruption to the economy" as a head is a permanent caution against inflation of claims.
(d) Satisfaction (¶¶366–373)

The Court recalled that "in general, a declaration of violation is, in itself, appropriate satisfaction in most cases" (¶368, citing Pulp Mills, Djibouti v. France and the Genocide case). Applying that rule: (i) the request for an order that Uganda investigate and prosecute its officers was unnecessary, because Uganda "has a duty to investigate, prosecute and punish those responsible … by virtue of the obligations incumbent on it" — no additional specific measure was required (¶371); (ii) the US$25 million claimed for a reconciliation fund was not awarded, because the material damage caused by the ethnic conflict was already covered by the compensation awarded, but the Court invited the Parties to co-operate in good faith to establish methods of promoting reconciliation between the Hema and the Lendu (¶372); and (iii) the claim for US$100 million for non-material harm to the DRC was rejected, since the non-material harm was included in the global sums awarded (¶373), and the Court referred to the practice of the Eritrea–Ethiopia Claims Commission in rejecting Ethiopia's claim for moral damage.

(e) The total sum, the instalments and the interest (¶¶374–378)

"The total amount of compensation awarded to the DRC is US$325,000,000. This global sum includes US$225,000,000 for damage to persons, US$40,000,000 for damage to property, and US$60,000,000 for damage related to natural resources. The total sum is to be paid in annual instalments of US$65,000,000, due on 1 September of each year, from 2022 to 2026. … should payment be delayed, post-judgment interest at an annual rate of 6 per cent on each instalment will accrue on any overdue amount from the day which follows the day on which the instalment was due." (¶¶374–375)

Two further points complete the remedial architecture. First, capacity to pay: "[t]he Court is satisfied that the total sum awarded, and the terms of payment, remain within the capacity of Uganda to pay. Therefore, the Court does not need to consider the question whether, in determining the amount of compensation, account should be taken of the financial burden imposed on the responsible State, given its economic condition" (¶376) — a recognition that the responsible State's resources may in principle be relevant. Second, distribution to victims: the Court noted with approval the DRC's undertaking that the compensation would be "fairly and effectively distributed to victims of the harm, under the supervision of organs whose members include representatives of victims and civil society and whose operation is supported by international experts", and encouraged the fund to consider measures for the benefit of the affected communities as a whole (¶377). The Court also rejected the DRC's claim for costs (¶378).

6. The principles evolved

  1. Two-stage reparation. A judgment on responsibility may fix liability in principle and leave quantum to a later phase (2005 → 2022), the same structure as Corfu Channel, Tehran Hostages and Costa Rica v. Nicaragua.
  2. The ILC Articles as customary law. Article 31 (full reparation) and Article 34 (forms of reparation) applied as customary international law; compensation is the appropriate form where restitution is materially impossible.
  3. Compensatory, not punitive. "Reparation … should not have a punitive character" (¶102) — the Lusitania rule restated for mass-atrocity cases, and a direct contrast with the punitive damages debate.
  4. Global sums for mass claims. Where thousands of individual injuries cannot be itemised, the Court may fix a single global sum per head, "within the range of possibilities indicated by the evidence and taking into account equitable considerations".
  5. The reversed burden in occupied territory. An occupying Power must repair all damage resulting from the conflict in the occupied area, including damage caused by third parties, unless it proves that a particular injury was not caused by its failure. This is the most far-reaching attribution-like holding of the case.
  6. Causation is not diluted. Macro-economic damage was rejected for want of a "sufficiently direct and certain causal nexus" — proof of catastrophic consequences is not proof of causal connection.
  7. Satisfaction is ordinarily by declaration; a request for criminal prosecutions is unnecessary where the obligation to prosecute already exists; and non-material harm to the State is not separately compensable where the global sums already reflect the injury.
  8. Modalities. Instalments, post-judgment interest (6%), capacity to pay, distribution to victims through a supervised fund, and costs refused — a complete picture of how a modern reparation judgment is executed.
  9. Consent, self-defence and occupation (2005). Consent to the presence of foreign troops is limited by its terms and by time; self-defence is unavailable without an armed attack, proportionality and satisfaction of the Charter's conditions; and the law of occupation imposes duties of prevention and protection enforceable by reparation.

7. The examination angle

Examination box

If asked: "Discuss the forms of reparation with reference to Armed Activities (DRC v. Uganda)." Set out Article 31/34 and Chorzów; then use the case for the following four propositions — quantum in mass-violation cases is fixed by global sums from a pool of evidence; reparation is compensatory and never punitive; restitution was impossible (an occupation cannot be undone), so compensation was ordered; and satisfaction takes the form of a declaration, additional remedies being refused. Finish with the modalities: US$325,000,000 (225 + 40 + 60), five instalments of US$65,000,000 from 1 September 2022, 6% post-judgment interest, and the costs claim rejected.

If asked about causation in the law of reparation. Give the standard ("sufficiently direct and certain causal nexus"), show its application to persons, property and natural resources (accepted) and to macro-economic loss (rejected), and then contrast the reversed burden in Ituri: an occupying Power must prove that a particular injury was not caused by its failure. Cite ¶¶85–88 and ¶¶256–257.

If asked about the defences (§4.3 / Article 20–21). Use the 2005 merits: consent (limited to the agreed purpose and exceeded), self-defence (no armed attack by the DRC, no proportionality), and the rejection of the argument that Uganda's intervention was justified by the invitation of the Congolese President. Then contrast with the DRC's own liability for the attack on Uganda's embassy — a reminder that both parties to an armed conflict are liable for their own violations, and the only ICJ case in which the counter-claim of an occupying State succeeded on the diplomatic immunities point.

Cross-reference. Topic 5 (use of force): the 2005 judgment is one of the four pillars (with Nicaragua, Oil Platforms and the Wall Opinion) on self-defence and intervention. Topic 7 (diplomatic immunities): the DRC's liability for the Kinshasa embassy. Topic 3: how the ILC Articles, as customary law, are applied by the Court in the absence of a treaty on responsibility.

↑ top

§ 4.5 Rule of Exhaustion of Local Remedies

The rule of the exhaustion of local remedies is the procedural price of diplomatic protection: before a State may bring an international claim on behalf of its national, the national must have used, and exhausted, the legal remedies available to him in the legal system of the State alleged to be responsible. The rule appears in the ILC Articles as one of the two conditions of admissibility of a claim, in Article 44:

Article 44 (Admissibility of claims). "The responsibility of a State may not be invoked if: (a) The claim is not brought in accordance with any applicable rule relating to the nationality of claims; (b) The claim is one to which the rule of exhaustion of local remedies applies and any available and effective local remedy has not been exhausted."

Article 45 (Loss of the right to invoke responsibility). "The responsibility of a State may not be invoked if: (a) The injured State has validly waived the claim; (b) The injured State is to be considered as having, by reason of its conduct, validly acquiesced in the lapse of the claim."

A. The rule, and its raison d'être

The prescribed text states the position in the following words, which are worth reproducing in an examination answer:

"It is an established rule of customary international law that before diplomatic protection is afforded, or before recourse may be made to international arbitral or judicial processes, local remedies must be exhausted. The raison d'être of the rule is: (i) respect for the sovereignty of States — that matters occurring in the territory of a State must be given the first opportunity to exercise jurisdiction; (ii) to allow the State concerned the opportunity to afford redress within its own legal system for the alleged wrong; to reduce the number of possible international claims; (iii) it rests on considerations of practical convenience as the local courts are better placed to evaluate the facts; and (iv) there can be no claim on the basis of denial of justice until local remedies have been exhausted."

Two consequences follow from the fourth reason. The first is doctrinal: on the classical or "denial of justice" view, the wrong to the alien is not complete until the local courts have failed to redress it — so that the exhaustion rule is not merely a procedural hurdle but the very definition of the moment when the international wrong matures. The rival or "direct injury" view holds that the wrong to the individual is complete when it occurs, and that the exhaustion rule is purely a matter of admissibility. The ILC chose the second view for the purposes of the Articles, placing the rule in Article 44 (admissibility) and not in Part One; but the older learning — and the whole law of "denial of justice" — belongs to the first. A good answer states both and says which the Articles adopt.

The second consequence is practical: the standard by which local justice is judged is a minimal one. In the Neer Claim (United States v. Mexico), 1926, 4 UNRIAA 60, the Commission held that the treatment of an alien, in order to constitute an international delinquency, "should amount to an outrage, to bad faith, to wilful neglect of duty, or to an insufficiency of governmental action so far short of international standards that every reasonable and impartial man would readily recognize its insufficiency" — a formula still cited, though modern human rights law has raised the standard of protection considerably.

B. To what claims does the rule apply?

  • Claims of diplomatic protection. The rule applies "only … in those cases where the State brings a claim on behalf of a national. In principle, exhaustion of local remedies is not required when a State is bringing a claim to protect its own direct interests" (Verma). Hence a claim for an attack on a State's warship, embassy or territory (the Corfu Channel case; the Tehran Hostages case; the Enrica Lexie arbitration) does not require the exhaustion of Albanian, Iranian or Indian remedies.
  • Mixed claims — where the State claims both for itself and for its national in the same proceeding — present the hard case. The jurisprudence (the Interhandel case; the ELSI case; Judge Dugard's Third Report on Diplomatic Protection, 2002) supports a "preponderance of interests" test: the exhaustion rule applies only where the claim is overwhelmingly concerned with the injury to the national. As Verma puts it, in such a case "it becomes difficult to decide whether the rule requiring exhaustion of local remedies applies or not".
  • Claims of the individual against the State. In the human-rights systems the same rule reappears in a different dress: Article 35(1) ECHR (domestic remedies must be exhausted), Article 5(2)(b) of the Optional Protocol to the ICCPR (the Committee shall not consider a communication unless the individual "has exhausted all available domestic remedies"), Article 46(1)(a) of the American Convention and Article 50 of the African Charter. These are independent treaty rules, but they are applied with the same "available and effective" logic.
  • Where the rule is waived. The requirement may be dispensed with by treaty, "but this must be explicitly stated and not merely implied" (Verma). Examples: Article 26 of the ICSID Convention (consent to arbitration is, unless otherwise stated, deemed consent to the exclusion of any other remedy, though a State may require the prior exhaustion of local remedies as a condition of its consent); most bilateral investment treaties; and the Optional Protocol to the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, under which the ICJ decided LaGrand (2001) and Avena (2004) without any exhaustion requirement — a point of real practical importance, since it enabled Germany and Mexico to reach the Court despite the exhaustion of all United States judicial remedies.

C. What must be exhausted, and what need not be

"Local remedies" means — in the words of the Ambatielos arbitration and of Article 14(2) of the ILC Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection (2006) — "legal remedies which are open to an injured person before the judicial or administrative courts or bodies, whether ordinary or special, of the State alleged to be responsible for causing the injury". Verma quotes the Ambatielos formulation in full:

"not only reference to the courts and tribunals, but also the use of the procedural facilities which municipal law makes available to litigants before such courts and tribunals. It is the whole system of legal protection, as provided by municipal law …" (Ambatielos Arbitration (Greece v. United Kingdom), 1956, 12 UNRIAA 83)

From that definition flow four working propositions:

  1. Only legal remedies count. "The obligation to exhaust local remedies relates only to legal remedies, and would exclude remedies that are discretionary" (Verma) — a petition for clemency, a pardon, an act of grace or an administrative indulgence is not a "local remedy" for this purpose.
  2. Exhaustion must be complete. "Local remedies must be exhausted to the fullest extent, including the appeal procedure provided under local law. An individual must, therefore, employ 'all administrative, arbitral or judicial remedies.'" The rule is satisfied only when the highest available instance has decided — a rule expressed in Article 14(1) of the Diplomatic Protection Articles as requiring the exhaustion of remedies "as provided for by the local law" including appeals.
  3. But only effective, and only those capable of affecting the outcome. "Only effective remedies which could affect the final outcome of the case need be exhausted." This is codified in Article 14(3) of the Diplomatic Protection Articles: "Local remedies shall be exhausted where an international claim, or request for a declaratory judgment related to the claim, is brought preponderantly on the basis of an injury to a national or other person …" and in the Finnish Ships arbitration (1934), the El Oro Mining and Railway Co. case (1931) and the Interhandel case (1959).
  4. Futility dispenses with the requirement. "Local remedies need not be exhausted if it is evident that there is no justice to exhaust, or where it is apparent that any attempt to seek redress would be thwarted or unlikely to yield any results" (Verma, citing the Robert E. Brown case, 1923). The ILC expresses the same idea in Article 15 of the Diplomatic Protection Articles (below).

D. The exceptions — Article 15 of the ILC Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection, 2006

Article 15 (Exceptions to the local remedies rule). Local remedies need not be exhausted: "(a) There are no reasonably available local remedies to provide effective redress, or the local remedies provide no reasonable possibility of such redress; (b) There is undue delay in the remedial process which is attributable to the State alleged to be responsible; (c) There was no relevant connection between the injured person and the State alleged to be responsible at the date of injury; (d) The injured person is manifestly precluded from pursuing local remedies; or (e) The State alleged to be responsible has waived the requirement that local remedies be exhausted."

Table 4.13 · The exhaustion rule — requirements, exceptions and authorities
PointRuleAuthority
ScopeApplies to diplomatic protection claims; not to claims for direct injury to the StateVerma; ILC Arts. 44(b); Diplomatic Protection Arts. 14–15
What counts as a remedy"The whole system of legal protection": courts, tribunals, administrative bodies, appeals — not discretionary reliefAmbatielos (1956); Art. 14(2) Diplomatic Protection Articles
EffectivenessOnly "available and effective" remedies need be used; those that cannot affect the outcome may be ignoredFinnish Ships (1934); El Oro Mining (1931); Interhandel (1959)
Futility / no justice to exhaustNo requirement where redress would be thwarted or is clearly impossibleRobert E. Brown (1923); Art. 15(a) Diplomatic Protection Articles
Undue delayExcused where delay in the local process is attributable to the respondent StateArt. 15(b) Diplomatic Protection Articles; human rights practice (ECHR)
No relevant connectionExcused where the injured person had no link with the respondent State at the date of injuryArt. 15(c) Diplomatic Protection Articles
Burden and testing the remediesContested: how far must the alien test apparently futile remedies? The mere paper existence of a remedy does not require its use in every caseILC Commentary to Art. 44(b); Interhandel (1959)
WaiverMay be dispensed with by treaty, expressly and not by implicationOptional Protocol, VCCR (LaGrand, Avena, Jadhav); ICSID Convention Art. 26; most BITs
Relation to denial of justiceNo claim for denial of justice until local remedies are exhausted; the standard of "denial" is a minimum standardVerma; Neer (1926); ELSI (1989)
Consequence of failureInadmissibility, not extinction — the claim may be brought once the remedies have been exhaustedArt. 44(b); Interhandel (Switzerland's claim held inadmissible because proceedings were still pending)

E. The leading cases

  • Ambatielos Arbitration (Greece v. United Kingdom), 1956, 12 UNRIAA 83. The classical modern authority on the content of the rule: it is not confined to the taking of proceedings before the courts, but extends to the "procedural facilities" which municipal law makes available — so that a litigant who has not raised the substance of his complaint in the local proceedings (here, the failure to call a particular witness) has not exhausted his remedies. The case is the explanation of why the rule is described in the Diplomatic Protection Articles as covering "the whole system of legal protection".
  • Finnish Ships Arbitration (Finland v. Great Britain), 1934, 3 UNRIAA 1479. The leading case on the effectiveness of remedies: the parties disputed which of them bore the burden of showing whether a remedy was available and effective. The arbitrator's approach (that the claimant must show that no effective remedy existed but that the respondent must identify the remedies which it says were available) remains the practical working rule, and it is the source of the modern formula "available and effective local remedy" in Article 44(b). Compare the El Oro Mining and Railway Co. case (1931), where a remedy which was in form available was held not to require exhaustion because of the delays and the state of the judicial system.
  • Interhandel Case (Switzerland v. United States), Preliminary Objections, 1959, ICJ Reports 1959, p. 6. The Interhandel corporation had litigated in the United States for some ten years over assets vested as enemy property. The Court held Switzerland's claim inadmissible because, at the date of the Application, proceedings were still pending in the United States courts: the local remedies had not been exhausted. The case is the standard authority for (i) the rule's application to corporate claims; (ii) the fact that the rule relates to the position at the time the international claim is brought; and (iii) the doctrinal discussion of mixed claims and the "preponderance of interests" test, which the Court's judgment opened up.
  • Elettronica Sicula S.p.A. (ELSI) (United States v. Italy), ICJ 1989, p. 15. A Chamber of the Court considered the requisition by Italy of the ELSI plant belonging to a United States corporation. The Chamber treated the exhaustion rule as a rule of substance and not of "formal character", scrutinised the course of the Italian proceedings and found that the failure of the Italian courts to grant relief did not amount to a denial of justice — while the requisition itself was held to be an "arbitrary" measure in breach of the FCN Treaty. The case is the leading modern authority on the relationship between the exhaustion rule, the denial-of-justice standard and the substantive protection of foreign investment.
  • Ahmadou Sadio Diallo (Guinea v. DRC), Preliminary Objections, ICJ 2007, p. 582. The Court upheld the DRC's objection of non-exhaustion in respect of Guinea's claims concerning Mr Diallo's rights as an associé of two Congolese companies — because the remedies available to the companies had not been used — while holding the claims concerning the protection of Mr Diallo himself (his arrest, detention and expulsion) admissible, since he had pursued the remedies available in the Congolese courts, including the recours en annulation against the expulsion decree. The case is the modern illustration both of the rule and of its limits.
  • North American Dredging Company of Texas Claim (United States v. Mexico), 1926, 4 UNRIAA 26 — the Calvo clause. The contract contained the Calvo clause: the claimant agreed to "be considered as a Mexican in all matters pertaining to the contract" and not to invoke diplomatic protection. The Commission held that while the clause bound the individual and barred his claim, it could not deprive the State of its own right of protection where the claim arose from a violation of international law independent of the contract. The case explains why the Calvo doctrine (beloved of Latin-American States in the nineteenth century) survives only as a rule of contract, not as a rule of international law.
  • LaGrand and Avena — the exceptions' outer edge. Because the VCCR Optional Protocol gives the ICJ jurisdiction without any exhaustion requirement, Germany and Mexico could bring the claims of their nationals despite the fact that all United States judicial remedies had been used up and that the "procedural default" rule barred further relief. The Court's answer to the procedural default problem was not the exhaustion rule but the substantive duty of review and reconsideration (Case 27 above). This is worth a sentence in any answer on the topic: the exhaustion rule applies only where the international obligation requires it, and States may — and frequently do — waive it by treaty.

F. Related rules and instruments

  • Nationality of claims (Article 44(a)). The companion condition of admissibility: the claim must be brought in accordance with the rules on nationality of claims — the continuous nationality rule, the "genuine link" requirement (Nottebohm, 1955, ¶¶95–96), the protection of corporations and shareholders (Barcelona Traction, 1970 — Case 24 above), and the rule in Mavrommatis (1924, ¶89) that by taking up the case of one of its subjects a State "is in reality asserting its own rights".
  • The ILC Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection, 2006. Articles 1–8 (nationality of individuals and corporations, continuous nationality, dual nationals, stateless persons, refugees), 14–15 (local remedies and exceptions) and 19 (recommended practice) are the modern codification. They were adopted together with the 2001 Articles and are the natural companion text for this section.
  • Human rights procedures. Article 35(1) ECHR — "the Court may only deal with the matter after all domestic remedies have been exhausted, according to the generally recognised rules of international law"; Article 5(2)(b) of the ICCPR Optional Protocol; Article 46(1)(a) ACHR (with Article 46(2)'s exceptions — ineffectiveness, denial of access, delay); Article 50 of the African Charter. The practice of the supervisory bodies on the "effectiveness" of remedies is the richest contemporary source for the exceptions in Article 15.
  • Investment treaty practice. Article 26 of the ICSID Convention is the classic example of a treaty in which the requirement is dispensed with: consent to ICSID arbitration is "deemed consent to such arbitration to the exclusion of any other remedy", so that no local-remedy requirement applies unless the treaty says so. Older bilateral investment treaties often did the same, or contained "fork in the road" clauses (if the investor chooses the domestic courts, the arbitral route is closed) and "no-U-turn" clauses (the investor may arbitrate only after withdrawing a domestic claim). India's Model BIT of 2015 deliberately reversed that older practice: the investor must first submit the dispute to the competent domestic court and pursue that remedy for at least five years before international arbitration may be commenced — a striking contemporary illustration of the point that the extent of the exhaustion requirement depends on the primary obligation, not on the general law of responsibility (and a policy response to India's experience as a respondent State in investor claims).

Fig. 4.10 — The exhaustion of local remedies: scope, test and exceptions.

Examination box

Model answer structure. (1) Definition and source: Article 44(b) ILC; customary international law; the ILC Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection, 2006, Articles 14–15. (2) Rationale: the four reasons in the prescribed text (sovereignty and the first opportunity to redress; the reduction of international claims; the practical convenience of local fact-finding; and the denial-of-justice rationale). (3) Scope: it applies to diplomatic protection, not to direct injury; mixed claims and the preponderance-of-interests test. (4) Content: Ambatielos — "the whole system of legal protection", including procedural facilities; legal, not discretionary, remedies; exhaustion to the fullest extent including appeals; only effective remedies. (5) Exceptions: the five in Article 15 of the Diplomatic Protection Articles, with Robert E. Brown (futility), El Oro Mining (delay), and waiver by treaty (the VCCR Optional Protocol; ICSID Article 26; India's Model BIT, 2015). (6) Cases: Ambatielos; Finnish Ships; Interhandel; ELSI; Diallo (2007); LaGrand/Avena as the waiver illustration. (7) Conclusion: the rule protects the respondent State's opportunity to do justice first, but it is a rule of admissibility only, it yields to futility and delay, and it is freely displaced by treaty — which is why modern investment and consular practice has narrowed it while human rights law has kept it alive.

Short-note version (5 marks). Definition (Art. 44(b)) → rationale (sovereignty, opportunity to redress, local fact-finding, denial of justice) → "available and effective" (Ambatielos; Finnish Ships) → exceptions (futility, delay, no connection, waiver) → leading cases (Interhandel; ELSI; Diallo) → relation to nationality of claims (Art. 44(a)) and to denial of justice (Neer).

A trap to avoid. The rule does not extinguish the claim: it postpones it. And it is not a rule about the merits of the local judgment; a claimant need not show that the local courts were wrong, only that he used them and that the injury persists — the exhaustion rule and the denial-of-justice standard are distinct, though they meet in the same cases.

↑ top

§ 4.6 ILC Draft Code on Responsibility of States for Internationally Wrongful Acts, 2001 — Full Text

This section reproduces, verbatim, the text of the Draft Articles as printed in the course material — Articles 1 to 54, arranged under Parts One to Three and their Chapters (the case material stops at Article 54; Part Four, Articles 55 to 59, is supplied afterwards from the official text of the Articles, clearly marked). Nothing has been paraphrased. The remaining sections of these notes (§§4.1–4.5 and the case digests) contain the commentary on each article; what follows is the instrument itself, which is what a DU paper expects to see quoted.

What the document is, and what it is not. The International Law Commission adopted the Articles on Responsibility of States for Internationally Wrongful Acts on 9 August 2001, at its fifty-third session, under the special rapporteurship of Mr. James Crawford (following Ago, Riphagen and Arangio-Ruiz). The General Assembly "took note of" the Articles and commended them to the attention of Governments by resolution 56/83 of 12 December 2001, the text being annexed; the item has stayed on the Assembly's agenda ever since — on a roughly triennial cycle since 2004 the Assembly has taken note of the Articles again, of the Secretary-General's compilations of decisions of international courts and tribunals referring to them, and of the comments of Governments, while leaving "the question of their future adoption or other appropriate action" open. The Sixth Committee continues to consider whether a diplomatic conference should be convened; no such decision has yet been taken. The Articles are therefore not a treaty: they bind no State as such. But they are the most authoritative statement of the customary law of State responsibility, and the International Court of Justice has repeatedly applied them on that footing — Article 31 and Article 34 were expressly described as reflecting customary international law in Armed Activities (DRC v. Uganda), Judgment of 9 February 2022, and the Court cited the Articles in Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros (1997, using the 1996 draft), in Bosnian Genocide (2007), in Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (2018) and elsewhere. Several hundred decisions of arbitral tribunals and human rights bodies now do the same. For examination purposes, the safest formula is: "the Articles are not a treaty; they are a codification and progressive development of customary international law, taken note of by GA resolution 56/83, and applied by the ICJ as evidence of custom."

Two structural warnings. First, the case material's printing stops at Article 54, so Part Four (Articles 55–59) is missing from it — the lex specialis rule in Article 55, the interplay with international organisations (Article 57) and with individual responsibility (Article 58) and the supremacy of the Charter (Article 59) must be added from the official text. Second, the Articles contained, in the official edition, the Commission's commentaries to each article: the commentaries are the most important aid to interpretation and are cited by courts almost as often as the text. Where an exam asks for "the ILC Articles and their commentary", the commentaries to Articles 2, 8, 20, 25, 31, 36, 39 and 44 are the ones most often referred to.

B. The full text (Articles 1–54, verbatim from the course material)

DRAFT CODE ON RESPONSIBILITY OF STATES FOR INTERNATIONALLY WRONGFUL ACTS [Adopted by the International Law Commission at its Fifty-third Session (2001)]

PART ONE

THE INTERNATIONALLY WRONGFUL ACT OF A STATE

CHAPTER I

General principles

Article 1 — Responsibility of a State for its internationally wrongful acts
Every internationally wrongful act of a State entails the international responsibility of that State.

Article 2 — Elements of an internationally wrongful act of a State
There is an internationally wrongful act of a State when conduct consisting of an action or omission:
(a) Is attributable to the State under international law; and
(b) Constitutes a breach of an international obligation of the State.

Article 3 — Characterization of an act of a State as internationally wrongful
The characterization of an act of a State as internationally wrongful is governed by international law. Such characterization is not affected by the characterization of the same act as lawful by internal law.

CHAPTER II

Attribution of conduct to a State

Article 4 — Conduct of organs of a State
1. The conduct of any State organ shall be considered an act of that State under international law, whether the organ exercises legislative, executive, judicial or any other functions, whatever position it holds in the organization of the State, and whatever its character as an organ of the central government or of a territorial unit of the State.
2. An organ includes any person or entity which has that status in accordance with the internal law of the State.

Article 5 — Conduct of persons or entities exercising elements of governmental authority
The conduct of a person or entity which is not an organ of the State under article 4 but which is empowered by the law of that State to exercise elements of the governmental authority shall be considered an act of the State under international law, provided the person or entity is acting in that capacity in the particular instance.

Article 6 — Conduct of organs placed at the disposal of a State by another State
The conduct of an organ placed at the disposal of a State by another State shall be considered an act of the former State under international law if the organ is acting in the exercise of elements of the governmental authority of the State at whose disposal it is placed.

Article 7 — Excess of authority or contravention of instructions
The conduct of an organ of a State or of a person or entity empowered to exercise elements of the governmental authority shall be considered an act of the State under international law if the organ, person or entity acts in that capacity, even if it exceeds its authority or contravenes instructions.

Article 8 — Conduct directed or controlled by a State
The conduct of a person or group of persons shall be considered an act of a State under international law if the person or group of persons is in fact acting on the instructions of, or under the direction or control of, that State in carrying out the conduct.

Article 9 — Conduct carried out in the absence or default of the official authorities
The conduct of a person or group of persons shall be considered an act of a State under international law if the person or group of persons is in fact exercising elements of the governmental authority in the absence or default of the official authorities and in circumstances such as to call for the exercise of those elements of authority.

Article 10 — Conduct of an insurrectional or other movement
1. The conduct of an insurrectional movement which becomes the new government of a State shall be considered an act of that State under international law.
2. The conduct of a movement, insurrectional or other, which succeeds in establishing a new State in part of the territory of a pre-existing State or in a territory under its administration shall be considered an act of the new State under international law.
3. This article is without prejudice to the attribution to a State of any conduct, however related to that of the movement concerned, which is to be considered an act of that State by virtue of articles 4 to 9.

Article 11 — Conduct acknowledged and adopted by a State as its own
Conduct which is not attributable to a State under the preceding articles shall nevertheless be considered an act of that State under international law if and to the extent that the State acknowledges and adopts the conduct in question as its own.

CHAPTER III

Breach of an international obligation

Article 12 — Existence of a breach of an international obligation
There is a breach of an international obligation by a State when an act of that State is not in conformity with what is required of it by that obligation, regardless of its origin or character.

Article 13 — International obligation in force for a State
An act of a State does not constitute a breach of an international obligation unless the State is bound by the obligation in question at the time the act occurs.

Article 14 — Extension in time of the breach of an international obligation
1. The breach of an international obligation by an act of a State not having a continuing character occurs at the moment when the act is performed, even if its effects continue.
2. The breach of an international obligation by an act of a State having a continuing character extends over the entire period during which the act continues and remains not in conformity with the international obligation.
3. The breach of an international obligation requiring a State to prevent a given event occurs when the event occurs and extends over the entire period during which the event continues and remains not in conformity with that obligation.

Article 15 — Breach consisting of a composite act
1. The breach of an international obligation by a State through a series of actions or omissions defined in aggregate as wrongful, occurs when the action or omission occurs which, taken with the other actions or omissions, is sufficient to constitute the wrongful act.
2. In such a case, the breach extends over the entire period starting with the first of the actions or omissions of the series and lasts for as long as these actions or omissions are repeated and remain not in conformity with the international obligation.

CHAPTER IV

Responsibility of a State in connection with the act of another State

Article 16 — Aid or assistance in the commission of an internationally wrongful act
A State which aids or assists another State in the commission of an internationally wrongful act by the latter is internationally responsible for doing so if:
(a) That State does so with knowledge of the circumstances of the internationally wrongful act; and
(b) The act would be internationally wrongful if committed by that State.

Article 17 — Direction and control exercised over the commission of an internationally wrongful act
A State which directs and controls another State in the commission of an internationally wrongful act by the latter is internationally responsible for that act if:
(a) That State does so with knowledge of the circumstances of the internationally wrongful act; and
(b) The act would be internationally wrongful if committed by that State.

Article 18 — Coercion of another State
A State which coerces another State to commit an act is internationally responsible for that act if:
(a) The act would, but for the coercion, be an internationally wrongful act of the coerced State; and
(b) The coercing State does so with knowledge of the circumstances of the act.

Article 19 — Effect of this chapter
This chapter is without prejudice to the international responsibility, under other provisions of these articles, of the State which commits the act in question, or of any other State.

CHAPTER V

Circumstances precluding wrongfulness

Article 20 — Consent
Valid consent by a State to the commission of a given act by another State precludes the wrongfulness of that act in relation to the former State to the extent that the act remains within the limits of that consent.

Article 21 — Self-defence
The wrongfulness of an act of a State is precluded if the act constitutes a lawful measure of self-defence taken in conformity with the Charter of the United Nations.

Article 22 — Countermeasures in respect of an internationally wrongful act
The wrongfulness of an act of a State not in conformity with an international obligation towards another State is precluded if and to the extent that the act constitutes a countermeasure taken against the latter State in accordance with chapter II of Part Three.

Article 23 — Force majeure
1. The wrongfulness of an act of a State not in conformity with an international obligation of that State is precluded if the act is due to force majeure, that is the occurrence of an irresistible force or of an unforeseen event, beyond the control of the State, making it materially impossible in the circumstances to perform the obligation.
2. Paragraph 1 does not apply if:
(a) The situation of force majeure is due, either alone or in combination with other factors, to the conduct of the State invoking it; or
(b) The State has assumed the risk of that situation occurring.

Article 24 — Distress
1. The wrongfulness of an act of a State not in conformity with an international obligation of that State is precluded if the author of the act in question has no other reasonable way, in a situation of distress, of saving the author’s life or the lives of other persons entrusted to the author’s care.
2. Paragraph 1 does not apply if:
(a) The situation of distress is due, either alone or in combination with other factors, to the conduct of the State invoking it; or
(b) The act in question is likely to create a comparable or greater peril.

Article 25 — Necessity
1. Necessity may not be invoked by a State as a ground for precluding the wrongfulness of an act not in conformity with an international obligation of that State unless the act:
(a) Is the only way for the State to safeguard an essential interest against a grave and imminent peril; and
(b) Does not seriously impair an essential interest of the State or States towards which the obligation exists, or of the international community as a whole.
2. In any case, necessity may not be invoked by a State as a ground for precluding wrongfulness if:
(a) The international obligation in question excludes the possibility of invoking necessity; or
(b) The State has contributed to the situation of necessity.

Article 26 — Compliance with peremptory norms
Nothing in this chapter precludes the wrongfulness of any act of a State which is not in conformity with an obligation arising under a peremptory norm of general international law.

Article 27 — Consequences of invoking a circumstance precluding wrongfulness
The invocation of a circumstance precluding wrongfulness in accordance with this chapter is without prejudice to:
(a) Compliance with the obligation in question, if and to the extent that the circumstance precluding wrongfulness no longer exists;
(b) The question of compensation for any material loss caused by the act in question.

PART TWO

CONTENT OF THE INTERNATIONAL RESPONSIBILITY OF A STATE

CHAPTER I

General principles

Article 28 — Legal consequences of an internationally wrongful act
The international responsibility of a State which is entailed by an internationally wrongful act in accordance with the provisions of Part One involves legal consequences as set out in this Part.

Article 29 — Continued duty of performance
The legal consequences of an internationally wrongful act under this Part do not affect the continued duty of the responsible State to perform the obligation breached.

Article 30 — Cessation and non-repetition
The State responsible for the internationally wrongful act is under an obligation:
(a) To cease that act, if it is continuing;
(b) To offer appropriate assurances and guarantees of non-repetition, if circumstances so require.

Article 31 — Reparation
1. The responsible State is under an obligation to make full reparation for the injury caused by the internationally wrongful act.
2. Injury includes any damage, whether material or moral, caused by the internationally wrongful act of a State.

Article 32 — Irrelevance of internal law
The responsible State may not rely on the provisions of its internal law as justification for failure to comply with its obligations under this Part.

Article 33 — Scope of international obligations set out in this Part
1. The obligations of the responsible State set out in this Part may be owed to another State, to several States, or to the international community as a whole, depending in particular on the character and content of the international obligation and on the circumstances of the breach.
2. This Part is without prejudice to any right, arising from the international responsibility of a State, which may accrue directly to any person or entity other than a State.

CHAPTER II

Reparation for injury

Article 34 — Forms of reparation
Full reparation for the injury caused by the internationally wrongful act shall take the form of restitution, compensation and satisfaction, either singly or in combination, in accordance with the provisions of this chapter.

Article 35 — Restitution
A State responsible for an internationally wrongful act is under an obligation to make restitution, that is, to re-establish the situation which existed before the wrongful act was committed, provided and to the extent that restitution:
(a) Is not materially impossible;
(b) Does not involve a burden out of all proportion to the benefit deriving from restitution instead of compensation.

Article 36 — Compensation
1. The State responsible for an internationally wrongful act is under an obligation to compensate for the damage caused thereby, insofar as such damage is not made good by restitution.
2. The compensation shall cover any financially assessable damage including loss of profits insofar as it is established.

Article 37 — Satisfaction
1. The State responsible for an internationally wrongful act is under an obligation to give satisfaction for the injury caused by that act insofar as it cannot be made good by restitution or compensation.
2. Satisfaction may consist in an acknowledgement of the breach, an expression of regret, a formal apology or another appropriate modality.
3. Satisfaction shall not be out of proportion to the injury and may not take a form humiliating to the responsible State.

Article 38 — Interest
1. Interest on any principal sum due under this chapter shall be payable when necessary in order to ensure full reparation. The interest rate and mode of calculation shall be set so as to achieve that result.
2. Interest runs from the date when the principal sum should have been paid until the date the obligation to pay is fulfilled.

Article 39 — Contribution to the injury
In the determination of reparation, account shall be taken of the contribution to the injury by wilful or negligent action or omission of the injured State or any person or entity in relation to whom reparation is sought.

CHAPTER III

Serious breaches of obligations under peremptory norms of general international law

Article 40 — Application of this chapter
1. This chapter applies to the international responsibility which is entailed by a serious breach by a State of an obligation arising under a peremptory norm of general international law.
2. A breach of such an obligation is serious if it involves a gross or systematic failure by the responsible State to fulfil the obligation.

Article 41 — Particular consequences of a serious breach of an obligation under this chapter
1. States shall cooperate to bring to an end through lawful means any serious breach within the meaning of article 40.
2. No State shall recognize as lawful a situation created by a serious breach within the meaning of article 40, nor render aid or assistance in maintaining that situation.
3. This article is without prejudice to the other consequences referred to in this Part and to such further consequences that a breach to which this chapter applies may entail under international law.

PART THREE

THE IMPLEMENTATION OF THE INTERNATIONAL RESPONSIBILITY OF A STATE

CHAPTER I

Invocation of the responsibility of a State

Article 42 — Invocation of responsibility by an injured State
A State is entitled as an injured State to invoke the responsibility of another State if the obligation breached is owed to:
(a) That State individually; or
(b) A group of States including that State, or the international community as a whole, and the breach of the obligation: (i) Specially affects that State; or (ii) Is of such a character as radically to change the position of all the other States to which the obligation is owed with respect to the further performance of the obligation.

Article 43 — Notice of claim by an injured State
1. An injured State which invokes the responsibility of another State shall give notice of its claim to that State.
2. The injured State may specify in particular:
(a) The conduct that the responsible State should take in order to cease the wrongful act, if it is continuing;
(b) What form reparation should take in accordance with the provisions of Part Two.

Article 44 — Admissibility of claims
The responsibility of a State may not be invoked if:
(a) The claim is not brought in accordance with any applicable rule relating to the nationality of claims;
(b) The claim is one to which the rule of exhaustion of local remedies applies and any available and effective local remedy has not been exhausted.

Article 45 — Loss of the right to invoke responsibility
The responsibility of a State may not be invoked if:
(a) The injured State has validly waived the claim;
(b) The injured State is to be considered as having, by reason of its conduct, validly acquiesced in the lapse of the claim.

Article 46 — Plurality of injured States
Where several States are injured by the same internationally wrongful act, each injured State may separately invoke the responsibility of the State which has committed the internationally wrongful act.

Article 47 — Plurality of responsible States
1. Where several States are responsible for the same internationally wrongful act, the responsibility of each State may be invoked in relation to that act.
2. Paragraph 1:
(a) Does not permit any injured State to recover, by way of compensation, more than the damage it has suffered;
(b) Is without prejudice to any right of recourse against the other responsible States.

Article 48 — Invocation of responsibility by a State other than an injured State
1. Any State other than an injured State is entitled to invoke the responsibility of another State in accordance with paragraph 2 if:
(a) The obligation breached is owed to a group of States including that State, and is established for the protection of a collective interest of the group; or
(b) The obligation breached is owed to the international community as a whole.
2. Any State entitled to invoke responsibility under paragraph 1 may claim from the responsible State:
(a) Cessation of the internationally wrongful act, and assurances and guarantees of non-repetition in accordance with article 30; and
(b) Performance of the obligation of reparation in accordance with the preceding articles, in the interest of the injured State or of the beneficiaries of the obligation breached.
3. The requirements for the invocation of responsibility by an injured State under articles 43, 44 and 45 apply to an invocation of responsibility by a State entitled to do so under paragraph 1.

CHAPTER II

Countermeasures

Article 49 — Object and limits of countermeasures
1. An injured State may only take countermeasures against a State which is responsible for an internationally wrongful act in order to induce that State to comply with its obligations under Part Two.
2. Countermeasures are limited to the non-performance for the time being of international obligations of the State taking the measures towards the responsible State.
3. Countermeasures shall, as far as possible, be taken in such a way as to permit the resumption of performance of the obligations in question.

Article 50 — Obligations not affected by countermeasures
1. Countermeasures shall not affect:
(a) The obligation to refrain from the threat or use of force as embodied in the Charter of the United Nations;
(b) Obligations for the protection of fundamental human rights;
(c) Obligations of a humanitarian character prohibiting reprisals;
(d) Other obligations under peremptory norms of general international law.
2. A State taking countermeasures is not relieved from fulfilling its obligations:
(a) Under any dispute settlement procedure applicable between it and the responsible State;
(b) To respect the inviolability of diplomatic or consular agents, premises, archives and documents.

Article 51 — Proportionality
Countermeasures must be commensurate with the injury suffered, taking into account the gravity of the internationally wrongful act and the rights in question.

Article 52 — Conditions relating to resort to countermeasures
1. Before taking countermeasures, an injured State shall:
(a) Call on the responsible State, in accordance with article 43, to fulfil its obligations under Part Two;
(b) Notify the responsible State of any decision to take countermeasures and offer to negotiate with that State.
2. Notwithstanding paragraph 1(b), the injured State may take such urgent countermeasures as are necessary to preserve its rights.
3. Countermeasures may not be taken, and if already taken must be suspended without undue delay if:
(a) The internationally wrongful act has ceased; and
(b) The dispute is pending before a court or tribunal which has the authority to make decisions binding on the parties.
4. Paragraph 3 does not apply if the responsible State fails to implement the dispute settlement procedures in good faith.

Article 53 — Termination of countermeasures
Countermeasures shall be terminated as soon as the responsible State has complied with its obligations under Part Two in relation to the internationally wrongful act.

Article 54 — Measures taken by States other than an injured State
This chapter does not prejudice the right of any State, entitled under article 48, paragraph 1 to invoke the responsibility of another State, to take lawful measures against that State to ensure cessation of the breach and reparation in the interest of the injured State or of the beneficiaries of the obligation breached.

C. Part Four — General provisions (Articles 55–59, official text)

Article 55 — Lex specialis. These articles do not apply where and to the extent that the conditions for the existence of an internationally wrongful act or the content or implementation of the international responsibility of a State are governed by special rules of international law.

Article 56 — Questions of State responsibility not regulated by these articles. The applicable rules of international law continue to govern questions concerning the responsibility of a State for an internationally wrongful act to the extent that they are not regulated by these articles.

Article 57 — Responsibility of an international organization. These articles are without prejudice to any question of the responsibility under international law of an international organization, or of any State for the conduct of an international organization.

Article 58 — Individual responsibility. These articles are without prejudice to any question of the individual responsibility under international law of any person acting on behalf of a State.

Article 59 — Charter of the United Nations. These articles are without prejudice to the Charter of the United Nations.

D. The four general provisions explained

  • Article 55 — lex specialis. This is the article that reconciles the general law with the special regimes: the WTO covered agreements (with the DSU's compulsory jurisdiction and countermeasure regime), the European Convention on Human Rights (its own remedial machinery), the ICSID/BIT system, the law of diplomatic immunities (Tehran Hostages's "self-contained régime"), and the risk-liability conventions studied at §4.2.3. Where the special rule governs, the general Articles stand aside — not because they are inferior, but because the parties have provided otherwise. Compare Article 42 of the 2011 DARIO for international organisations.
  • Article 56. A savings clause: whatever the Articles do not regulate continues to be governed by general international law (for example the law of treaties, of State succession, of diplomatic protection, and the law of the use of force). It is the point at which State responsibility links back to the sources doctrine.
  • Article 57. The responsibility of international organisations lies outside the Articles — hence the separate ILC Draft Articles on the Responsibility of International Organizations (2011), and cases such as Reparation for Injuries (1949), Behrami (2007) and Nuhanović (2013), studied at §4.3(F).
  • Article 58. State responsibility and individual criminal responsibility are concurrent, not alternative: the same conduct may engage the State (in LaGrand, Iran, the United States), the organisation (Nuremberg, Tokyo), and the individual (the ICTY, ICTR, ICC, and the Pinochet proceedings). This is the article which answers the common examination objection that "the State cannot be responsible because the individual perpetrator is".
  • Article 59. The Charter prevails: obligations under Articles 25, 39 and 103 of the Charter, and the powers of the Security Council, are unaffected. The point was made in the Lockerbie provisional measures orders (1992), where the Court held that prima facie Libya's rights under the Montreal Convention could not have been violated by Security Council resolutions adopted under Chapter VII.

E. How the ICJ has used the Articles — an examination-ready list

Table 4.15 · The Articles in the case law (from the prescribed materials)
ArticleCaseUse
Arts. 1, 2, 4–7Tehran Hostages (1980); LaGrand (2001)Basis of responsibility; organs; federal and state organs; "act of State".
Arts. 8, 11Nicaragua (1986), ¶¶115–116; Tehran Hostages, ¶74Effective control; acknowledgment and adoption.
Art. 20Armed Activities (DRC v. Uganda) (2005), ¶¶42–54Consent limited in scope; exceeded by the intervening State.
Art. 21Nicaragua; Oil Platforms (2003); Armed Activities; Wall AO (2004)Self-defence: armed attack, request, necessity, proportionality, report.
Art. 22 with 49–54Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros (1997), ¶¶82–87Countermeasures must be proportionate, reversible and preceded by negotiation.
Art. 25Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros, ¶¶49–58; Wall AO, ¶140Necessity: essential interest, grave and imminent peril, "only way", not self-judged.
Art. 31Chorzów Factory (1928) as codified; DRC v. Uganda (2022), ¶69Full reparation for injury caused; expressly "reflects customary international law".
Art. 34DRC v. Uganda (2022), ¶101; Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (2018), ¶31Forms of reparation; compensation where restitution is impossible.
Art. 35Chorzów Factory; Temple of Preah Vihear (1962); Rainbow Warrior (1990)Restitution in kind; material impossibility; disproportionate burden.
Art. 36Corfu Channel (1949); DRC v. Uganda (2022); Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (2018)"Financially assessable damage"; environmental damage; global sums.
Art. 37Corfu Channel; Rainbow Warrior; Genocide (2007); DRC v. Uganda (2022), ¶368Satisfaction; the declaration as the usual remedy; proportionality; no humiliation.
