Art. 39Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros (both parties' contribution); general practiceContribution of the injured State to the injury reduces reparation.
Arts. 40–41General practice after Iraq's invasion of Kuwait (1990); non-recognition of annexationSerious breaches of peremptory norms and the duty of non-recognition.
Art. 44(b)Interhandel (1959); ELSI (1989); Diallo (2007/2010)Exhaustion of local remedies; see §4.5 below.
Art. 48Barcelona Traction (1970), ¶33; Gambia v. Myanmar (2019/2020)Invocation by a State other than the injured State; erga omnes.
Art. 55Tehran Hostages (the diplomatic "self-contained régime"); WTO and ECHR systemsLex specialis.
Art. 59Lockerbie (1992); Charter Article 103The Charter prevails.
Examination box — how to use the full text in an answer

Never paraphrase where quotation is available. A DU answer earns marks for reproducing, accurately and briefly, the operative words: Article 2 ("action or omission … attributable … breach of an international obligation"); Article 4 ("whether the organ exercises legislative, executive, judicial or any other functions … whatever its character as an organ of the central government or of a territorial unit of the State"); Article 7 ("even if it exceeds its authority or contravenes instructions"); Article 8 ("instructions … direction or control"); Article 11 ("acknowledges and adopts … as its own"); Article 25 ("only way … essential interest … grave and imminent peril"); Article 31 ("full reparation for the injury caused"); Article 34 ("restitution, compensation and satisfaction, either singly or in combination"); Article 35 ("materially impossible … burden out of all proportion"); Article 36 ("financially assessable damage including loss of profits"); Article 37 ("acknowledgement … expression of regret … formal apology"); Article 41 ("No State shall recognize as lawful a situation created by a serious breach"); Article 44(b) ("available and effective local remedy"); Article 55 (lex specialis). Quote two or three per answer, attribute them to the Articles, and add the case which applies them.

If asked "Is the ILC Draft Code binding?" Answer: no — it is a codification annexed to a General Assembly resolution (56/83 of 12 December 2001) which merely "took note" of it; but it is the most authoritative statement of customary international law, applied by the ICJ as such (Article 31 and 34 in DRC v. Uganda, 2022), and scholarship, arbitral tribunals and national courts treat it as the standard reference. Then add Article 55 and the point that the Articles are themselves displaced by special regimes — which is the best evidence that they are a general law, not a treaty charter.

↑ top

Supporting Authorities — Digests of the Other Cases You Must Be Able to Cite

The seven prescribed cases (23–29) are digested in full above. The cases below are the supporting authorities which the case material, the doctrinal chapters and the past papers expect a candidate to be able to name, date and use for a single proposition. Each digest gives the citation, the facts in one or two sentences, and the principle for which the case is cited in Topic 4.

A. Foundations of responsibility

Table 4.16 · Foundational cases
CaseFactsPrinciple for Topic 4
Factory at Chorzów (Germany v. Poland), Jurisdiction 1927, P.C.I.J. Series A No. 9; Merits 1928, Series A No. 17Germany claimed against Poland for the expropriation of a nitrate factory in Upper Silesia in breach of the 1922 Geneva Convention."It is a general conception of law that every violation of an engagement involves an obligation to make reparation" (1927, p. 21); the classic statement of full reparation — "wipe out all the consequences of the illegal act and re-establish the situation which would, in all probability, have existed if that act had not been committed" (1928, p. 47). The origin of Articles 31, 34 and 35.
Mavrommatis Palestine Concessions (Greece v. Great Britain), P.C.I.J. 1924, Series A No. 2Greece espoused the claim of a Greek national whose Jerusalem concessions had been refused by the British mandatory authorities."By taking up the case of one of its subjects and by resorting to diplomatic action or international judicial proceedings on his behalf, a State is in reality asserting its own rights" (¶89) — the foundation of diplomatic protection, and the reason a State may invoke responsibility for injury to a national without the national being a party.
Reparation for Injuries Suffered in the Service of the United Nations, Advisory Opinion, ICJ 1949, p. 174The assassination of Count Folke Bernadotte; the question whether the UN could claim reparation from Israel for injury to its agent.The UN possesses international legal personality and functional protection of its agents; the case is the starting point for the responsibility of international organizations (and hence for Article 57 ILC and the DARIO 2011).
South West Africa Cases (Ethiopia v. South Africa; Liberia v. South Africa), Second Phase, ICJ 1966, p. 6Ethiopia and Liberia challenged South Africa's conduct of the Mandate over South West Africa.The claim failed for want of a "legal right or interest" in the applicants — the jurisprudential ancestor of the modern law of standing (Articles 42 and 48 ILC) and of the damage theory debate (§4.2.1).

B. Attribution — organs, officials, private persons

Table 4.17 · Attribution cases
CaseFactsPrinciple
Caire Claim (France v. Mexico), 1929, 5 UNRIAA 516Mexican soldiers took a French national from his home and killed him; the claim was for the soldiers' conduct.A State is responsible for the acts of its officials performed "in their official character" or by the use of powers appropriate to that character, even if they exceeded their authority or disobeyed instructions — the fountainhead of Article 7.
Youmans Claim (United States v. Mexico), 1926, 4 UNRIAA 110A mob attacked American nationals at Angangueo; Mexican troops were involved and the local authorities gave no protection.Responsibility both for the involvement of officials (attribution) and for the failure of the authorities to protect aliens — the ancestor of the "due diligence" branch of responsibility illustrated by Corfu Channel and Tehran Hostages.
Mallén Claim (Mexico v. United States), 1927, 4 UNRIAA 173A Mexican consul in Texas assaulted a Mexican national.A State is responsible for the wrongful acts of its consuls; the character of the office, not the nationality of the victim, determines attribution.
Bolívar Railway Company Claim (Great Britain v. Venezuela), 1903, 9 UNRIAA 445Damage to the railway during the operations of the successful revolutionary movement of 1899–1900 which then became the government of Venezuela.Article 10(1): the conduct of an insurrectional movement which becomes the government is attributable to the State — retrospectively and in full.
Home Missionary Society Claim (United States v. Great Britain), 1920, 6 UNRIAA 42The 1898 rebellion in Sierra Leone (the "Hut Tax War") destroyed the claimant's property; the British authorities suppressed it.The conduct of an unsuccessful insurrection is not attributable to the State; nor was Great Britain in fault for failing to prevent the rebellion. The companion rule to Bolívar Railway.
Pinson Claim (France v. Mexico), 1928, 5 UNRIAA 327Claim for damage caused by a revolutionary movement which did not succeed in establishing a government.Article 10 again: no attribution where the movement fails — although the State remains responsible for its own conduct under Articles 4–9.
Prosecutor v. Tadić, ICTY Appeals Chamber, 1999, 38 ILM 1518Appeal against conviction; the question whether the Bosnian Serb forces were acting on behalf of the FRY for the purpose of classifying the conflict.The "overall control" test: control going beyond financing and equipping, involving participation in planning and supervision of military operations, suffices for the classification of an armed conflict — but the ICJ has held that it is "unsuitable" for State responsibility (Genocide, 2007).
Application of the Genocide Convention (Bosnia and Herzegovina v. Serbia and Montenegro), ICJ 2007, p. 43Claims arising from the war in Bosnia; Srebrenica.(i) "Effective control" reaffirmed for Article 8 and "overall control" rejected; (ii) the duty to prevent genocide is breached only if the State "manifestly failed to take all measures to prevent genocide which were within its power"; (iii) Article 16 (aiding and assisting) considered but not established; (iv) satisfaction by declaration is enough for the failure to prevent.
Behrami and Behrami v. France; Saramati v. France, Germany and Norway, ECtHR Grand Chamber, 2007Conduct of KFOR and UNMIK personnel in Kosovo.Attribution to the United Nations, which retained "ultimate authority and control"; the troop-contributing States were not responsible — a decision criticised for treating attribution as "either/or".
Nuhanović v. The Netherlands, Dutch Supreme Court, 6 September 2013The eviction of the family of a Dutchbat interpreter from the compound at Srebrenica in July 1995.Dual attribution: the Netherlands had "effective control" over the specific conduct and was responsible for it, notwithstanding that the Dutchbat operation was mandated by the United Nations.
Loizidou v. Turkey, ECtHR 1996; Cyprus v. Turkey, ECtHR 2001; Ilaşcu v. Moldova and Russia, ECtHR 2004Human rights applications concerning northern Cyprus and Transdniestria.Attribution of the acts of an unrecognised entity to the State exercising "effective overall control" over the area — the regional counterpart of Articles 4 and 8 (substance over form).
Kotov v. Russia, ECtHR Grand Chamber, 2011A private company administered the affairs of a bankrupt enterprise in Russia.An illustration of the limits of Article 5: the company did not exercise governmental authority, so its conduct was not attributable to Russia.

C. Breach, causation, defences and use of force

Table 4.18 · Defences and related cases
CaseFactsPrinciple
Naulilaa Incident (Portugal v. Germany), 1928, 2 UNRIAA 1013German reprisals against Portuguese posts in Angola after the killing of German officials — an incident not attributable to Portugal.The classical conditions of reprisals/countermeasures: a prior wrongful act, a prior demand for redress, and proportionality. The ancestor of Articles 49–52.
Air Services Agreement Arbitration (United States v. France), 1978, 54 ILR 303Mutual suspension of airline routes.A proportionate countermeasure, taken after negotiation failed, is lawful; the test of proportionality looks to the aim and effect of the measure. The modern companion to Naulilaa.
Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros Project (Hungary/Slovakia), ICJ 1997, p. 7Hungary's suspension and abandonment of works on the Danube; Slovakia's unilateral "Variant C" diversion.Necessity: strictly defined cumulative conditions; the State is not the sole judge; necessity does not terminate a treaty (¶¶49–58). Countermeasures: Czechoslovakia's diversion was not a lawful countermeasure because it was not proportionate and not reversible (¶¶82–87). Restitution requires negotiation to restore the joint régime. The Court relied on the ILC's 1996 draft articles.
Oil Platforms (Iran v. United States), ICJ 2003, p. 161United States attacks on Iranian oil platforms, justified as self-defence against Iranian attacks on shipping.Self-defence requires an armed attack and the response must be necessary and proportionate; the evidence of Iranian responsibility was insufficient. The modern authority on the burden of proof in self-defence.
Legal Consequences of the Construction of a Wall in the Occupied Palestinian Territory, Advisory Opinion, ICJ 2004, p. 136The question whether the wall and its régime were contrary to international law.Self-defence under Article 51 has no application to a threat originating within the State's own territory (¶139); necessity (Article 25) could not be relied upon (¶140); the breach of erga omnes obligations entails consequences for all States (¶¶154–159).
Russian Indemnity (Russia v. Turkey), PCA 1912, 11 UNRIAA 421Turkey's delay in paying an indemnity to Russia; plea of financial impossibility.Pecuniary difficulty is not force majeure or necessity; the classic rejection of "financial incapacity" as a defence.
Rainbow Warrior (New Zealand v. France), 1990, 82 ILR 499The bombing of the Rainbow Warrior by French agents in Auckland harbour (1985) and the failure to keep the two officers on Hao island.Force majeure/necessity rejected; breach of an obligation entails reparation in adequate form; satisfaction (a formal apology) plus a monetary payment constituted the appropriate reparation; the absence of a contractual or tortious division in international responsibility.
Armed Activities on the Territory of the Congo (DRC v. Uganda), ICJ 2005, p. 168Ugandan military intervention and occupation of Ituri; counter-claims concerning the Kinshasa embassy.Consent (Art. 20) limited in scope and exceeded; self-defence (Art. 21) rejected for want of an armed attack and of proportionality; the law of occupation imposes duties enforceable by reparation; the DRC liable for the attack on Uganda's embassy.
M.C. Mehta v. Union of India, (1987) 1 SCC 395 (India)Oleum gas leak, Delhi.An enterprise engaged in a hazardous activity owes "absolute and non-delegable" liability to the community, not subject to exceptions like Act of God — the Indian expression of the risk theory and the precursor of the Public Liability Insurance Act, 1991.

D. Reparation

Table 4.19 · Reparation cases
CaseFactsPrinciple
Corfu Channel (United Kingdom v. Albania), ICJ 1949, p. 4 and (Compensation) 1949, p. 244The minefield incident of 22 October 1946 and the United Kingdom's minesweeping of 12–13 November 1946.Compensation of £843,947 for ships and lives; the declaration of the Court was "in itself appropriate satisfaction" for the violation of Albania's sovereignty; the two-stage reparations procedure.
Lusitania (United States v. Germany), 1923, 7 UNRIAA 32The sinking of the Lusitania in 1915; claims for damages, including punitive damages.No punitive or exemplary damages in international law: reparation is compensatory. Still the leading authority for the point (and cited as such in the Argentinian awards and in DRC v. Uganda, 2022, ¶102).
I'm Alone (Canada v. United States), 1935, 3 UNRIAA 1609The sinking of a Canadian rum-running vessel on the high seas by a United States coastguard vessel.The sinking was unlawful; the Commission recommended US$25,000 as a material indemnity and a formal acknowledgment of the illegality. A classic mix of compensation and satisfaction, and a rejection of punitive damages.
Temple of Preah Vihear (Cambodia v. Thailand), ICJ 1962, p. 6Sovereignty over the temple of Preah Vihear and the surrounding area.Thailand ordered to withdraw its forces and to return the sculptures removed from the temple — restitution in kind.
Trail Smelter Arbitration (United States v. Canada), 1938 and 1941, 3 UNRIAA 1905Sulphur dioxide fumes from a smelter in British Columbia damaging farms and timber in Washington State."No State has the right to use or permit the use of its territory in such a manner as to cause injury by fumes … to the territory of another … when the case is of serious consequence and the injury is established by clear and convincing evidence." The first international award of compensation for transboundary environmental damage; the ancestor of the prevention/allocation-of-loss regime discussed at §4.2.3.
Certain Activities Carried Out by Nicaragua in the Border Area (Costa Rica v. Nicaragua), ICJ 2015, p. 665; Compensation, ICJ 2018The excavation of the caños in the northern part of Isla Portillos and the damage to the Ramsar wetland.Environmental damage, including the impairment of environmental goods and services, is compensable; the ecosystem-wide valuation; approximation permitted; interest only when necessary; total US$378,890.59. (Case 28 above.)
Ahmadou Sadio Diallo (Guinea v. DRC), ICJ 2007 (Preliminary Objections), 2010 (Merits), 2012 (Compensation)The detention, expulsion and expropriation concerning a Guinean national in Kinshasa.Causation must be "sufficiently direct and certain" (¶14 of the Compensation judgment); reparation for individuals assessed case by case; the modern application of the exhaustion of local remedies rule.
Armed Activities (DRC v. Uganda), ICJ 2022 (Reparation)See Case 29 above.Global sums (US$225m + 40m + 60m = US$325m), five instalments, 6% post-judgment interest, capacity to pay, reparation "compensatory not punitive", satisfaction by declaration, macro-economic damage refused.
Eritrea–Ethiopia Claims Commission, Final Awards, 17 August 2009Claims arising out of the 1998–2000 war, including the forcible expulsion of civilians on both sides.The Commission rejected claims for moral damage to the State while awarding compensation for unlawful expulsion, loss of life and property; heavily relied on by the ICJ in DRC v. Uganda (2022) as regards both causation and satisfaction.
United Nations Compensation Commission, 1991–2005 (Iraq's invasion of Kuwait)Mass claims for loss and damage caused by Iraq's unlawful invasion and occupation of Kuwait.The largest reparation exercise in history (about US$52 billion awarded); the Security Council's framework (resolution 687 of 1991) is itself an example of institutionalised reparation, and of the operation of the lex specialis preserved by Article 55.

E. Admissibility — nationality and exhaustion of local remedies

Table 4.20 · Admissibility cases
CaseFactsPrinciple
Ambatielos Arbitration (Greece v. United Kingdom), 1956, 12 UNRIAA 83A Greek shipowner's claim arising out of a contract with the British Government."Local remedies" means not only recourse to courts and tribunals but "the whole system of legal protection" including the procedural facilities available to litigants — so failure to raise the substance in the local proceedings defeats the claim.
Finnish Ships Arbitration (Finland v. Great Britain), 1934, 3 UNRIAA 1479Claims of Finnish shipowners after the First World War.Only effective remedies need be exhausted; the leading case on the burden of showing that a remedy was available and effective — the source of the "available and effective local remedy" formula of Article 44(b).
El Oro Mining and Railway Co. (Great Britain v. Mexico), 1931, 5 UNRIAA 191A long-pending claim in the Mexican courts.Delay and the practical state of the local judicial system may dispense with the requirement — the ancestor of the "undue delay" exception.
Robert E. Brown Claim (United States v. Great Britain), 1923, 6 UNRIAA 120Proceedings in South Africa concerning mining concessions.Remedies need not be exhausted where it is evident "that there is no justice to exhaust" — the futility exception.
Interhandel Case (Switzerland v. United States), Preliminary Objections, ICJ 1959, p. 6The Swiss corporation's assets vested by the United States as enemy property; ten years of litigation still pending.The claim was inadmissible because local remedies had not been exhausted; the case also introduced the "preponderance of interests" analysis for mixed claims.
Elettronica Sicula S.p.A. (ELSI) (United States v. Italy), ICJ 1989, p. 15The requisition by Italy of a plant owned by a United States corporation.The exhaustion rule is not a "formal" requirement; the Italian courts' decisions did not amount to a denial of justice, but the requisition was an arbitrary measure in breach of the FCN Treaty.
Neer Claim (United States v. Mexico), 1926, 4 UNRIAA 60The killing of a United States national in Mexico and the alleged failure of the authorities to investigate.The classical minimum standard for denial of justice: "an outrage … bad faith … wilful neglect of duty, or … an insufficiency of governmental action so far short of international standards that every reasonable and impartial man would readily recognize its insufficiency."
Nottebohm Case (Liechtenstein v. Guatemala), Second Phase, ICJ 1955, p. 4A naturalised Liechtenstein national of German origin, long resident in Guatemala.Nationality of claims requires a genuine link between the individual and the claimant State (¶¶95–96) — the companion of the exhaustion rule in Article 44.
North American Dredging Company of Texas (United States v. Mexico), 1926, 4 UNRIAA 26A contract containing a Calvo clause by which the claimant agreed to be treated as a Mexican and not to seek diplomatic protection.The Calvo clause binds the individual but cannot deprive the State of its right of protection where international law itself is violated — the reason the Calvo doctrine is not part of international law.
Kulbhushan Jadhav (India v. Pakistan), ICJ 2019An Indian national sentenced to death in Pakistan without consular notification.LaGrand/Avena applied: Pakistan in breach of Article 36 VCCR; ordered to provide consular access and "effective review and reconsideration" of the conviction and sentence, and to suspend the death sentence pending that review. The leading Indian-party case on consular protection and reparation.
The "Enrica Lexie" Incident (Italy v. India), PCA/UNCLOS Annex VII, Award 21 May 2020Italian marines shot two Indian fishermen off Kerala in 2012.India's right to navigate violated; Italy to pay ₹10 crore in compensation (paid 2021) — a modern Article 36 award involving India, and an illustration of a "direct" claim requiring no exhaustion of local remedies.
Examination box — how to deploy supporting authorities

Examiners reward precision, not accumulation. For each proposition, name one case and one reason: attribution to organs → Caire; ultra vires → Article 7 with Caire; private persons controlled → Nicaragua ¶115 with Tadić and Genocide (2007); adoption → Tehran ¶74; insurrection → Bolívar Railway and Home Missionary Society; consent → DRC v. Uganda (2005); self-defence → Nicaragua, Oil Platforms, Wall; countermeasures → Naulilaa, Air Services, Gabčíkovo; necessity → Gabčíkovo ¶¶49–58; force majeure → Russian Indemnity; distress → Torrey Canyon; full reparation → Chorzów; restitution → Temple of Preah Vihear; compensation → Corfu Channel (money) and DRC v. Uganda (global sums); no punitive damages → Lusitania; satisfaction → Corfu Channel (declaration) and Rainbow Warrior (apology); assurance of non-repetition → LaGrand; environment → Trail Smelter and Costa Rica v. Nicaragua; exhaustion → Ambatielos, Finnish Ships, Interhandel, ELSI; denial of justice → Neer; nationality of claims → Nottebohm and Barcelona Traction. Add the Indian cases (M.C. Mehta, Jadhav, Enrica Lexie) where a modern or local illustration will earn credit.

★ Previous Years' Questions on State Responsibility — with Model Answers

Every past-paper question on State Responsibility from the DU LL.B. papers available in the project materials is reproduced below with a model answer. The questions fall into four recurring types: (i) the "constituent elements" question (2011, 2012, 2013); (ii) the "forms of reparation with the ILC Draft Code" question (2011, 2012, 2013, 2015); (iii) the "basis of responsibility with the Nicaragua case" question (2015); and (iv) the short note on the exhaustion of local remedies (2011, 2013) — now supplemented by the 2025 paper's two problem questions on attribution (Tarkin/Yoda) and diplomatic protection (UFO/Zedex). Where two questions are substantially the same, the full model answer is given once and the variations are noted, because repeating identical answers wastes examination time.

A. The 2025 Paper (DU LL.B., Public International Law LB-205, May–June 2025)

Question 2 (20 marks) — the Tarkin / Yoda problem

"State of Tarkin has passed a national legislation, which authorizes President of Tarkin to give monetary and other support to a rebel guerilla group in neighbouring country Yoda. The group is using force to overthrow the existing government of Yoda among other activities like smuggling. Tarkin and Yoda are members of the U.N. and other treaties banning intervention in other States. Yoda brought a suit before the International Court of Justice (ICJ) against Tarkin on the ground that Tarkin was responsible for illegal military and paramilitary activities in and against Yoda. Decide the responsibility of Tarkin, if any, under International Law."

Model answer — structure and content

1. The issue. Tarkin's legislation authorises its President to finance and support a guerrilla group using force to overthrow the government of Yoda. The questions are: (i) are the guerrilla group's acts attributable to Tarkin? (ii) If not, is Tarkin nevertheless responsible for its own conduct in supporting the group? (iii) Do Tarkin's acts breach the prohibition of the use of force, the principle of non-intervention, and the duty not to assist a rebellion?

2. The applicable law. Article 2 of the ILC Articles: an internationally wrongful act requires (a) conduct attributable to the State and (b) a breach of an international obligation. The prohibition of the use of force and the principle of non-intervention are rules of customary international law, declared in the Declaration on Friendly Relations (GA resolution 2625 (XXV), 1970), which expressly prohibits a State from organising, instigating, assisting or participating in acts of civil strife or terrorist acts in another State, and from acquiescing in organised activities within its territory directed towards the commission of such acts. Article 2(4) of the Charter and the Nicaragua case (ICJ Rep. 1986, p. 14) supply the authority.

3. Attribution — are the guerrillas Tarkin's agents? Apply Article 8 and the Nicaragua test. The guerrillas are not organs of Tarkin (Article 4) and are not empowered to exercise governmental authority (Article 5). Attribution therefore requires that they acted "on the instructions of, or under the direction or control of" Tarkin in carrying out the conduct. The Court in Nicaragua held (¶115) that United States participation "even if preponderant or decisive, in the financing, organizing, training, supplying and equipping of the contras, … and the planning of the whole of its operation, is still insufficient in itself … to attribute to the United States the acts committed by the contras", and that what must be proved is that the State "had effective control of the military or paramilitary operations in the course of which the alleged violations were committed". On the facts stated, Tarkin finances and supports the group but there is nothing to show that it directs or controls the specific operations (the overthrow attempts, the smuggling). Unless that effective control is proved, the guerrillas' criminal acts — including their killings and smuggling — are not attributable to Tarkin. Note the contrast with Tehran Hostages (1980, ¶74): there the militants became agents of the State because the State approved and adopted their conduct as its own (Article 11). Financing and permitting is not adopting.

4. Tarkin's own responsibility for its own conduct (the decisive point). Nicaragua ¶116 is the key: "the United States is not responsible for the acts of the contras, but for its own conduct vis-à-vis Nicaragua, including conduct related to the acts of the contras." Tarkin's legislation, its President's authorisation, and the actual provision of money and material to an armed group seeking to overthrow the government of Yoda are Tarkin's own acts. They attract responsibility on two independent grounds:

  • Non-intervention. In Nicaragua the Court held that the support given by the United States to the military and paramilitary activities of the contras — "by financial support, training, supply of weapons, intelligence and logistic support" — "constitutes a clear breach of the principle of non-intervention" (¶242). Tarkin's conduct is squarely within that holding. The qualification in the same paragraph is important and cuts against Tarkin: only "strictly humanitarian aid" is protected; aid to a group using armed force to overthrow the government is not humanitarian.
  • Use of force. If the support involves a threat or use of force — arming a group which then attacks Yoda's forces or infrastructure — it violates the prohibition of the use of force (Article 2(4) of the Charter and custom: Nicaragua, ¶¶227–228, 238). Even if the aid is not itself an armed attack, "the sending by or on behalf of a State of armed bands ... which carry out acts of armed force of such gravity as to amount to ... an actual armed attack" is itself unlawful (GA resolution 3314, the Definition of Aggression, Article 3(g); Nicaragua, ¶195).
  • Aid and assistance in the act of another (Article 16 ILC) and complicity. Where Tarkin's assistance is given "with knowledge of the circumstances" of the guerrilla group's wrongful acts and those acts would be wrongful if done by Tarkin, Tarkin is responsible for aiding and assisting. The requirement of knowledge is the point on which the Genocide case (2007) turned on the facts.

5. Defences available to Tarkin, and why they fail. Consent (Article 20) is absent: the Government of Yoda has not consented; on the contrary, the group seeks to overthrow it. Self-defence (Article 21) is unavailable: there is no armed attack on Tarkin. Countermeasures (Article 22) require a prior wrongful act by Yoda. And no circumstance precluding wrongfulness can justify a breach of the prohibition of the use of force in any event (Article 26 read with Article 50(1)(a)).

6. The domestic-legislation point. The fact that the support is authorised by Tarkin's own statute is irrelevant: Article 3 provides that the characterisation of an act as internationally wrongful "is governed by international law" and "is not affected by the characterisation of the same act as lawful by internal law"; Article 32 adds that a responsible State may not rely on its internal law to justify non-compliance. The legislation is, on the contrary, itself an act of the State attributable under Article 4 — the conduct of the legislative organ — and it is evidence of a pattern of conduct, and of the absence of any diligence to prevent the group's activities.

7. Consequences. Tarkin is under an obligation of cessation (Article 30(a)) — to stop the financing and support, and indeed to suppress the activities organised within its territory, as the Declaration on Friendly Relations requires; to give assurances and guarantees of non-repetition (Article 30(b)); and to make full reparation for the injury caused to Yoda by its own wrongful conduct (Article 31) — restitution where possible, compensation for the material damage caused by the support attributable to its own acts, and satisfaction for the injury to Yoda's sovereignty. Because the breaches are continuing, the claim is ripe and the position of the Court (as in Tehran, Nicaragua and DRC v. Uganda) is to declare responsibility, order cessation, and reserve the assessment of reparation if the parties do not agree.

8. Conclusion. Tarkin is not responsible for the guerrilla group's acts unless effective control (Article 8) or adoption (Article 11) is proved; but it is responsible, and clearly so, for its own conduct of financing, equipping and encouraging an armed rebellion against Yoda, which violates the principle of non-intervention and, if it involves force, the prohibition of the use of force, and which engages the consequences in Articles 30 and 31. Nicaragua is the authority throughout, and the answer should say so sentence by sentence.

Question 7 (20 marks) — the UFO / Zedex problem

"State 'UFO' has an embassy premise in Greedo, capital city of State of Zedex. The embassy of UFO was captured by a certain group of people of Zedex after a provocative speech given by a local leader. Many diplomats and other attaché of UFO were taken hostages. State Zedex failed to provide any assistance despite several requests from UFO. All efforts to arrange meetings were also forbidden by the local leaders. UFO brought a claim before the International Court of Justice, claiming that the attack was carried out by 'agents' of Zedex and Zedex is responsible for breach of the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961. In light of the privileges and immunities of diplomats under the said Convention and previously decided cases discuss the responsibility of Zedex."

Model answer — structure and content

1. The issue. Two distinct questions arise on these facts, and the examiner is testing whether the candidate can separate them: (i) was the group's attack on the embassy attributable to Zedex (i.e. were they its "agents"?), and (ii) irrespective of attribution, is Zedex responsible for its own failure to protect the embassy and its staff? The governing case is United States Diplomatic and Consular Staff in Tehran (ICJ Rep. 1980, p. 3) — which, as it happens, is printed twice in the course material (as case 25 for Topic 4 and as a diplomatic-immunities case in Topic 7).

2. The obligations. Under the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961: Article 22(1) — the premises of the mission are inviolable and the agents of the receiving State "shall not enter them, except with the consent of the head of the mission"; Article 22(2) — "the receiving State is under a special duty to take all appropriate steps to protect the premises of the mission against any intrusion or damage and to prevent any disturbance of the peace of the mission or impairment of its dignity"; Article 24 — the archives and documents are inviolable; Articles 25, 26 and 27 — full facilities, freedom of movement and communication; Article 29 — the person of a diplomatic agent is inviolable and "he shall be treated with due respect"; Article 31 — immunity from criminal jurisdiction; Articles 44 and 45 — the protecting Power and the duty to respect and protect the premises even in case of armed conflict or a breach of relations. There are also obligations under general international law and, on these facts, the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of Crimes against Internationally Protected Persons, 1973.

3. First phase — the attack itself: attribution. In Tehran the Court held that the conduct of the militants in mounting the attack "cannot … be regarded as imputable to that State on that basis" merely because they were private persons, and that it would be imputable only "if it were established that, in fact, on the occasion in question the militants acted on behalf of the State, having been charged by some competent organ of the Iranian State to carry out a specific operation" (¶58). The Court refused to treat the Ayatollah's general denunciations, or the congratulations which followed the event, as authorisation. The same test applies here (Article 8 ILC): the "provocative speech given by a local leader" is not an instruction to seize the embassy; unless it is proved that Zedex directed or controlled the specific operation — or that a competent organ charged the group with it — the acts of the group are not attributable to Zedex on the basis of agency. Contrast Nicaragua, where even massive financing and organising of a paramilitary force was held insufficient without "effective control" of the specific operations (¶115).

4. But there is a second, independent head of responsibility: the failure to protect. This is where Tehran is decisive and where Zedex's case is hopeless. The Court held that the absence of attribution "does not mean that Iran is, in consequence, free of any responsibility in regard to those attacks; for its own conduct was in conflict with its international obligations" (¶61). Iran had failed "altogether to take any 'appropriate steps' to protect the premises, staff and archives", and the failure was "due to more than mere negligence or lack of appropriate means" (¶63) — as evidenced by the contrast with Iran's own conduct on earlier occasions when it acted promptly to protect the embassy. On the facts of this problem, Zedex (i) failed to provide any assistance "despite several requests" — the precise failing identified in ¶63; (ii) permitted the local leadership to forbid meetings, which shows the absence of any effort to secure the release or to restore the premises; and (iii) failed to do anything to enforce its own responsibility as the receiving State. Zedex therefore breached Article 22(2) and Articles 24–29 of the VCDR, and, if the hostages include consular officers or the consular premises are affected, the corresponding provisions of the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963.

5. The second phase — adoption. Even if the initial seizure is not attributable, the position changes if Zedex approves, endorses or decides to perpetuate it. In Tehran the Court held that "the approval given to these facts by the Ayatollah Khomeini and other organs of the Iranian State, and the decision to perpetuate them, translated continuing occupation of the Embassy and detention of the hostages into acts of that State. The militants, authors of the invasion and jailers of the hostages, had now become agents of the Iranian State" (¶74; now Article 11 ILC). On the facts as stated, no such endorsement appears, so this limb depends on evidence to be led: if Zedex's organs endorse the group or refuse to restore the situation, the group's conduct becomes the State's own. The candidate should say that this is the correct legal route by which the claim of "agency" can succeed — and only by this route.

6. Zedex's possible excuses, and why they fail. (i) The "self-contained régime" argument (that diplomatic law provides its own remedies — declaration of persona non grata, severance of relations: VCDR Articles 9, 44 and 45; Tehran, ¶86) does not help Zedex: it is the receiving State, and those remedies exist to protect it against the sending State, not to license its own failure to protect the mission. (ii) A plea of necessity (Article 25 ILC) fails: internal unrest and a local leader's obstruction are not circumstances that make protection "impossible", and the breach is of obligations of cardinal importance; Israel's failure in the Wall Opinion and Hungary's in Gabčíkovo are the authorities on how strictly Article 25 is construed. (iii) Zedex cannot point to any countermeasure or consent. (iv) Neither can it say that the group is "private": private status excludes attribution of the group's acts (Article 8) but not the State's own omission (Article 2 read with the primary rule).

7. Consequences. Zedex is under an obligation to cease the continuing breach, to secure the release of the hostages and restore the premises, archives and documents (Article 30(a); the Tehran dispositif, ¶95(3)), to ensure that no diplomat is subjected to judicial proceedings or required to be a witness (VCDR Article 31; Tehran ¶95(4)), to offer assurances and guarantees of non-repetition (Article 30(b)), and to make reparation (Articles 31, 34) — restitution by restoration of the premises; compensation for the injury to the staff and property; and, at the very least, satisfaction through an acknowledgment of the breach and a formal apology, to which the Court's own declaration of illegality may be added, as in Corfu Channel ("this declaration is in itself appropriate satisfaction"). Where the breaches are continuing, the "form and amount" of reparation may be reserved, as the Court did at ¶90 and ¶95(6) of Tehran.

8. Conclusion. Zedex is responsible to UFO: not (on the facts as pleaded) for the group's attack as such, because the group's conduct is not attributable to it without effective control or adoption — but for its own failure to take the "appropriate steps" required by Article 22(2) and Articles 24–29 VCDR, a failure which the Court in Tehran described as "more than mere negligence". If the facts show endorsement or perpetuation, the group's acts also become acts of Zedex under Article 11.

B. The 2011 to 2015 Papers

2015 Q.5(a) (10 marks) — "What is the basis of State responsibility? Explain how the principle of State responsibility was applied in Nicaragua case (Nicaragua v. USA, ICJ Rep. 1986 p. 14)."

(The second version of the 2015 paper prints the same question as: "What is the basis of state responsibility? Explain how the principle of state responsibility was applied in Nicaragua case.")

Model answer

Part I — the basis (5 marks). State responsibility is the legal consequence which international law attaches to an internationally wrongful act: as Article 1 of the ILC Articles puts it, "every internationally wrongful act of a State entails the international responsibility of that State". Its basis is to be found in four propositions. First, in the general principle recognised by the Permanent Court in Chorzów Factory (1928) that "every violation of an engagement involves an obligation to make reparation". Second, in the sovereign equality of States: because no State may sit in judgment on another and self-help is unlawful, the law itself must provide the consequence of a breach. Third, in the structure of the modern law: primary rules (what States must do) are distinguished from secondary rules (what happens when they do not) — the insight of Special Rapporteur Ago which shapes the 2001 Articles. Fourth, in Articles 1–3 of those Articles, which codify the rule, define the wrongful act by two elements (attribution and breach) and make clear that the characterisation of an act as wrongful is governed by international law, not by internal law. (If asked for "constituent elements", expand here: Article 2, and then the theories of damage, fault and absolute liability — see the 2011 answer below and §4.2 of these notes.)

Part II — the application in Nicaragua (5 marks). The case is used for six distinct applications of the principle:

  1. Attribution of the conduct of private persons. The contras were not organs of the United States (Article 4). Even the financing, organising, training, supplying and equipping of the force, and the planning of its operations, were "insufficient in itself … for the purpose of attributing to the United States the acts committed by the contras"; what had to be proved was "effective control of the military or paramilitary operations in the course of which the alleged violations were committed" (¶115) — the ancestor of Article 8. Tadić's "overall control" must be mentioned as the rival test, and the ICJ's refusal to follow it for State responsibility in the Genocide case (2007).
  2. Responsibility for the State's own conduct. "The United States is not responsible for the acts of the contras, but for its own conduct vis-à-vis Nicaragua, including conduct related to the acts of the contras" (¶116) — hence responsibility for financing, training and arming the contras, and for the acts of its own personnel.
  3. Direct acts of State organs. The laying of mines in Nicaraguan internal or territorial waters in early 1984 and certain attacks on Nicaraguan ports, oil installations and a naval base were acts of United States personnel; they violated the prohibition of the use of force (¶¶227, 238), and the overflights violated Nicaragua's territorial sovereignty (¶292(4)).
  4. Non-intervention. The support given to the contras constituted "a clear breach of the principle of non-intervention" (¶242), while strictly humanitarian aid to persons or forces in another country "cannot be regarded as unlawful intervention".
  5. Defences. The United States' plea of collective self-defence failed: there was no armed attack proved against El Salvador or the others; a request by the victim State and a declaration that it had been attacked are required (¶199); and the plea of self-defence could not justify a response which was not necessary and proportionate. See also Articles 20–27 of the ILC Articles.
  6. Consequences. Because the breaches were continuing, the Court did not award damages but declared that the United States was "under a duty immediately to cease and to refrain" from the unlawful acts (¶292(10)) and reserved the question of reparation — the same two-stage structure later used in Tehran Hostages, DRC v. Uganda and Costa Rica v. Nicaragua.

Note to the candidate. If the question adds "and how is responsibility established in the absence of a treaty on responsibility?", add that because the United States' Multilateral Treaty Reservation excluded the Charter, the Court decided the case on customary law — and used that occasion to state how custom is proved (¶¶183–188: practice and opinio juris; practice need not be perfectly consistent; GA resolution 2625 may evidence opinio juris). This is a favourite additional part of the question in DU papers.

2015 Q.5(b), 2013 Q.3(b), 2012 Q.5 (part), 2011 Q.5 (part) — "Explain the different forms of reparation by referring to the ILC Draft Code on Responsibility of States for Internationally Wrongful Acts, 2001" / "various forms of reparation for the breach of international obligation"
Model answer (the standard answer to be adapted to the marks available)

1. The principle. Begin with Chorzów Factory (1928): "it is a general conception of law that every violation of an engagement involves an obligation to make reparation" and "reparation must, as far as possible, wipe out all the consequences of the illegal act and re-establish the situation which would, in all probability, have existed if that act had not been committed". Then Article 31 of the Draft Code: the responsible State "is under an obligation to make full reparation for the injury caused", and "injury includes any damage, whether material or moral". Article 34 then breaks reparation into the forms: "restitution, compensation and satisfaction, either singly or in combination".

2. Restitution (Article 35) — the re-establishment of the situation existing before the wrongful act, unless it is (a) materially impossible or (b) involves a burden out of all proportion to the benefit of restitution as against compensation. Explain the two kinds — restitution in kind (return of property, territory, archives; release of persons) and juridical restitution (revocation of an unlawful law, decree or judgment). Authorities: Chorzów Factory (restitution in kind as the primary remedy); Temple of Preah Vihear (1962) (return of the sculptures); Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros (1997) (restoration of the joint régime by negotiation); Rainbow Warrior (1990) (restitution impossible, hence satisfaction).

3. Compensation or indemnity (Article 36) — the payment of a sum for the damage caused "insofar as it is not made good by restitution", covering "any financially assessable damage including loss of profits insofar as it is established". Explain damnum emergens and lucrum cessans; the inclusion of moral damage insofar as financially assessable; the exclusion of punitive damages (Lusitania, 1923 — no exemplary damages; the same rule restated in DRC v. Uganda, 2022, ¶102); interest under Article 38 (payable "when necessary in order to ensure full reparation"); the requirement of causation and the influence of the injured State's own contribution (Article 39). Authorities with figures: Corfu Channel (1949) — £843,947; I'm Alone (1935) — US$25,000; DRC v. Uganda (2022) — US$325,000,000 in five instalments with 6% post-judgment interest, the Court refusing the macro-economic claim; Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (2018) — US$378,890.59, including compensation for environmental damage and services and 4% pre-judgment interest on costs.

4. Satisfaction (Article 37) — the remedy "insofar as [the injury] cannot be made good by restitution or compensation"; it may consist in "an acknowledgement of the breach, an expression of regret, a formal apology or another appropriate modality"; it must not be out of proportion to the injury and may not take a humiliating form. Authorities: Corfu Channel — the Court's declaration "is in itself appropriate satisfaction" for the violation of Albania's sovereignty; I'm Alone — a formal acknowledgment of the illegality; Rainbow Warrior — the formal apology of France and the payment of a fund as satisfaction; Genocide (2007) — the declaration sufficient for the failure to prevent; DRC v. Uganda (2022), ¶368 — "a declaration of violation is, in itself, appropriate satisfaction in most cases".

5. Assurance and guarantee of non-repetition (Article 30(b)). Note the drafting point on which a good answer earns credit: the syllabus and the older literature treat this as a fourth form of reparation, but the ILC places it in Article 30 with cessation, because it does not repair the injury but reinforces the primary obligation. Authorities: LaGrand (2001) — the Court held that "an apology is not sufficient" and treated the United States' programme of consular notification as "meeting Germany's request for a general assurance of non-repetition" (¶¶123–124), with the duty of review and reconsideration if the assurance fails (¶125); Nicaragua (the order to cease and refrain); human-rights practice (the Inter-American Court's guarantees of non-repetition; the UN Basic Principles and Guidelines on the Right to a Remedy and Reparation, 2005).

6. Two closing points which separate a first-class answer. (i) Cessation is not reparation (Article 30(a)): it is compliance, not a remedy. (ii) The choice of means and the two-stage procedure: reparation may be agreed between the parties (as in a lump-sum settlement), and where the breach continues or the quantum is contested, the Court will declare responsibility first and settle the form and amount later — Corfu Channel (1949), Tehran Hostages (1980), DRC v. Uganda (2005 and 2022), Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (2015 and 2018).

2013 Q.3(a) and 2012 Q.5 and 2011 Q.5 (first part) — "What are the constituent elements of (the principle of) State responsibility?" / "… constituent elements of State responsibility for internationally wrongful acts? Discuss with reference to the Draft Code of the ILC …"

(The 2011 paper adds: "Also discuss the forms of reparation for the breach of an obligation by a State with the help of relevant provisions of the Draft Code …" — use the answer above for that part. The 2012 paper asks the constituents "with reference to the Draft Code", so the codification history and Articles 1–3 should be given in more detail.)

Model answer

1. The statutory formulation. Article 2 of the ILC Draft Code, 2001: "There is an internationally wrongful act of a State when conduct consisting of an action or omission: (a) is attributable to the State under international law; and (b) constitutes a breach of an international obligation of the State." Read with Article 1 ("every internationally wrongful act of a State entails the international responsibility of that State") this gives the modern answer: two elements, not three — attribution and breach. Explain each: attribution under Articles 4–11 (organs; persons exercising governmental authority; organs placed at the disposal of another State; ultra vires conduct; persons acting on instructions, direction or control; conduct in the absence of official authorities; insurrectional movements; acknowledgment and adoption); breach under Articles 12–15 (non-conformity "regardless of the origin or character" of the obligation; the obligation must be in force when the act occurs; continuing breaches; composite acts). Add Article 3 (internal law is irrelevant to characterisation) and Article 2's word "omission" — the State is responsible for what it fails to do, as Corfu Channel and Tehran Hostages show.

2. The classical trio and the theories. The older learning treated the elements as (i) an international legal obligation in force between the two States, (ii) an act or omission violating it and imputable to the State, and (iii) loss or damage resulting. The third element — damage — is now handled by the "damage theory" debate: the ILC commentary "makes it clear that there is no general rule in this respect. Whether the element of damage is required depends on the content of the primary obligation." Give the illustration: the obligation to enact a uniform law is breached by the failure to enact it, without proof of specific damage; but in transboundary pollution cases actual damage must be established (Trail Smelter; Gabčíkovo; Lake Lanoux). Add that damage survives as injury in the law of reparation (Article 31(2): "material or moral"), and as the question of standing (Articles 42 and 48; South West Africa contrasted with Barcelona Traction).

3. Fault. The second theory: is a mental element required? "It is not necessary for State responsibility that there should be an intention to cause harm. In the absence of any specific requirement of a mental element in terms of the primary obligation, it is only the act of the State that matters, independently of any intention" (syllabus text). Responsibility is therefore objective in principle; but where the primary obligation is one of diligence ("appropriate steps", "necessary measures", "prevent"), the standard of care enters through the content of the obligation — Caire (1929) on official character and ultra vires; Corfu Channel (1949) on control ≠ knowledge; Tehran Hostages (1980) on "more than mere negligence". Verma's conclusion: "there is no clear rule that responsibility is always based on fault, nor one that it is independent of it … everything depends on the context and the interpretation of the obligation breached."

4. Absolute liability and risk. The third theory: liability for lawful but ultra-hazardous activities, imposed not as a general principle but "in circumstances and conditions which are clearly defined in international treaties" — the 1972 Convention on International Liability for Damage Caused by Space Objects (absolute liability of the launching State), the nuclear conventions of 1960 and 1963, the 1969/1971 oil-pollution regime, and the ILC's separate work on prevention and allocation of loss (Article 55 preserves these leges speciales). Indian illustration: M.C. Mehta v. Union of India (1987) and the Public Liability Insurance Act, 1991.

5. Conclusion. The modern law is codified in two elements (Article 2), damage and fault being elements not of the wrongful act but sometimes of the primary rule or of the remedy, and absolute liability being confined to special treaty regimes. Cite the cases in support, and finish with the codification's authority: not a treaty, but annexed to GA resolution 56/83 of 2001 and applied by the ICJ as customary law.

2013 short note (b) and 2011 short note (v) — "Principle of Exhaustion of Local Remedies"
Model short note (about 250–300 words)

Statement. Article 44(b) of the ILC Articles: the responsibility of a State may not be invoked if "the claim is one to which the rule of exhaustion of local remedies applies and any available and effective local remedy has not been exhausted".

Nature and rationale. The rule is one of admissibility, not of substance: it applies where a State brings a claim on behalf of its national, not where it claims for direct injury to itself. Its raison d'être (as the prescribed text puts it) is respect for the sovereignty of the respondent State and its first opportunity to remedy the wrong within its own system; the reduction of the number of international claims; the practical advantage that local courts are better placed to find the facts; and the principle that there can be no claim for denial of justice until the local remedies have been used.

Content. "Local remedies" means the whole system of legal protection — courts, tribunals and administrative bodies, and the procedural facilities available to litigants (Ambatielos, 1956); but only legal, not discretionary, remedies. Exhaustion must extend through appeals; but only remedies which are "available and effective", i.e. capable of affecting the outcome, need be tried (Finnish Ships, 1934; El Oro Mining, 1931). The requirement is dispensed with where redress would be futile (Robert E. Brown, 1923), where there is undue delay attributable to the respondent, where the injured person had no relevant connection with the respondent State, or where the requirement is waived by treaty — as in the ICSID Convention, most BITs and the VCCR Optional Protocol under which LaGrand, Avena and Jadhav were decided. Leading case: Interhandel (ICJ 1959) — the claim was inadmissible because proceedings were still pending in the United States; ELSI (1989) — the rule is not a formal requirement; Diallo (2007) — applied to individual and corporate claims in the same case. Companion conditions: nationality of claims (Article 44(a)) and the rules on waiver and acquiescence (Article 45). (Cross-reference: §4.5 above, and Article 15 of the ILC Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection, 2006, for the exceptions.)

↑ top

C. Problem Questions and Question Bank

DU papers have carried problem questions on State responsibility in the 2025 paper (the Tarkin/Yoda and UFO/Zedex problems answered above), and problem-type questions appear regularly in the eight-mark and ten-mark slots. The technique is always the same: identify the obligation; ask whether the conduct is attributable; ask whether it is a breach; run the defences; then state the consequences (cessation, assurances, reparation). The problems below are arranged in that order, with skeleton answers containing the citations an examiner expects.

Problem 1 — the minefield

State A's warships are passing through a narrow strait used for international navigation which lies within the territorial sea of State B. Two of them strike mines newly laid by persons unknown; 44 sailors die. State B knew of the minelaying — its shore batteries had watched the operation — but gave no warning to shipping, and its officials later protested against the passage. State C, whose merchant vessel was also damaged, claims compensation. Discuss the responsibility of State B.

Skeleton. (i) The obligations of State B: elementary considerations of humanity, the freedom of maritime communication, and the duty not to allow knowingly its territory to be used for acts contrary to the rights of other States — the three "general and well-recognised principles" of Corfu Channel (1949, p. 22); plus Article 42(a) of UNCLOS (no hampering of transit passage) and Article 24 (no unreasonable impairment of innocent passage). (ii) Attribution: the mine-laying itself is not attributable to State B because the authors are unknown; Corfu Channel is explicit that "the mere fact of the control exercised by a State over its territory and waters" does not entail knowledge and "neither involves prima facie responsibility nor shifts the burden of proof" (p. 18). (iii) But State B's own omissions are attributable under Article 4 (organs — its shore authorities, navy and government) and constitute breaches: it knew of the mine-laying (inferred from the two-hour interval during which its officers observed the operation, and from its subsequent protests) and failed to notify shipping, thereby "grave omissions" for which it is responsible. The standard of proof is satisfied by "liberal recourse to inferences of fact" where the State is in exclusive control of the evidence (p. 18). (iv) Defences: no consent; necessity fails (Article 25 — no grave and imminent peril to an essential interest, and notification was the obvious alternative); countermeasures fail (no prior wrongful act by A; and countermeasures cannot affect humanitarian or peremptory obligations). (v) Consequences: compensation to State A (£843,947 in the real case) and to State C by the same principles (Article 36 — "financially assessable damage"); satisfaction for the affront if any; cessation in the form of clearing the mines and warnings.

Problem 2 — denial of justice and espousal

Mr X, a national of State A, invests in a factory in State B. The factory is requisitioned by the B Government under an emergency law without compensation. X litigates in B's courts for six years; his claim is dismissed on a technical ground after repeated adjournments caused by the Government's failure to produce documents. X has exhausted every appeal available in B. State A wishes to take up his case. Advise State A.

Skeleton. (i) Two distinct claims: (a) the direct claim of State A for the breach of an obligation owed to it (e.g. a treaty of friendship, commerce and navigation, as in ELSI, or Article 32 of the ILC Articles) — no exhaustion required; and (b) the diplomatic protection claim for the injury to X — Article 44(b): "the claim is one to which the rule of exhaustion of local remedies applies and any available and effective local remedy has not been exhausted". On these facts X has exhausted them, so the claim is admissible. (ii) The substantive wrong: the requisition if it is "arbitrary" (ELSI, ICJ 1989, where the requisition of the plant was held arbitrary in breach of the FCN Treaty), the failure to pay compensation, and the conduct of the proceedings assessed against the Neer standard ("an outrage … bad faith … wilful neglect of duty") — noting that modern practice, and Article 15(b) of the ILC Draft Articles on Diplomatic Protection (undue delay attributable to the State), would find a denial of justice where six years of obstruction is proved. (iii) Causation and quantum: Diallo (Compensation, 2012) — "sufficiently direct and certain causal nexus"; heads of loss for the value of the investment and for the individual's own injury; not punitive damages (Lusitania). (iv) The diplomatic protection requirements: continuous nationality (Nottebohm — genuine link), and, for corporate claims, the rules in Barcelona Traction: the State of incorporation may protect the company; shareholders' own States may not, save in the exceptional cases (company defunct, or the State of incorporation cannot exercise protection). (v) If the factory is owned by a company incorporated in State B, State A must disclose the corporate structure carefully — this is the classic trap set by Barcelona Traction.

Problem 3 — the insurrection and the officials

An armed insurrection breaks out in State P against the Government. Some army officers, acting without orders, arrest and ill-treat foreign nationals, and a rebel group seizes a foreign embassy, holding its staff hostage. The insurrection succeeds; the rebel leaders form the new Government of P. The Government of the foreign State claims reparation for the ill-treatment and the hostage-taking. Advise.

Skeleton. (i) The officers: their conduct is attributable to State P under Articles 4 and 7 (conduct of organs "even if it exceeds its authority or contravenes instructions" — Caire, 1929) unless it was purely private conduct; and their ill-treatment breaches the primary obligations on the treatment of aliens and (if torture) the Torture Convention, plus human rights obligations. (ii) The rebels' seizure of the embassy: under Article 10(1), the conduct of an insurrectional movement "which becomes the new government of a State shall be considered an act of that State under international law" — so the new Government of P is responsible for the hostage-taking, retrospectively, from the moment it occurred (Bolívar Railway Co., 1903; contrast Home Missionary Society, 1920 and Pinson, 1928, where the insurrection failed and no attribution followed). (iii) The former Government: even had the insurrection failed, the State would have remained responsible for its own failure to take the measures within its power to protect the mission and nationals (Article 9 and the due diligence branch; Tehran Hostages ¶¶61–63). Article 10(3) preserves that responsibility expressly. (iv) If the new Government endorses the hostage-taking, Article 11 (acknowledgment and adoption) provides an additional route — the holding of Tehran ¶74. (v) Consequences: cessation (release, restoration of premises), assurances of non-repetition, compensation to the victims (or to the State in diplomatic protection), and satisfaction — the whole remedial set of Articles 30–37; the obligations of the VCDR (Articles 22, 24, 29, 44, 45) bind the new Government as the successor in responsibility of the same State.

Problem 4 — the hazardous installation

State M authorises a company to operate a chemical plant near its border with State N. A lawful operation of the plant causes a release of toxic gas which crosses the border and kills livestock and damages crops in State N. Nothing unlawful under State M's law or the applicable environmental treaties is proved, but State M had received warnings that the plant's safety systems were inadequate and took no action. Advise State N.

Skeleton. (i) Even where the activity is lawful, State M may be responsible for its own preventive failure: Trail Smelter (1938/1941) — "no State has the right to use or permit the use of its territory in such a manner as to cause injury by fumes … when the case is of serious consequence and the injury is established by clear and convincing evidence"; Pulp Mills (2010) — the duty to conduct an environmental impact assessment and to exercise due diligence in prevention; the ILC's 2001 Articles on Prevention of Transboundary Harm and the 2006 Draft Principles on Allocation of Loss (strict liability, insurance, funds). (ii) Attribution: the failure to act on the warnings is the conduct of State M's organs (Article 4) — but if the plant is privately owned and operated, its own acts are not attributable (Article 8: no direction or control; a licensing relationship is not control) — this is the distinction the problem is designed to test. (iii) Breach: a due diligence obligation of prevention (Article 12 with the primary rule; Article 14(3) — the breach occurs when the event occurs). (iv) Defences: necessity fails (the harm was not the State's peril); consent absent; force majeure unavailable because the State was warned (Article 23(2)(a) — self-induced). (v) Reparation: compensation for the material damage (Trail Smelter's award; Articles 31 and 36), and for environmental damage per se under Costa Rica v. Nicaragua (2018); restoration of the affected land where possible (Article 35); and, prospectively, assurances of non-repetition — a stronger regulatory framework and monitoring (Article 30(b)). (vi) Mention the Indian parallels: absolute liability for hazardous enterprises (M.C. Mehta, 1987), the Public Liability Insurance Act 1991, and the polluter-pays principle in the National Green Tribunal Act 2010.

Problem 5 — cyber conduct by private hackers

A group of hackers operating from the territory of State G launches ransomware attacks on hospitals in State H, causing deaths. State G's intelligence services knew of the group, discouraged but did not stop it, and in one case purchased the stolen data. State H claims that State G is responsible. Advise.

Skeleton. (i) Attribution: the group is not an organ (Article 4) and has no governmental authority (Article 5); Article 8 requires that the group acted "on the instructions of, or under the direction or control of" State G in carrying out the attack — knowledge and toleration are not control (Nicaragua ¶115). (ii) But the purchase of the data by State G's intelligence service is the conduct of a State organ (Article 4) and, if it amounts to participation in or the receipt of the proceeds of the wrongful attack, may engage Article 16 (aid or assistance) — although the assistance must be given with knowledge of the circumstances and must facilitate the act; a later purchase may raise questions of acquiescence and of the duty of non-recognition under Article 41 rather than of Article 16. (iii) The failure to stop the group, if State G owed a due diligence obligation (e.g. under a mutual legal assistance or cyber-crime treaty, or the customary duty of prevention of transboundary harm by non-State actors), is State G's own breach; the "due diligence" standard is one of conduct, and the relevant authorities are the Corfu Channel principle of "no knowing use of territory against the rights of others", the Genocide case (2007) on prevention obligations, and the Tallinn Manual's rules on State responsibility in cyberspace. (iv) Consequences: cessation, assurances, compensation for the harm caused by acts attributable to State G, and satisfaction. (v) Concessions to make in the answer: the problem has no ICJ authority directly on point; the 2010 and 2015 UN GGE reports and the 2021 UN OEWG consensus report state that international law — including the law of State responsibility — applies to cyberspace.

Problem 6 — occupation, quantum and the forms of reparation

State U's armed forces occupy a district of State V for three years. During the occupation, U's troops kill civilians, destroy villages, and loot timber and minerals; rebel groups allied to U also cause damage. U withdraws after a peace agreement which says nothing about compensation. Ten years later V brings a claim. Advise on reparation.

Skeleton. (i) The findings would follow Armed Activities (DRC v. Uganda) (2005): violation of the non-use of force and non-intervention; violations of human rights and humanitarian law; looting and exploitation of natural resources; and the duties of an occupying Power to protect and to prevent. (ii) Attribution: U's own forces — Articles 4 and 7; and for the allied groups' damage, the Court's holding in the 2022 Judgment (¶87) that the occupying Power must repair "all damage resulting from the conflict in Ituri, even that resulting from the conduct of third parties, unless it has established … that it was not caused by Uganda's failure to meet its obligations as an occupying Power" — a reverse onus operating in occupied territory. (iii) Causation: a "sufficiently direct and certain causal nexus" (¶85); macro-economic loss will be refused (¶365). (iv) Forms: restitution is materially impossible (the fruits of the looting are gone; the occupation is ended), so compensation is the appropriate form (Chorzów; Articles 35 and 36; DRC v. Uganda ¶101); global sums for mass claims, "within the range of possibilities indicated by the evidence and taking into account equitable considerations"; satisfaction by declaration (¶368) since non-material harm to the State is not separately compensable (¶373); and, if the peace agreement contains an amnesty, note that a waiver of claims requires clear words and cannot cover breaches of peremptory norms (Article 45(a) and Article 26). (v) Modalities: instalments, post-judgment interest (6% in the 2022 case), capacity to pay (¶376), distribution to victims through a supervised fund (¶377) — and the political question of how far reparation should be individualised rather than paid to the State.

Question bank with answer plans

Table 4.21 · Expected questions and one-line answer plans
QuestionAnswer plan
Define State responsibility. What is its legal basis?Art. 1 + Chorzów + sovereign equality + Ago's primary/secondary rule distinction + Art. 3 no excuse of internal law.
Distinguish between primary and secondary rules of international law.Ago's reports; primary rules create obligations; secondary rules (the ILC Articles) govern the consequences of breach; illustrate with the VCDR (primary) and Articles 30–37 (secondary).
"No damage, no responsibility." Discuss the damage theory.No general requirement; depends on the primary obligation (uniform law example); damage re-enters as "injury" (Art. 31(2)); standing (Arts. 42, 48); Trail Smelter and Gabčíkovo contrasted with embassy inviolability.
Is fault a necessary element of State responsibility?Objective responsibility in principle; fault returns through diligence obligations (Caire; Corfu; Tehran); Verma's "no clear rule either way".
Explain the theory of absolute liability and the risk theory.Treaty-based strict liability: 1972 Space Objects; nuclear 1960/1963; oil 1969/1971; channelling, insurance, caps, funds; Art. 55; Cosmos 954; M.C. Mehta; 1991 Act.
What is meant by "attribution" or "imputability"? Discuss Articles 4–11.Definition (legal fiction connecting the individual's act to the State); each article with a case (see §4.3).
"A State is not responsible for the acts of private persons." Examine.True as to attribution (Art. 8's limits; Nicaragua ¶115); but responsibility for the State's own failure (Corfu; Tehran), adoption (Art. 11), insurrection (Art. 10), complicity (Arts. 16–18).
Discuss the rule in the Corfu Channel case.Facts; two Special Agreement questions; knowledge inferred; general principles; Operation Retail and satisfaction; £843,947; the standard of proof ("liberal recourse to inferences of fact").
Explain the principle of Barcelona Traction.Separate legal personality; shareholders' State lacks jus standi; exceptions; erga omnes dictum and its descent (Art. 48; Gambia v. Myanmar).
What did Tehran Hostages decide about attribution?Two phases; ¶58 (no initial attribution); ¶61–63 (failure to protect); ¶74 (adoption → agents of the State); self-contained régime (¶86); deferred reparation (¶90).
Discuss "effective control" with reference to Nicaragua and Tadić.Nicaragua ¶115; Tadić overall control; Genocide 2007 rejecting Tadić for State responsibility; practical difference; Art. 8.
"An apology is enough." Discuss with reference to LaGrand.¶123 (apology insufficient); ¶124 (programme as assurance); ¶125 (review and reconsideration); Art. 37 satisfaction and Art. 30(b).
Are provisional measures of the ICJ binding?LaGrand ¶¶92–110 (Art. 41; Art. 94 Charter; Sofia order); consequences — breach engages responsibility (DRC v. Uganda, 2000).
Discuss the forms of reparation with the ILC Articles.See the model answer at 2015/2013/2011 above: Arts. 31, 34–39 with cases.
What is restitutio in integrum? When may it be refused?Art. 35; material impossibility; disproportionate burden; Chorzów; Temple of Preah Vihear; Rainbow Warrior; Gabcíkovo.
Are punitive damages available in international law?No: Lusitania; I'm Alone; DRC v. Uganda ¶102; individual criminal responsibility is the proper channel (Art. 58).
Explain satisfaction and its limits.Art. 37; declaration, regret, apology; proportionality; no humiliation; Corfu Channel; Rainbow Warrior; Genocide 2007.
Discuss guarantees of non-repetition.Art. 30(b) (and the older classification as a form of reparation); LaGrand; Nicaragua's order to cease; human-rights practice; the 2005 UN Basic Principles.
What is the rule of exhaustion of local remedies? What are its exceptions?Art. 44(b); rationale; Ambatielos; Finnish Ships; Interhandel; ELSI; Diallo; Art. 15 Diplomatic Protection Articles.
Distinguish nationality of claims from exhaustion of local remedies.Art. 44(a) and (b); Nottebohm (genuine link); Barcelona Traction (corporations); continuance of nationality; the two are cumulative conditions of admissibility.
Discuss the circumstances precluding wrongfulness.Arts. 20–27 with cases (see §4.3(H) and the defences section).
What are countermeasures? State their conditions.Arts. 22, 49–54; Naulilaa; Air Services; Gabčíkovo; retorsion distinguished; human-rights limits.
When may a State plead necessity?Art. 25; cumulative conditions; Gabčíkovo ¶¶49–58; Wall AO; Argentinian cases; Art. 27(a).
What is the legal status of the ILC Articles, 2001?Not a treaty; annexed to GA res. 56/83; applied as customary law (DRC v. Uganda, 2022; Genocide, 2007); lex specialis (Art. 55); the question of a future convention.
Write short notes on: (i) erga omnes; (ii) serious breaches of peremptory norms; (iii) plurality of responsible States; (iv) interest; (v) contribution to the injury.(i) Barcelona Traction ¶33; Arts. 42, 48. (ii) Arts. 40–41; non-recognition; Iraq/Kuwait. (iii) Art. 47; Art. 19; recourse. (iv) Art. 38; 4%/6% splits. (v) Art. 39; Gabčíkovo.

How to structure the answer — mark-by-mark

Table 4.22 · What the examiner is looking for
MarksWhat to writeTime
2–3 (short note)Definition with the article number; three or four sentences of content; one case; one limitation. No introduction, no conclusion.5 minutes
5–8Definition; the rule stated in the article's own words; two or three cases; one application or example; a one-line conclusion.10 minutes
10 (half question)Definition → the rule → the cases with facts in a sentence each → the exceptions or limits → a short application to a real situation. Use at least three article references and three cases.18 minutes
16–20 (full question)The above plus: (i) the statutory framework (Articles 1, 2, 31, 34 … as relevant); (ii) a structured answer with headings matching the sub-parts of the question; (iii) at least five cases, each used for a specific proposition; (iv) a paragraph of criticism or evaluation (the Articles are not a treaty; the effective/overall control controversy; the erga omnes standing debate; whether reparation can ever be adequate for mass atrocities); (v) a conclusion which answers the question asked and does not merely summarise.35 minutes
Problem question (any weight)Issues → obligations → attribution → breach → defences → consequences (cessation, assurances, reparation) → conclusion. Cite one case per step. Never decide the merits of the primary facts before identifying the obligation.Pro rata
