Appendix B · Glossary of the terms you must use accurately

Table 4.23 · Glossary
TermMeaning in this topic
Attribution (imputability)The legal operation by which the conduct of a person or entity is treated as the conduct of a State. Governed by international law (Arts. 4–11); "the legal fiction which assimilates the actions or omissions of State officials to the State itself".
De facto organAn entity which, though not an organ under internal law, is so completely dependent on the State as to be a mere instrument; distinguished from the Article 8 case of instructions/direction/control (Genocide, 2007).
Effective control / overall controlRival tests for attributing the acts of a group: Nicaragua ¶115 (effective control of the specific operation — the ICJ's test for State responsibility) and Tadić (overall control — the ICTY's test for conflict classification and individual responsibility).
Dual attributionThe possibility that the same conduct is attributable both to an international organisation and to a State (as in Nuhanović; contrast Behrami).
Continuing breach / composite actArt. 14(2): a breach extending over the whole period during which the act continues (Tehran hostages; occupation). Art. 15: a breach constituted by a series of acts or omissions defined in aggregate as wrongful (systematic torture; Art. 40(2) "gross or systematic failure").
Due diligenceAn obligation of conduct requiring the State to take all reasonable measures within its power to prevent or punish — the obligation in Corfu Channel, Tehran Hostages, the Genocide case and Pulp Mills.
Circumstances precluding wrongfulnessConsent, self-defence, countermeasures, force majeure, distress and necessity (Arts. 20–25); no excuse for peremptory breaches (Art. 26); obligation revives and compensation may be owed (Art. 27).
Countermeasure (reprisal)Conduct which would otherwise be unlawful, taken against a responsible State to induce compliance; lawful only within Arts. 49–54 (reversible, proportionate, not affecting human rights/humanitarian obligations/diplomatic inviolability, and subject to prior demand and to suspension before a tribunal).
RetorsionAn unfriendly but lawful act (recall of an ambassador, refusal of a visa, suspension of voluntary aid) — needing no legal justification because no obligation is breached.
SatisfactionThe remedy for non-material injury where restitution and compensation do not suffice: acknowledgment, expression of regret, formal apology, declaration of a court or "another appropriate modality" (Art. 37).
Restitutio in integrumRestitution in kind or juridical restitution: the re-establishment of the situation existing before the breach (Art. 35), subject to material impossibility and disproportionate burden.
Damnum emergens / lucrum cessansActual loss / loss of profits — the two measures of "financially assessable damage" under Art. 36(2).
Global sumA single overall award in place of itemised individual assessments, used in mass-claim cases (DRC v. Uganda, 2022) "within the range of possibilities indicated by the evidence and taking into account equitable considerations".
Cessation / assurances and guarantees of non-repetitionArt. 30: to stop the continuing breach, and to offer assurances (a statement of intention) and guarantees (structural measures) if circumstances so require.
Erga omnes / erga omnes partesObligations owed to the international community as a whole / to a group of States for a collective interest; breach permits invocation by States other than the injured State (Art. 48), but only for cessation and assurances, not compensation.
Jus cogens (peremptory norm)A norm accepted and recognised by the international community as a whole from which no derogation is permitted (Art. 53 VCLT); no circumstance precludes wrongfulness for its breach (Art. 26); serious breaches entail the consequences in Arts. 40–41.
Lex specialisArt. 55: the Articles do not apply where special rules govern the existence, content or implementation of responsibility (WTO, ECHR, diplomatic law's "self-contained régime", the risk-liability conventions).
Self-contained régimeThe idea, from Tehran Hostages ¶86, that a body of law (e.g. diplomatic immunities?) provides its own remedies for breach and abuse.
Denial of justiceThe failure of a State's judicial system, measured by the Neer standard ("an outrage … bad faith … wilful neglect of duty"), for which a claim arises only after local remedies are exhausted.
Calvo clause / Calvo doctrineA contractual undertaking by an alien to resort to local remedies only and not to seek diplomatic protection; binds the individual but not the State's right of protection (North American Dredging, 1926).
Inter-temporal lawArt. 13: the State must have been bound by the obligation when the act occurred; conduct is judged by the law in force at the time.

Appendix F · Cross-references to the other topics in this series

  • Topic 1 (Nature and Sources). The Articles as a codification of customary law; the effect of GA resolutions (res. 56/83) — the same problem as the "Short notes on GA resolutions" noted in the syllabus; and the authority of the ILC's work as "the most highly qualified publicists" under Article 38(1)(d) of the Statute.
  • Topic 2 (Subjects and Recognition). The responsibility of international organisations (Reparation for Injuries, 1949); individual criminal responsibility and the concurrent operation of State and individual responsibility (Article 58); the responsibility of an insurrectional movement which becomes a State (Article 10(2)) and the position of successor States.
  • Topic 3 (Relationship between International Law and Municipal Law). Article 3 (characterisation by international law), Article 32 (irrelevance of internal law), LaGrand and Medellín on the enforcement of international obligations in domestic law, and the Kuwait Airways case studied at §4.3(I) — where customary international law influenced the development of English law.
  • Later topics. Law of the Sea (the Corfu Channel straits question; the Enrica Lexie compensation); Diplomatic and Consular Immunities (Tehran Hostages in its second life as a Topic-7 case; the "self-contained régime"; the DRC's liability for the attack on Uganda's embassy in Armed Activities); Use of Force (Nicaragua; Oil Platforms; the Wall Opinion; self-defence as a circumstance precluding wrongfulness); Human Rights (exhaustion of local remedies in treaty bodies; guarantees of non-repetition in the Inter-American system).
Appendix G · The ten sentences to memorise (if nothing else)
  1. "Every internationally wrongful act of a State entails the international responsibility of that State." — Article 1.
  2. "There is an internationally wrongful act of a State when conduct consisting of an action or omission is attributable to the State under international law and constitutes a breach of an international obligation of the State." — Article 2.
  3. "The characterization of an act of a State as internationally wrongful is governed by international law. Such characterization is not affected by the characterization of the same act as lawful by internal law." — Article 3.
  4. "For this conduct to give rise to legal responsibility of the United States, it would in principle have to be proved that that State had effective control of the military or paramilitary operations in the course of which the alleged violations were committed." — Nicaragua, ¶115 (the ancestor of Article 8).
  5. "The militants, authors of the invasion and jailers of the hostages, had now become agents of the Iranian State for whose acts the State itself was internationally responsible." — Tehran Hostages, ¶74 (the ancestor of Article 11).
  6. "Reparation must, as far as possible, wipe out all the consequences of the illegal act and re-establish the situation which would, in all probability, have existed if that act had not been committed." — Chorzów Factory, 1928.
  7. "Full reparation for the injury caused by the internationally wrongful act shall take the form of restitution, compensation and satisfaction, either singly or in combination." — Article 34.
  8. "the Court considers that this declaration is in itself appropriate satisfaction" — Corfu Channel, 1949 (satisfaction in practice); and "an apology is not sufficient" — LaGrand, ¶123.
  9. "[T]he state of necessity can only be invoked under certain strictly defined conditions which must be cumulatively satisfied; and the State concerned is not the sole judge of whether those conditions have been met." — Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros, ¶51.
  10. "The responsibility of a State may not be invoked if … the claim is one to which the rule of exhaustion of local remedies applies and any available and effective local remedy has not been exhausted." — Article 44(b).

Add, for luck, the two modern figures: US$325 million (DRC v. Uganda, 2022) and US$378,890.59 (Costa Rica v. Nicaragua, 2018) — and the two great declarations of the 2001 Articles: "reparation … should not have a punitive character" (DRC v. Uganda, ¶102) and "a declaration of violation is, in itself, appropriate satisfaction in most cases" (DRC v. Uganda, ¶368).
